The paper highlights that the effect of punishment is most positive when it is peer-driven, combined with transparency and communication, and not imposed externally. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment, reputation, and communication mechanisms should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control games without these features.

- source: Chen, X. P., Pillutla, M. M., & Yao, X. (2009). Unintended Consequences of Cooperation Inducing and Maintaining Mechanisms in Public Goods Dilemmas: Sanctions and Moral Appeals. *Group Processes & Intergroup Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Sanctions (punishment or reward) in public goods games increase cooperation while present, but when removed, cooperation drops sharply—sometimes to levels below those in groups that never had sanctions. Moral appeals also increase cooperation, but this effect is more persistent after the appeal is removed. The drop in cooperation after removing sanctions is mediated by reduced trust in others' cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so findings are based on contribution rates, not direct payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment (and reward) increases cooperation in the short term, but this effect is not sustained after removal, and may even backfire. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes for games with punishment enabled versus disabled. The findings are most useful for predicting contribution rates and for understanding the psychological mechanisms (trust) that may moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation. For efficiency prediction, the results suggest caution: increased cooperation under punishment may not translate to sustained efficiency gains, especially if punishment is later removed.

- source: Kiyonari, T., & Barclay, P. (2008). Cooperation in social dilemmas: Free riding may be thwarted by second-order reward rather than by punishment. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The main finding is that, in one-shot public goods games, participants are much more likely to support cooperation through reward (both first- and second-order) than through punishment. First-order punishment of defectors occurs, but second-order punishment of nonpunishers is rare or absent, and punishers are not socially endorsed. In contrast, both first- and second-order rewards are common, and rewarders are socially endorsed. The presence of both punishment and reward options does not increase support for punishment. The paper does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in one-shot, four-player public goods games with binary contribution and surprise sanctioning opportunities, punishment is not socially supported at the second order, and reward is more likely to be used and socially endorsed. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency changes when punishment is enabled. The behavioral results suggest that, in similar game designs, punishment may not be self-sustaining and may not improve efficiency unless supported by other mechanisms. The findings are most relevant for predicting the likelihood of punishment and reward behaviors, not their direct impact on efficiency.

- source: Gächter, S., Herrmann, B., & Thöni, C. (2010). Culture and cooperation. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a standard public goods game, the opportunity to punish increases average contributions in some cultures but not in others. Cultural background explains a much larger share of the variance in contributions when punishment is available than when it is not. Punishment patterns themselves (including antisocial punishment) differ strongly across cultures and are linked to differences in cooperation. The presence of punishment reduces within-group variance in contributions but has mixed effects on between-group variance. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but higher contributions in the punishment condition in some cultures suggest likely higher efficiency in those cases, while in other cultures the effect is negligible.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by implication, efficiency) is highly culture-dependent, even in otherwise identical public goods game designs. For prediction tasks, this means that game design dimensions alone (such as player count, MPCR, punishment cost, etc.) are not sufficient to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency without accounting for cultural context. The paper's results suggest that, in some environments, punishment can substantially increase cooperation (and likely efficiency), while in others it has little or no effect. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its quantitative value for efficiency prediction is limited to inferring that higher contributions generally imply higher efficiency.

- source: Xiao, E., & Houser, D. (2011). Punish in public. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Privately-implemented weak punishment reduces cooperation compared to a baseline with no punishment, while publicly-implemented punishment (where all group members are informed of punishment events) increases and sustains cooperation at higher levels than both baseline and private punishment. The effect is not due to shame, signaling, or differences in information about contributions, as all treatments provide full information about group contributions and maintain anonymity. The public punishment mechanism makes the cooperation norm more salient, leading to higher voluntary and conditional cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the higher contribution rates in the public punishment treatment suggest that public punishment can reverse the detrimental effects of weak punishment on group outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the public observability of punishment (even when the punished individual's identity is anonymous) is a key moderator of punishment's effect on cooperation in public goods games. When predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, the design dimension of whether punishment is public or private is critical: private, weak punishment can reduce cooperation below baseline, while public punishment can increase it above baseline. Although the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the close mapping between contribution rates and group welfare in this linear PGG means that higher contributions in the public punishment treatment likely translate to higher efficiency. The findings are most applicable to settings with small groups, fixed partners, linear PGG structure, and exogenous, weak punishment. The results are less informative for settings with endogenous punishment, strong sanctions, or different information structures.

- source: Aoyagi, M., & Fréchette, G. (2009). Collusion as public monitoring becomes noisy: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that, in both homogeneous and heterogeneous groups, contributions decline over time in the absence of punishment, but increase over time when punishment is available. The opportunity to punish leads to higher contributions, with no significant difference between homogeneous and heterogeneous groups. The origin of endowment (effort vs. windfall) does not affect contribution levels or punishment patterns. Punishment is used to enforce the equal-contribution rule, and both effort and windfall subjects contribute similarly. There is no evidence of a normative conflict arising from endowment heterogeneity. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the increase in contributions with punishment is consistent with higher efficiency, subject to the cost of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard linear public good game with 4 players, 12 rounds, and a punishment stage, enabling punishment increases contributions regardless of whether endowments are earned or windfall. While efficiency is not directly reported, the increase in contributions is a strong proxy for increased efficiency, though the cost of punishment may offset some gains. The design details (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, no chat, continuous contributions, no reward, anonymity of punishment, feedback on others' contributions) are all specified and match standard PGG lab designs. For prediction, this paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment will increase efficiency (proxied by contributions) in similar game designs, and that endowment origin does not moderate this effect.

- source: Carpenter, J. P., & Matthews, P. H. (2012). NORM ENFORCEMENT: ANGER, INDIGNATION, OR RECIPROCITY?. *Journal of the European Economic Association*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that both second-party and third-party punishment increase contributions in a one-shot public goods game, with the effect size increasing as opportunities for third-party punishment and reciprocity increase. The order of mean contributions is: no punishment < second-party punishment < one-way third-party punishment < simultaneous two-way third-party punishment < sequential two-way third-party punishment. The study finds that third-party punishment is motivated by both indignation and group reciprocity, and that the presence of third-party punishment increases the expected cost of norm violation, leading to higher contributions. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (especially third-party punishment with reciprocity) increases contributions in a one-shot public goods game, relative to a no-punishment baseline. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on contribution rates, and suggest that the structure of punishment (second-party vs. third-party, reciprocity opportunities) is an important moderator. For efficiency prediction, the results imply that efficiency is likely to increase with punishment, but the magnitude cannot be directly inferred from the reported data.

- source: Grossman, G., & Baldassarri, D. (2012). The Impact of Elections on Cooperation: Evidence from a Lab-in-the-Field Experiment in Uganda. *American Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The introduction of a centralized sanctioning authority (monitor) in a PGG increases contributions to the public good compared to a baseline with no punishment. When the monitor is elected rather than randomly assigned, contributions increase further, suggesting a legitimacy effect. Both types of monitors punish defectors at a personal cost, but the actual punishment strategies are similar between elected and random monitors. The study does not report group efficiency or total earnings, so the direct effect on efficiency is not measured. The findings are robust to controls for monitor characteristics and are supported by observational data linking legitimacy to cooperation in real-world farmer groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing centralized punishment in a PGG increases contributions, and that the legitimacy of the punisher (via election) further increases cooperation. However, because the main outcome is contribution rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as indirect evidence for predicting efficiency effects. The game design dimensions are well specified, and the experimental manipulation is clear, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff reporting limits the precision of predictions for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Gächter, S., & Herrmann, B. (2009). Reciprocity, culture and human cooperation: previous insights and a new cross-cultural experiment. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that in one-shot public goods games, the introduction of punishment opportunities increases contributions in Swiss participant pools but not in Russian pools. Antisocial punishment (punishing cooperators) is much more common in Russia, leading to lower earnings and less effective cooperation. The paper reports that 80% of Russian participants earned less than the selfish prediction, compared to 33% in Switzerland. However, the study does not directly report group efficiency (payoff relative to the cooperative maximum) or compare average payoffs between punishment and no-punishment conditions. The findings highlight that the effect of punishment on cooperation and earnings is strongly moderated by cultural context, with punishment sometimes failing to improve, or even reducing, group welfare due to antisocial punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation and earnings in public goods games is highly context-dependent, especially on cultural background. While the design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost, etc.) are well specified, the key moderator in this study is culture, which is not a standard game design variable. The paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency (payoff relative to maximum) from control efficiency and design dimensions, but it does show that punishment can increase, decrease, or have no effect on group earnings depending on the prevalence of antisocial punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of antisocial punishment (which may be proxied by cultural or social variables) is a critical moderator, and that design dimensions alone may be insufficient to predict efficiency effects of punishment without accounting for such context.

- source: Espín, A. M., Brañas-Garza, P., Herrmann, B., & Gamella, J. F. (2012). Patient and impatient punishers of free-riders. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that punishment in a one-shot public goods game is implemented by two distinct types of individuals: patient cooperators (who punish free-riders, likely for moralistic/future-oriented reasons) and impatient free-riders (who punish other free-riders, likely for competitive/spiteful reasons). The likelihood of punishing is positively associated with cooperation among patient individuals and with free-riding among impatient individuals. The paper does not analyze or report the effect of punishment on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides no direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. It does, however, offer insight into the psychological heterogeneity of punishers, which may be relevant for understanding the mechanisms behind punishment behavior. For the downstream prediction task, this paper does not provide usable data on efficiency outcomes or how game design dimensions affect efficiency with or without punishment. Its main value is in highlighting that the motivations for punishment can differ (moralistic vs. competitive) depending on individual patience and contribution behavior, but this is not tied to efficiency outcomes.

- source: Baldassarri, D., & Grossman, G. (2011). Centralized sanctioning and legitimate authority promote cooperation in humans. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Centralized sanctioning, where a single monitor can punish, increases contributions to the public good compared to a baseline with no punishment. When the monitor is elected (legitimate authority), contributions increase even more than when the monitor is randomly assigned. The effect is attributed to the legitimacy of the authority, not to differences in the monitors' intrinsic characteristics or sanctioning behavior. The study is conducted in the field with Ugandan farmer cooperatives, using a repeated PGG with 8-12 players per group. The main outcome is contribution, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that centralized punishment (by a single monitor) increases cooperation (contributions) in public goods games, and that legitimacy of the authority (election vs. random assignment) further enhances this effect. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for predicting the effect of centralized punishment and authority legitimacy on contribution rates, and suggest that similar effects may be expected for efficiency if contributions translate directly to group payoff in the game design. The study is highly relevant for understanding the impact of centralized punishment and authority legitimacy, but less so for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Fowler, J. H., & Christakis, N. A. (2010). Cooperative behavior cascades in human social networks. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in both standard and punishment-enabled public goods games, individuals' contributions are causally influenced by the contributions of others in their group, and this influence spreads to others in subsequent rounds and groups, up to three degrees of separation. The presence of punishment increases contributions (as established in the original Fehr & Gachter 2002 study), but in this analysis, punishment does not fundamentally change the pattern or extent of behavioral cascades. Punishment can trigger cooperative cascades, but punishment behavior itself does not spread. The main outcome is the spread and persistence of cooperative behavior, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the spread of cooperative behavior in public goods games with and without punishment, but it does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Therefore, it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. However, it does confirm that punishment increases contributions (as in the original Fehr & Gachter 2002), and that the presence of punishment does not fundamentally alter the network dynamics of cooperation spread. For efficiency prediction, this paper is only indirectly informative, as it does not quantify payoff or efficiency effects.

- source: Anderson, L. R., & Stafford, S. L. (2003). Punishment in a regulatory setting: Experimental evidence from the VCM. *Journal of Regulatory Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that introducing probabilistic punishment for free riding in a public goods game increases group contributions, and that increasing the expected cost of punishment (probability × severity) further increases compliance. Punishment severity has a larger marginal effect on contribution than punishment probability. In repeated games, past punishment can actually decrease subsequent compliance, possibly due to a 'lightning doesn't strike twice' belief. The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so its findings are most directly about contribution behavior rather than welfare or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard VCM public goods game, introducing and increasing the expected cost of externally imposed punishment increases contribution rates. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff as a function of punishment, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The findings are highly relevant for predicting the direction of contribution changes when punishment is enabled, and suggest that both punishment probability and severity matter, with severity being more influential. For efficiency prediction, the results imply that efficiency is likely to increase with punishment (since contributions increase), but the magnitude and any possible over-punishment effects on group payoff are not quantified.

- source: Mulder, L. B., van Dijk, E., De Cremer, D., & Wilke, H. A. M. (2006). Undermining trust and cooperation: The paradox of sanctioning systems in social dilemmas. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that sanctioning systems in public goods games can undermine trust in others' intrinsic motivation to cooperate. When the sanctioning system is removed, both trust and cooperation decrease, especially among participants with high initial trust. The negative effect on cooperation is mediated by the reduction in trust. The effect is stronger for large sanctions and when initial trust is high. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be determined from the reported results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of a punishment system can undermine trust and, after its removal, reduce cooperation, especially when initial trust is high. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on baseline trust levels and that the removal of punishment can have negative aftereffects on cooperation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict efficiency outcomes. The findings are most useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms and potential moderators (such as initial trust and punishment size), rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Ones, U., & Putterman, L. (2007). The ecology of collective action: A public goods and sanctions experiment with controlled group formation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment confirms that allowing punishment in a public goods game sustains high contribution levels over time, in contrast to the typical decay seen in standard VCMs without punishment. Punishment is mostly directed at low contributors, but a significant fraction is 'perverse' (aimed at high contributors), which can reduce efficiency. Grouping high contributors and strong punishers together leads to higher average contributions, while groups with perverse punishers contribute less. Individual tendencies to contribute and punish are persistent. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total earnings, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency is not quantified.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment sustains high contributions in repeated public goods games, and that group composition (especially the presence or absence of perverse punishers) is a key moderator. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, the findings are most useful for predicting contribution rates rather than efficiency per se. The qualitative discussion suggests that punishment can increase efficiency when perverse punishment is minimized, but can reduce efficiency when perverse punishment is common. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that punishment generally increases cooperation, but the efficiency effect depends on the nature of punishment (targeted vs. perverse) and group composition.

- source: Carpenter, J. P. (2007). Punishing free-riders: How group size affects mutual monitoring and the provision of public goods. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that allowing punishment in public goods games sustains high contribution rates, especially when participants can monitor and punish a larger fraction of the group. Increasing group size from 5 to 10 does not reduce contributions and may even increase them, contrary to standard theory. However, when the ability to monitor/punish is restricted (e.g., Single monitoring), contributions fall to levels similar to the no-punishment control. The effect of punishment is thus strongly moderated by the monitoring technology. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the high contribution rates in the Full and Half monitoring conditions suggest that efficiency would be higher with punishment enabled, especially when monitoring is not restricted.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with costly punishment, enabling punishment (especially with broad monitoring) increases contribution rates, and likely efficiency, relative to control. The effect is robust to group size (5 vs 10) and is strongly moderated by the monitoring fraction: punishment is only effective when players can monitor/punish at least half the group. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, predictions about efficiency must be inferred from contribution rates. The findings are most informative for settings where the main outcome of interest is contribution/cooperation, and less directly for efficiency or total payoff prediction.

- source: Krupp, D. B., Debruine, L. A., & Barclay, P. (2008). A cue of kinship promotes cooperation for the public good. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study shows that subtle cues of kinship (facial self-resemblance) increase cooperation in a public goods game with punishment. Participants contributed more to the public good as the number of self-resembling (kin-cue) group members increased, but did not change their punishment behavior toward free riders based on kinship cues. The experiment used a yoked control design to isolate the effect of kinship cues. The main outcomes are contribution and punishment levels; efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured. The findings suggest that kinship cues can promote cooperation, but do not alter punitive responses to free riding.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a standard public goods game with punishment, increasing perceived kinship among group members increases contributions but does not affect punishment rates. While efficiency is not directly measured, higher contributions generally imply higher efficiency, unless offset by excessive punishment costs. Since punishment rates did not increase, it is reasonable to infer that efficiency would likely increase with kinship cues. However, the paper does not manipulate or analyze any of the core game design dimensions (such as MPCR, punishment cost, or player count) except for the kinship cue. Thus, its direct value for predicting efficiency as a function of game design dimensions is limited, but it does suggest that social context (kinship cues) can moderate cooperation independently of punishment mechanics.

- source: Kocher, M. G., Martinsson, P., & Visser, M. (2008). Does stake size matter for cooperation and punishment?. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that increasing the monetary stakes in a one-shot public goods game, both with and without punishment, does not significantly affect the average contribution rate or the frequency and intensity of punishment. The presence of a punishment stage increases average contributions compared to the no-punishment condition, but this effect is not moderated by stake size. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on the design of public goods games with and without punishment, including group size, MPCR, punishment cost and technology, and the absence of communication. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does confirm that, in this design, punishment increases average contributions, and that this effect is robust to stake size. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that punishment increases cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency outcomes.

- source: Lubell, M., & Scholz, J. T. (2001). Cooperation, reciprocity, and the collective-action heuristic. *American Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that both reciprocity and niceness in the environment increase cooperation rates, but their effects depend on whether subjects are initially predisposed to cooperate or defect. Past experience with reciprocity increases cooperation even when current reciprocity is absent, while past nonreciprocity does not reduce cooperation when reciprocity is present. The introduction of punishment (penalties) increases cooperation among defectors and in nonreciprocal environments, but can reduce cooperation among initial cooperators in reciprocal environments. The effect of punishment is thus asymmetric: it is most beneficial in environments lacking reciprocity, but may undermine cooperation where social capital is already high. The main outcome is cooperation rate, not direct efficiency or group payoff, but the authors discuss the implications for the evolution of cooperation and social capital.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by implication, efficiency) depends on the underlying level of reciprocity and the initial disposition of players. Punishment increases cooperation in environments where reciprocity is low (nonreciprocal), but can reduce cooperation among those already inclined to cooperate in high-reciprocity environments. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the marginal effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive in low-cooperation, low-reciprocity settings, but may be neutral or even negative in high-cooperation, high-reciprocity settings. However, since the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, these implications are inferred from cooperation rates. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, punishment cost, etc.) are well specified, but the main outcome is behavioral (cooperation), not payoff-based.

- source: Alexander, M., & Christia, F. (2011). Context Modularity of Human Altruism. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that costly punishment (sanctions) increases contributions to the public good, but only in institutional contexts characterized by integration (i.e., integrated schools). In segregated institutional contexts, the availability of punishment does not increase contributions. Group diversity (ethnic heterogeneity) reduces contributions in segregated contexts but not in integrated ones. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the results imply that the effect of punishment on efficiency would be positive in integrated contexts and negligible in segregated contexts, assuming higher contributions translate to higher group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency (proxied by contributions) is highly context-dependent: punishment increases contributions only when institutional integration is present. The game design dimensions most directly supported are player count (4), number of rounds (20), continuous contribution, no chat, and the presence/absence of punishment. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so predictions must infer from contribution rates. The findings suggest that, in similar field settings, enabling punishment will only improve efficiency if the institutional context supports integration among participants.

- source: Rockenbach, B., & Milinski, M. (2011). To qualify as a social partner, humans hide severe punishment, although their observed cooperativeness is decisive. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that in a public goods game with punishment, players strategically hide low contributions and severe punishment from an observer who may select future partners. Observers focus on observed contributions when making partner choices, largely ignoring punishment behavior. Punishment is used mainly to discipline low contributors, and its presence increases average contributions compared to the no-punishment game. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff as a main outcome, nor does it analyze the effect of punishment on efficiency directly. The main findings are about behavioral strategies for hiding and observing, and the determinants of partner selection.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punishment increases average contributions in a public goods game, but does not directly report on efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks, it supports the idea that punishment can raise cooperation, but the effect on efficiency must be inferred indirectly. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are reported and can be mapped, but the main outcomes are behavioral rather than payoff-based. The presence of strategic hiding and observation may moderate the effect of punishment, but the paper does not quantify this in terms of efficiency. Thus, the paper is informative for understanding behavioral mechanisms, but less directly useful for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Carpenter, J., Bowles, S., Gintis, H., & Hwang, S. H. (2009). Strong reciprocity and team production: Theory and evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing costly punishment in a public goods game prevents the typical decay in contributions over time, with average contributions rising or remaining stable across rounds. Punishment is widely used and is targeted at those who contribute less. The amount of punishment assigned increases with the degree of shirking and the harm imposed by shirking (i.e., higher MPCR), and decreases with group size. Individuals with higher reciprocity are more likely to punish, while those with higher unconditional altruism punish less. Punishment leads to increased contributions by shirkers, but the direct material return to the punisher is less than the cost incurred. The paper does not directly report group efficiency or total earnings, but the sustained high contributions suggest improved group welfare relative to the no-punishment baseline.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling costly peer punishment in public goods games increases and sustains cooperation (contribution rates) compared to the standard game without punishment. The effect is robust across different group sizes (4 and 8) and MPCRs (0.3 and 0.7), but the magnitude of punishment and contributions is sensitive to these parameters: punishment and contributions decrease as group size increases, and increase as MPCR increases. While the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, the sustained high contributions in the punishment condition imply higher efficiency than in the control. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment will increase efficiency, especially in smaller groups and with higher MPCR, but the effect is likely attenuated as group size increases.

- source: Hilbig, B. E., Zettler, I., & Heydasch, T. (2012). Personality, Punishment and Public Goods: Strategic Shifts Towards Cooperation as a Matter of Dispositional Honesty-Humility. *European Journal of Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The presence of punishment in a PGG increases individual contributions to the public good, replicating prior findings. This effect is moderated by the Honesty-Humility personality trait: individuals low in Honesty-Humility increase their contributions substantially when punishment is possible, while those high in Honesty-Humility contribute at a high level regardless of punishment. The study uses hypothetical (non-incentivized) PGGs, and the main outcome is contribution behavior, not actual efficiency or group payoff. A simulation based on observed contributions suggests that group efficiency (total group gain) increases with the number of high Honesty-Humility members, but this is not an experimental outcome. The findings are robust across two experiments, including one with a large, heterogeneous sample and a substantial time lag between personality and PGG measurement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment increases contributions in PGGs, especially among individuals low in Honesty-Humility. However, because the experiments are hypothetical and do not use real monetary incentives, and because efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured as an experimental outcome, the findings are most useful for predicting changes in contribution rates rather than efficiency per se. The simulation of group efficiency based on observed contributions provides indirect evidence that punishment could increase efficiency, but this is not a direct experimental result. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper offers informative indirect evidence that punishment increases contributions (and thus likely efficiency), but the lack of real incentives and direct efficiency measurement are important limitations.

- source: Carpenter, J., Matthews, P., & Ong'ong'a, O. (2004). Why Punish? Social reciprocity and the enforcement of prosocial norms. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment in public goods games is best explained by social reciprocity: individuals are motivated to punish norm violators (free riders) even when doing so is costly and does not improve their own material payoff, and even when the violator is outside their immediate group. Experimental and survey evidence shows that punishment is not solely driven by the desire to reduce payoff differentials, and that people continue to punish even when the cost of punishment exceeds the harm inflicted. The demand for punishment is relatively inelastic to price, and motivations are primarily normative. While the models present payoff calculations and show that punishment can stabilize cooperation under certain initial conditions, the empirical focus is on behavioral motivations and patterns rather than direct measures of group efficiency or total earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment mechanisms in public goods games can sustain higher levels of cooperation, but it does not directly report on group efficiency or total payoff outcomes. The theoretical models show that, under certain conditions, populations with punishers can achieve higher average payoffs than those dominated by free riders, but these are model-based results rather than empirical efficiency measurements. The experimental and survey data are primarily about who punishes, why, and under what conditions, rather than about the quantitative effect of punishment on group efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper suggests that punishment can support cooperation even when it is costly, and that motivations are primarily normative, but it does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, punishment cost, ability to punish outgroup) are discussed in the context of behavioral patterns, not efficiency outcomes.

- source: Van Vugt, M., & De Cremer, D. (1999). Leadership in social dilemmas: The effects of group identification on collective actions to provide public goods. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) Instrumental (punishing) leaders increase contributions to the public good, especially when group identification is low. (2) Relational (encouraging) leaders only increase contributions when group identification is high. (3) In low group identification groups, relational leaders are ineffective and may even be counterproductive. (4) The legitimacy of the leader mediates the effect on contributions. (5) There is a general preference for democratic, elected, and internal leaders, especially when group identification is high. (6) No direct group payoff or efficiency outcomes are reported, but the pattern of contribution changes is clear.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness of punishment (via an instrumental leader) in increasing contributions depends on group identification. In low group identification settings, punishment is much more effective than encouragement. In high group identification settings, both punishment and encouragement can be effective. However, the paper does not report group payoff or efficiency, so its direct value for predicting efficiency is limited. The design details (player count, rounds, punishment cost, etc.) are partially specified and can inform dimension coding, but the main outcome is contribution, not efficiency.

- source: Szolnoki, A., Szabó, G., & Czakó, L. (2011). Competition of individual and institutional punishments in spatial public goods games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in spatial public goods games with both peer and pool punishment, the prevailing strategy depends sensitively on the synergy factor (MPCR analog), punishment cost, and fine. Peer punishers often dominate and are more effective at eliminating defectors, especially at lower synergy factors where cooperation would otherwise collapse. However, when both punishment types are present, their competition can sometimes undermine the effectiveness of punishment, leading to the reemergence of defectors. The paper provides detailed phase diagrams mapping out the conditions under which each strategy or combination of strategies prevails. The findings are based on the frequencies of strategies and do not directly quantify group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how the presence and type of punishment (peer vs pool), as well as the synergy factor (MPCR), punishment cost, and fine, affect the prevalence of cooperation and defection in spatial PGGs. However, because the outcomes are reported as strategy frequencies and not as group efficiency or payoff, the results must be interpreted as indirect evidence for efficiency. The elimination of defectors and the dominance of cooperators or punishers can be taken as a proxy for higher efficiency, but the paper does not provide quantitative efficiency values. The detailed mapping of parameter regimes is useful for predicting when punishment will be effective, but direct prediction of efficiency outcomes requires caution.

- source: Szolnoki, A., Szabó, G., & Perc, M. (2011). Phase diagrams for the spatial public goods game with pool punishment. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that pool punishment in spatial public goods games can support the coexistence of cooperation and punishment, but only within limited parameter regions (low synergy factor, low punishment cost, and intermediate fines). For high fines, the system often evolves into a cyclic dominance phase where defectors, cooperators, and pool punishers coexist in a rock-paper-scissors dynamic. Pool punishment is less effective than peer punishment at eliminating defectors, and cooperators can invade pool punishers' territories due to the cost of punishment. The paper provides detailed phase diagrams for different values of the synergy factor, showing the boundaries between homogeneous, coexistence, and cyclic phases. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so its findings are most directly relevant to predicting the prevalence of cooperation and punishment strategies rather than efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed evidence on how the cost and effectiveness of pool punishment, as well as the synergy factor (mpcr), affect the prevalence of cooperation and punishment in spatial public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the phase diagrams and strategy frequencies can inform predictions about when punishment is likely to increase or decrease efficiency, especially in structured populations with spatial interactions. The findings suggest that pool punishment is only effective in a narrow range of parameters and can lead to complex coexistence rather than full cooperation. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the paper's results are only indirectly informative, as they do not quantify group payoff or efficiency directly.

- source: Helbing, D., Szolnoki, A., Perc, M., & Szabó, G. (2010). Defector-accelerated cooperativeness and punishment in public goods games with mutations. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in spatial public goods games, costly punishment (when fines are sufficiently high relative to costs) allows punishing cooperators to outcompete defectors and non-punishing cooperators, solving the second-order free-rider problem in structured populations. This effect is robust to small and moderate mutation rates, but high mutation rates (which mimic well-mixed conditions) restore the tragedy of the commons by enabling defectors to dominate. The presence of even tiny mutation rates can dramatically accelerate the spread of punishing cooperators by temporarily introducing defectors, which punishing cooperators can then outcompete. The main outcomes are the stationary fractions of each strategy, not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatially structured public goods games, enabling punishment (with sufficiently high fine-to-cost ratio) can lead to the dominance of punishing cooperators and the elimination of defectors and non-punishing cooperators, especially at moderate mutation rates. While efficiency is not directly measured, the dominance of punishing cooperators is likely associated with higher group payoff than defector-dominated states. The results suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the structure of interactions (spatial vs. well-mixed) and to mutation rates. For prediction tasks, the presence of spatial structure, the fine-to-cost ratio, and mutation rate are critical moderators of punishment's effect on group outcomes.

- source: Helbing, D., Szolnoki, A., Perc, M., & Szabó, G. (2010). Punish, but not too hard: how costly punishment spreads in the spatial public goods game. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in spatial public goods games, the introduction of punishing cooperators (PC) or punishing defectors (PD) can promote the spread of cooperation, but through different mechanisms. For punishing cooperators, increasing the punishment fine monotonically increases the cooperation level up to a threshold, after which non-punishing cooperators and defectors are eliminated. For punishing defectors, there is a non-monotonic (reentrant) effect: increasing the fine initially increases cooperation, but too high a fine reduces the fraction of punishing defectors, causing cooperation to collapse and the system to revert to defection. The results are presented as phase diagrams and time-evolution of strategy fractions, not as direct efficiency or group payoff measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and type of punishment mechanism, as well as the punishment cost and fine, can have large effects on the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games. However, because the outcomes are reported as fractions of strategies and cooperation level, not as group efficiency or total payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency. The results suggest that enabling punishment (especially by punishing cooperators) can increase cooperation and likely efficiency, but the relationship is mediated by the spatial structure and the specific punishment parameters. The non-monotonic effect for punishing defectors highlights the importance of tuning punishment severity. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment can improve efficiency in structured populations, but the mapping to efficiency must be inferred from cooperation levels.

- source: Boyd, R., Gintis, H., Bowles, S., & Richerson, P. J. (2003). The evolution of altruistic punishment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that adding punishment to a public goods game model with group selection allows for the maintenance of high levels of cooperation in much larger groups than group selection alone. The effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation depends on the cost structure of punishment (punishment must be more costly to the punished than to the punisher, and costs to punishers must decline as defectors become rare). The model is robust to various parameter changes, but does not directly report efficiency or group payoff; instead, it focuses on the frequency of cooperation and punishment as the main outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that punishment can increase and sustain cooperation in public goods game-like environments, especially in larger groups, under certain cost structures. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, so its value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is indirect. It is most useful for understanding the mechanisms and conditions under which punishment is likely to have a positive effect on cooperation, which is often correlated with higher efficiency, but this link is not explicitly modeled or measured in terms of payoffs.

- source: Traulsen, A., Hauert, C., De Silva, H., Nowak, M. A., & Sigmund, K. (2009). Exploration dynamics in evolutionary games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in finite-population public goods games with punishment, the rate of random exploration (mutation) crucially shapes the evolutionary outcome. For low mutation rates, defectors dominate in compulsory games, and cooperation/punishment only emerge with voluntary participation. For high mutation rates, cooperators can dominate even in compulsory games, with punishers playing a pivotal but not dominant role. The analysis is based on theoretical models and simulations, reporting the average abundance of each strategy type. The paper does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff, but the prevalence of cooperation and punishment is interpreted as beneficial for group outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation in public goods games is highly sensitive to the rate of random exploration (mutation) in the population. It suggests that punishment alone does not guarantee high cooperation or efficiency unless exploration rates are high or participation is voluntary. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is indirect: it supports the idea that punishment's effect on efficiency is contingent on the broader evolutionary or behavioral context, especially the rate of exploration/mutation. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful for understanding when punishment is likely to increase cooperation, but less so for quantifying efficiency changes.

- source: Wang, J., Wu, B., Ho, D. W. C., & Wang, L. (2011). Evolution of cooperation in multilevel public goods games with community structures. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that in multilevel PGGs with community structure, the prevalence of cooperation and punishment depends on the strengths of imitation within and between communities. When within-community imitation is weak or between-community imitation is strong, cooperation and punishment are more abundant than defection. If both imitation strengths are strong, defection dominates. The model also finds that, under moderate within-community imitation and small between-community imitation, punishers can become the most abundant strategy while cooperators nearly disappear. The analysis is based on the stationary distribution of homogeneous states (all-cooperator, all-defector, all-punisher) and fixation probabilities, not on direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how punishment and cooperation can be sustained in multilevel PGGs with community structure, depending on imitation dynamics. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff with and without punishment. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that punishment can increase the prevalence of cooperation under certain imitation regimes, which may imply higher efficiency, but this is not directly modeled or quantified. The results are most useful for understanding the conditions under which punishment and cooperation are likely to be sustained, rather than for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: García, J., & Traulsen, A. (2012). Leaving the loners alone: Evolution of cooperation in the presence of antisocial punishment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary success of cooperation and punishment in public goods games with voluntary participation depends critically on whether loners (abstainers) can be punished or can punish others. If loners are truly isolated (neither punish nor are punished), cooperators who punish defectors become dominant in the population, even when antisocial punishment is allowed. If loners can be punished, cooperation and punishment do not evolve, and the population is split among cooperators, defectors, and loners, with no strategy dominating. The results are robust across different strategy sets and selection intensities. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff; instead, it focuses on the abundance of strategies as the main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the ability to punish or be punished, especially for abstainers (loners), is a critical moderator of whether punishment can support cooperation in public goods games. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that if the game design allows abstainers to be punished, punishment is unlikely to increase efficiency or cooperation. If abstainers are truly isolated, punishment can support the dominance of cooperative strategies. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results are most useful for understanding which game design features (especially the treatment of abstainers) are likely to enable or undermine the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation.

- source: Balliet, D., Mulder, L. B., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2011). Reward, Punishment, and Cooperation: A Meta-Analysis. *Psychological Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, punishment_cost, reward_cost
  findings: The meta-analysis finds that both punishment and reward robustly increase cooperation in public goods and related social dilemma games. The effect of punishment is stronger when it is costly to the punisher and when participants interact repeatedly with the same group (partner matching). Decentralized punishment is more effective than centralized punishment, but this is largely explained by the correlation with costliness. Group size and cost-to-fine ratio do not significantly moderate the effect. While the main outcomes are cooperation rates, the paper discusses that punishment can reduce group efficiency in the short run (due to punishment costs) but may increase efficiency in the long run as cooperation stabilizes and punishment use declines. The effect of punishment varies by country, with some countries (e.g., Russia) showing no positive effect. The paper does not provide meta-analytic estimates of efficiency or group payoff, but references to primary studies suggest that efficiency effects are context-dependent and may be negative in the short term and positive in the long term.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods and similar social dilemma games, enabling punishment increases cooperation rates, especially when punishment is costly and the game is repeated with fixed partners. However, the paper does not provide direct meta-analytic estimates of efficiency or group payoff, which are the target outcomes for the prediction task. The discussion and references to primary studies suggest that efficiency may initially decrease when punishment is introduced (due to punishment costs), but can increase over time as cooperation rises and punishment use declines. For prediction, this means that the effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be more positive in longer, repeated games with partner matching and costly punishment, but may be less positive or even negative in short or one-shot games, or when punishment is free or centralized. The lack of direct efficiency outcomes in the meta-analysis limits the precision of quantitative prediction for efficiency, but the qualitative patterns are informative for understanding moderators.

- source: Fehr, E., & Fischbacher, U. (2004). Social norms and human cooperation. *Trends in Cognitive Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the opportunity to punish norm violators in public goods games leads to a dramatic increase in cooperation rates, often approaching full cooperation. Punishment is frequently administered by group members or even third parties, and is motivated by non-selfish concerns for norm enforcement rather than self-interest. The costliness of punishment reduces its frequency and strength, but punishment remains prevalent even when it is costly. The presence of punishment opportunities transforms the dynamics of cooperation, preventing the typical decline in contributions over time seen in standard public goods games without punishment. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative analysis of efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong qualitative evidence that enabling punishment in public goods games leads to much higher and more stable cooperation rates, and that the effect is robust across various experimental designs. For the downstream prediction task, the paper supports the expectation that introducing punishment will increase efficiency, provided that efficiency is closely linked to contribution rates. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes directly, its value is primarily in establishing the behavioral mechanism and the robustness of the punishment effect on cooperation. It also highlights that the cost and effectiveness of punishment, as well as group size and information structure, are important moderators, but does not provide quantitative estimates for these effects on efficiency.

It reviews evidence that, in public goods games, cooperation typically decays over time unless costly punishment is available, in which case cooperation is sustained or even increases. The paper references Fehr & Gächter's experiments showing that allowing punishment prevents the decay of cooperation, but does not report efficiency or group payoff data. The main findings are about the prevalence and rationality of prosocial and punitive behaviors, not about their impact on group efficiency or welfare.

However, it does not provide quantitative evidence or direct analysis of efficiency or group payoff outcomes, which limits its direct utility for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions. The referenced experiments (e.g., Fehr & Gächter) are more directly relevant for such predictions, but this paper itself does not supply the necessary outcome data.

Punishment is frequently used, mostly by high contributors targeting low contributors. The authors argue that punishment can induce and stabilize cooperation, and discuss the evolutionary puzzle of why individuals punish at a cost to themselves. However, it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it analyze how game design dimensions moderate the effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it supports the expectation that punishment increases cooperation, which is often associated with higher efficiency, but does not provide data or analysis to calibrate this relationship or its dependence on design features.

  findings: The paper reviews evidence that punishment of non-cooperators in Public Goods Games and related paradigms increases cooperation and reduces free-riding, with punishment typically administered by cooperators and targeted at defectors. It discusses the neural correlates of punishment and cooperation, the evolutionary rationale for sanctioning, and the influence of individual differences (e.g., personality, genetics, psychopathology) and contextual factors (e.g., culture, gender, resource availability) on social exchange behavior. However, it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff effects of punishment.

However, it does not report or synthesize quantitative efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used to directly predict the magnitude of efficiency changes due to punishment. - source: Fehr, E., & Rockenbach, B. (2004). Human altruism: economic, neural, and evolutionary perspectives. *Current Opinion in Neurobiology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper reviews evidence that the availability of costly punishment in public goods games leads to much higher and more stable cooperation levels compared to games without punishment. Voluntary selection into punishment regimes further increases cooperation compared to exogenous assignment. The review also highlights that punishment is only effective at promoting cooperation when it is perceived as fair or socially justified. Neuroeconomic studies show that both cooperation and the act of punishing defectors activate reward-related brain circuits, suggesting a neural basis for strong reciprocity. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative results on efficiency or group payoff, instead focusing on behavioral outcomes and neural mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong qualitative support that enabling punishment in public goods games increases cooperation and contributions, especially when punishment is voluntary and perceived as justified. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks, this review supports the expectation that punishment will increase efficiency when it increases cooperation, but the magnitude and conditions for this effect must be inferred from other sources or from the primary studies it cites.

- source: Singer, T., & Steinbeis, N. (2009). Differential Roles of Fairness- and Compassion-Based Motivations for Cooperation, Defection, and Punishment. *Values, Empathy, and Fairness Across Social Barriers*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that fairness-based motivation leads to punishment of norm violations, which can sustain cooperation in public goods games, as shown in referenced empirical studies. Compassion-based motivation, in contrast, can prevent the breakdown of cooperation in noisy environments by promoting forgiveness, but may not be effective in the long term against persistent defectors. The emotional and neural mechanisms underlying these motivations are distinct. The paper does not provide new empirical results or direct measures of efficiency or group payoff, but summarizes that punishment options in public goods games are associated with higher cooperation rates and norm enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and literature-based support for the idea that enabling punishment in public goods games increases cooperation rates and helps sustain cooperation, referencing empirical studies that show this effect. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the general expectation that punishment increases cooperation (and thus likely efficiency), but does not allow for fine-grained prediction based on game design dimensions. It is most useful for understanding the motivational and emotional mechanisms behind punishment and cooperation, and for contextualizing why punishment may be effective in sustaining cooperation.

- source: Jensen, K. (2010). Punishment and spite, the dark side of cooperation. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper argues that punishment, including altruistic and third-party forms, is a key mechanism for maintaining cooperation in humans, especially in public goods game contexts. It reviews evidence from the literature that allowing punishment in public goods games increases cooperation, but it does not provide new quantitative results on efficiency or group payoff. The paper also discusses the psychological and evolutionary underpinnings of punishment and spite, suggesting that negative other-regarding preferences (spite) may be as important as positive ones (empathy) in shaping human cooperation. The review highlights that while punishment can deter free-riding and enforce norms, it can also be motivated by antisocial preferences, and the net effect on group welfare or efficiency is not always clear from the literature.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and conceptual support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation in public goods games, referencing experimental findings from the literature. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the inclusion of punishment as a potentially positive moderator of cooperation, but does not allow for precise quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes. It is useful for understanding mechanisms and for contextualizing empirical findings, but not for direct parameterization of efficiency effects.

- source: Cinyabuguma, M., Page, T., & Putterman, L. (2005). Cooperation under the threat of expulsion in a public goods experiment. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compared a standard public goods game (VCM) with a treatment in which group members could vote to expel others. In the expulsion treatment, contributions rose to nearly 100% of endowments, and average earnings (efficiency) were significantly higher than in the baseline. Expulsion was used sparingly, but the threat alone was enough to sustain cooperation. Expelled members earned much less, but overall group efficiency was still higher with expulsion. When the expulsion threat was removed, cooperation collapsed. The results suggest that the threat of exclusion is a powerful mechanism for sustaining cooperation and improving efficiency in public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a credible expulsion (ostracism) mechanism in a public goods game with known group size, fixed rounds, and full information about others' contributions can substantially increase efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust even when the punishment (expulsion) is rarely used, as the threat alone is sufficient. The design dimensions—large group size (16), 15 known rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, no reward option, and full feedback—are all specified, making the findings directly applicable to prediction tasks involving similar settings. The expulsion mechanism is a form of binary, irreversible punishment with a small cost to punishers. The results suggest that, in similar environments, enabling expulsion or strong exclusionary sanctions will lead to much higher efficiency than control.

- source: Maier-Rigaud, F. P., Martinsson, P., & Staffiero, G. (2010). Ostracism and the provision of a public good: experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
