  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that allowing group members to ostracize (exclude) others by majority vote in a repeated linear public goods game leads to a substantial and statistically significant increase in group contributions and average earnings (efficiency) compared to the standard game without ostracism. This effect is robust except in the first and last periods, where contributions are similar across treatments due to initial uncertainty and end-game effects. Ostracism is targeted at low contributors, and after initial exclusions, remaining members tend to converge to high contribution levels. The positive effect on efficiency is observed both when ostracism is introduced first or second in the session, and is higher than in the control. The design ensures that the only difference between treatments is the possibility of exclusion, and there is no direct monetary punishment or cost to voting. The results suggest that ostracism is an effective endogenous sanctioning mechanism for sustaining cooperation and increasing group welfare in public goods environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling ostracism (exclusion by majority vote) in a repeated linear public goods game with fixed partners, full information on others' contributions, and no cost to voting, leads to a substantial increase in group efficiency (average earnings as a fraction of the social optimum) compared to the same game without ostracism. The effect is robust across session orderings and is not offset by the reduction in group size due to exclusion. For prediction tasks, the presence of an ostracism mechanism (even with no direct monetary punishment) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, especially in games with similar design parameters (6 players, 10 rounds, continuous contributions, MPCR=0.4, no chat, no reward, no punishment cost, anonymous feedback on contributions). The effect is strongest in the middle rounds and may diminish at the end due to end-game effects.

- source: Masclet, D. (2003). Ostracism in work teams: a public good experiment. *International Journal of Manpower*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that allowing group members to exclude (ostracize) others after observing their contributions in a public good game leads to much higher contributions and significantly higher group payoffs compared to a control without exclusion. Both costless and costly exclusion treatments yield similar increases in efficiency, with group payoffs rising by about 28-30% relative to the control. Actual exclusions are rare, but the threat is sufficient to sustain cooperation. The effect is robust to whether exclusion is directly costly or not. The study provides direct evidence that non-monetary peer punishment (ostracism) can substantially improve efficiency in public goods environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling exclusion (ostracism) in a standard public good game with four players and continuous contributions leads to a large and robust increase in efficiency (group payoff), regardless of whether exclusion is directly costly or not. For prediction tasks, the presence of an exclusion mechanism (even if rarely used) should be expected to substantially increase efficiency relative to a control without punishment, under similar game parameters (n=4, 10 rounds, MPCR=0.4, no chat, continuous contributions, no rewards). The effect is observed even when the control efficiency is low and is robust to the cost structure of exclusion. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment-like mechanisms (ostracism) in similar lab PGG designs.

- source: Güth, W., Levati, M. V., Sutter, M., & Van der Heijden, E. (2007). Leading by example with and without exclusion power in voluntary contribution experiments. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a linear public goods game with four players and 24 rounds, the introduction of a leader who moves first increases contributions and group profits compared to the standard simultaneous game. When the leader is given exclusion power (the ability to exclude a follower from the next round), both contributions and group profits rise substantially more. The effect of exclusion power is robust to whether the leader is fixed or rotates among group members. In the endogenous phase, only about 40% of groups successfully appoint a leader, but those that do achieve much higher contributions and profits than those that do not. The presence of exclusion power (punishment) is a strong moderator of the positive effect on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment via exclusion power in a public goods game with leadership substantially increases group efficiency compared to both the standard game and leadership without punishment. The effect is robust across fixed and rotating leader appointment. The control (no leader) game yields the lowest efficiency, leadership without punishment yields a moderate increase, and leadership with punishment yields the highest efficiency. For prediction, the presence of punishment (exclusion), the marginal per-capita return, and the group size are all well-specified and informative. The absence of communication, the use of continuous contribution, and the lack of reward options are also clearly specified. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in similar game designs.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2012). Evolutionary advantages of adaptive rewarding. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Adaptive rewarding in spatial public goods games can eliminate defectors and promote high levels of cooperation, outperforming steady (non-adaptive) rewarding. The effectiveness of adaptive rewarding depends on the synergy factor (r), the cost of rewarding (alpha), and the strength of the reward (Delta). When rewards are strong and not too costly, adaptive rewarding can lead to defector-free states, interpreted as maximal efficiency. However, adaptive punishment is even more effective: for the same cost, punishment achieves defector elimination at lower required intensity. The paper provides detailed phase diagrams showing under what parameter regimes each strategy dominates, coexists, or is eliminated. The main efficiency outcome is the prevalence of defector-free states, which is used as a proxy for group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatial public goods games, adaptive punishment is more effective than adaptive rewarding at achieving maximal efficiency (defector-free states), given the same cost-to-impact ratio. The results are highly informative for predicting the effect of introducing punishment (or reward) in spatial PGGs: punishment is more likely to yield higher efficiency than reward, especially when the cost of sanctioning is not prohibitive. The findings are most directly applicable to spatial, lattice-based PGGs with local interactions, and may be less directly transferable to well-mixed or non-spatial settings. The paper's phase diagrams and minimum required cost-to-impact ratios for defector elimination can inform quantitative predictions about efficiency under different game design parameters.

- source: Gerber, A., & Wichardt, P. C. (2009). Providing public goods in the absence of strong institutions. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a voluntary deposit-commitment mechanism in public goods games can fully implement efficient contribution profiles as equilibrium outcomes, even in the absence of strong external enforcement or punishment institutions. The mechanism works by making non-contribution costly (loss of deposit) only if all players commit, thus aligning individual incentives with group welfare. Theoretical results show that, under this mechanism, the group can achieve payoffs and efficiency levels that strictly Pareto-dominate the standard Nash equilibrium of zero contribution. The mechanism is robust to various extensions, including repeated games and alternative uses of forfeited deposits.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that self-commitment mechanisms (such as refundable deposits) can substitute for traditional punishment in public goods games, leading to higher efficiency and group payoffs. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, in environments where such a deposit-commitment mechanism is available and properly designed, the efficiency with 'punishment' (here, self-sanctioning via deposit) can approach the social optimum, even if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low. The results are most directly applicable to settings where the deposit mechanism is feasible and all players can observe and verify commitments. The paper does not provide empirical estimates or parameterized predictions, but the qualitative direction is clear: enabling credible self-sanctioning or commitment mechanisms can dramatically improve efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Gintis, H., Smith, E. A., & Bowles, S. (2001). Costly signaling and cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a multi-player public goods game, honest signaling of quality by providing a public good (or by punishing defectors) can be an evolutionarily stable strategy, provided that the cost of signaling is lower for high-quality individuals and that the benefits of alliances or partnerships are sufficiently high. The signaling equilibrium yields higher average group payoffs than the non-signaling equilibrium. The model also shows that punishment can function as a costly signal, and that under certain conditions, prosocial signals (including punishment) are more likely to evolve than neutral or antisocial signals. The model provides explicit conditions for when signaling equilibria exist and are stable, and analyzes the evolutionary dynamics leading to these equilibria.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling costly signaling (including punishment) in public goods games can increase group efficiency and average payoff, even in the absence of repeated interactions or reciprocity. The model is directly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, especially in settings where punishment is interpreted as a costly signal of quality. The findings suggest that, when the cost structure and benefit parameters are appropriate, introducing punishment (as a form of signaling) should increase efficiency relative to a control without punishment. The paper also highlights the importance of the cost differential between high- and low-quality individuals, the size of the group, and the benefits of alliances in determining the stability and efficiency of the signaling equilibrium. However, as a theoretical model, it does not provide empirical effect sizes or direct experimental evidence.

- source: Galbiati, R., & Vertova, P. (2008). Obligations and cooperative behaviour in public good games. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that, for a given structure of low, fixed marginal incentives (probabilistic monitoring, penalty, and reward), increasing the minimum required contribution (the 'obligation') leads to higher average contributions in a repeated public good game. This effect is significant only when the obligation is set high (80% of endowment); a low obligation (40%) does not significantly increase contributions relative to no obligation. Contributions decline over time in all conditions, but the rate of decline is slower with higher obligations. Unexpected increases in the minimum contribution obligation cause a temporary jump in contributions, while decreases do not reverse the downward trend. The study does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes, so the effect on efficiency is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated public good games with low-probability, low-magnitude punishment and reward, increasing the minimum required contribution (obligation) can increase average contributions, especially when the obligation is set high. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes. The results suggest that, under similar game parameters (6 players, 10 rounds, continuous contribution, no chat, fixed MPCR, probabilistic monitoring, anonymous play), introducing or raising a minimum contribution obligation (with fixed marginal incentives) will likely increase contributions, but may not sustain them over time. The effect on efficiency would depend on the cost of monitoring and the penalty/reward structure, which are not analyzed in terms of group payoff. The findings are most relevant for predicting contribution behavior, not efficiency.

- source: Kerr, N. L., Rumble, A. C., Park, E. S., Ouwerkerk, J. W., Parks, C. D., Gallucci, M., & van Lange, P. A. M. (2009). How many bad apples does it take to spoil the whole barrel?: Social exclusion and toleration for bad apples. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games, the presence of even a single 'bad apple' (defector) can sharply reduce cooperation among other group members when there is no threat of social exclusion. However, when group members can be excluded for uncooperative behavior (i.e., there is a credible threat of social exclusion), participants are much less likely to follow the bad apple's example, and cooperation remains high unless a majority of the group are defectors. This deterrent effect of exclusion is strong in small groups (4-5 people) but disappears in larger groups (8 people), where exclusion threats do not increase cooperation. The dependent variable is contribution/cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff, but the findings imply that exclusion threats would increase group efficiency in small groups.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence that enabling a social exclusion mechanism (a form of non-monetary punishment) in small public goods games (4-5 players) increases cooperation and would likely increase efficiency, especially when the baseline (control) efficiency is degraded by the presence of defectors. However, the effect of exclusion threats disappears in larger groups (8 players), suggesting that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment via exclusion is moderated by group size. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so predictions must be inferred from cooperation rates. The exclusion mechanism studied is not monetary punishment but social ostracism, so results may not generalize to all punishment technologies.

- source: Milinski, M., Semmann, D., & Krambeck, H. J. (2002). Reputation helps solve the 'tragedy of the commons'. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when public goods games are alternated with indirect reciprocity games (where players' reputations are visible and affect future help), cooperation in the public goods game remains high and group payoffs are significantly higher than in treatments where public goods games are played in blocks without reputation incentives. When the possibility of future indirect reciprocity is removed (by announcing only public goods rounds remain), cooperation and efficiency quickly decline. Thus, reputation-based incentives can substitute for direct punishment in maintaining cooperation and efficiency in public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mechanisms which link reputation to future rewards (indirect reciprocity) can sustain high efficiency in public goods games, even in the absence of explicit punishment. For prediction tasks, it suggests that the presence of reputation/reward mechanisms (even without punishment) can lead to high efficiency, especially when players expect future interactions where their reputation matters. The findings are most relevant for settings where reputation is salient and can affect future payoffs, and less directly informative for games with explicit costly punishment but no reputation/reward linkage.

- source: Carpenter, J., & Seki, E. (2011). DO SOCIAL PREFERENCES INCREASE PRODUCTIVITY? FIELD EXPERIMENTAL EVIDENCE FROM FISHERMEN IN TOYAMA BAY. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study uses a field experiment with fishermen and students, employing a repeated public goods game (VCM) with a costly, non-material 'social disapproval' mechanism (unhappy face) in the last five rounds. Pooling fishermen (who share catch and income) are more productive in real life, and this is linked to higher social preference measures from the experiment. The social disapproval mechanism increases contributions among poolers, and social preference measures (conditional cooperation, propensity to disapprove, response to disapproval) are significant predictors of both experimental contributions and real-world fishing productivity. The effect of social preferences on productivity is substantial, and the productivity advantage of pooling boats is largely explained by these preferences. The 'punishment' in the experiment is not material, so the findings are most directly about social (rather than monetary) sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that social preference mechanisms, even when implemented as costly but non-material signals (social disapproval), can increase both experimental and real-world efficiency in public-goods-like environments. However, the 'punishment' here is not material: it is a costly signal with no direct monetary penalty. The findings suggest that enabling even non-material, costly social sanctions can increase efficiency, especially among groups with strong social ties or cooperative institutions. For prediction tasks, the presence of a social disapproval mechanism (even without material punishment) is likely to increase efficiency, particularly in groups with pre-existing cooperative norms. The results are most applicable to settings where punishment is non-material but salient, and less directly informative about material punishment effects.

- source: Semmann, D., Krambeck, H. J., & Milinski, M. (2004). Strategic investment in reputation. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: This experiment demonstrates that the possibility of reputation transfer between a public goods game and an indirect reciprocity game leads to higher cooperation and significantly higher average payoffs in the public goods game. When players' identities are consistent across both games (transferable name), they invest more in cooperation, resulting in higher group earnings. When identities are not transferable, cooperation and payoffs decline. The manipulation of reputation transferability is the key treatment; no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. The findings show that reputation incentives can substitute for punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling reputation transfer between a public goods game and another social context (indirect reciprocity game) increases efficiency, as measured by average payoff per player. While the study does not manipulate punishment, it is highly informative for prediction tasks involving reputation mechanisms as an alternative to punishment. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, MPCR, number of rounds, information structure) are clearly specified, and the efficiency outcomes are directly reported. For prediction, this paper supports the view that mechanisms increasing the salience or transferability of reputation can have a strong positive effect on efficiency, even in the absence of punishment.

- source: Kitts, J. A. (2006). Collective action, rival incentives, and the emergence of antisocial norms. *American Sociological Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that collective action and group efficiency in public-goods-like environments are shaped by a three-way interaction between the value of selective incentives (rewards), the rivalness of those incentives, and the strength of peer influence (cohesiveness). When incentives are nonrival, increasing their value or increasing peer influence both increase participation and group welfare. When incentives are rival, however, strong peer influence can lead to the enforcement of antisocial norms that discourage contribution, reducing group efficiency. The model demonstrates that under high rivalness and high incentive value, increasing peer influence or incentive value can actually decrease collective action and group welfare. The findings are robust across a range of parameter values and are most applicable to small groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and computational evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, rival selective incentives and norm enforcement) on group efficiency is highly contingent on the design dimensions of the incentive system and the strength of peer influence. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling strong peer enforcement or increasing the value of selective incentives will not always increase efficiency—if the incentives are rival, these interventions can backfire and reduce group welfare. The model is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with reward-based incentives, especially when those rewards are rival. While the paper does not model punishment per se, the logic and results are highly informative for any system where sanctions or rewards are rival and peer enforcement is possible.

- source: Hamman, J. R., Weber, R. A., & Woon, J. (2011). An Experimental Investigation of Electoral Delegation and the Provision of Public Goods. *American Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Electoral delegation, where a group elects a member to allocate contributions in a linear public goods game, results in sustained high efficiency and group payoffs, with most groups achieving or nearly achieving the social optimum. In contrast, standard voluntary contribution games (with or without communication) see contributions and efficiency decline to near zero. Communication boosts efficiency, but only when paired with delegation does it sustain high contributions. When delegation is endogenous (players can opt in), high efficiency is only achieved if communication is allowed, as it facilitates coordination and trust. The main mechanism is the selection of prosocial allocators and the ability to sanction selfish ones. Majority tyranny (where a coalition exploits a minority) is observed but infrequent. The results show that democratic delegation can overcome the free-rider problem and lead to efficient public goods provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with a low MPCR and large groups, introducing a democratic delegation mechanism (electoral selection of a central allocator) dramatically increases efficiency compared to standard voluntary contribution mechanisms. Communication further enhances this effect, especially when delegation is endogenous. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of electoral delegation (even without explicit punishment) can substitute for or outperform punishment in achieving high efficiency, especially when communication is allowed. The control efficiency (voluntary contribution) is very low in this design, but efficiency with delegation is near the social optimum. The results are most applicable to settings with large groups, low MPCR, and centralized decision-making via election.

- source: Sasaki, T., & Unemi, T. (2011). Replicator dynamics in public goods games with reward funds. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a compulsory public goods game with a pool-rewarding mechanism, cooperation and high efficiency can be maintained if the net benefit from the reward fund exceeds the cost of contributing to the public good. Specifically, if c2*(r2-1) > sigma (where sigma is the net cost of contributing to the public good), the population can converge to all rewarders (full efficiency) or exhibit stable cycles with high average payoff. If this condition is not met, defectors dominate and efficiency collapses. The model also shows that introducing second-order sanctions (reducing rewards to second-order free riders) can stabilize full cooperation. The findings are directly relevant to predicting efficiency in PGG-like games with reward mechanisms, but only indirectly inform about punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games with reward funds, the efficiency outcome depends critically on the relative effectiveness and cost of the reward mechanism compared to the cost of cooperation. The explicit condition c2*(r2-1) > sigma can be used to predict when enabling a reward mechanism will increase efficiency relative to the control (no reward) case. However, the paper does not analyze punishment, so its direct value for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency is limited. The model structure and results may be informative by analogy for understanding how the cost and effectiveness of sanctions (punishment or reward) interact with game parameters (group size, MPCR, etc.) to determine efficiency outcomes.

- source: Hauert, C. (2010). Replicator dynamics of reward & reputation in public goods games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games, the opportunity to reward contributors (with or without reputation) can encourage the emergence of cooperation if rewards are sufficiently appealing and visible (via reputation). However, reward alone cannot stabilize cooperation, as second-order free-riders (who cooperate but do not reward) undermine the system, leading to cycles of cooperation and defection. The analysis is based on replicator dynamics and explicit payoff calculations for different strategy types. The paper contrasts these findings with the literature on punishment, noting that punishment can stabilize cooperation but is less effective at establishing it. Efficiency (average population payoff) is analyzed as a function of the strategic composition of the population, but the paper does not provide direct results for punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into the mechanisms by which reward and reputation affect efficiency and cooperation in public goods games. It does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency, but its discussion of the contrasting roles of reward and punishment can inform predictions: reward can increase cooperation and thus efficiency in the short term, but cannot maintain high efficiency due to second-order free-riding. Punishment, as discussed from prior literature, can stabilize high efficiency once cooperation is established. The explicit payoff equations and analysis of group size, reward cost/benefit, and reputation can inform parameterization of models, but direct quantitative predictions for punishment effects are not provided.

- source: Bergstrom, T. C. (2002). Evolution of social behavior: Individual and group selection. *Journal of Economic Perspectives*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in linear public goods games (multiplayer prisoners' dilemmas), cooperation cannot be sustained in equilibrium under nonassortative (random) matching and individual selection; only defectors survive, leading to low group efficiency. However, cooperation and higher group payoffs can be sustained if (a) matching is assortative (e.g., kin selection, spatial structure, partner choice), (b) the game is not a prisoners' dilemma (e.g., stag hunt), or (c) the game is repeated and social norms (including punishment) are enforced. The paper discusses the theoretical role of punishment in repeated games as a means to sustain cooperation and efficient outcomes, but does not provide direct analysis or predictions about the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical context for the importance of game structure (e.g., matching, repetition, group size, payoff parameters) in determining whether cooperation and high efficiency can be sustained in public goods games. It highlights that punishment can, in theory, support efficient cooperation in repeated games, but does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence about the magnitude or direction of punishment's effect on efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of repeated interaction and enforceable punishment norms are key moderators, but does not provide parameter-level guidance for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in specific game designs.

- source: Suzuki, S., & Akiyama, E. (2007). Evolution of indirect reciprocity in groups of various sizes and comparison with direct reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in n-person public goods games with indirect reciprocity, both cooperative (discriminating) and defective strategies can be evolutionarily stable, but the threshold for cooperation increases sharply with group size. As group size increases, it becomes much harder for cooperation to evolve and persist, leading to lower expected group efficiency/payoff. The reputation mechanism (standing vs. image scoring) is a strong moderator: standing allows for cooperation to be stable under a wider range of parameters. The presence of implementation errors (failures to cooperate) further undermines the stability of cooperation. When unconditional cooperators are present, cooperation is only stable for group sizes greater than two and under certain parameter ranges. The results are derived analytically from payoff and replicator dynamics equations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that increasing group size in public goods games with indirect reciprocity (and no explicit punishment mechanism) makes it much harder for cooperation and high efficiency to evolve. Thus, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in large groups, the baseline efficiency is likely to be low, and the marginal effect of punishment may depend on whether it can overcome the group size barrier. The paper also highlights the importance of the reputation mechanism: if the punishment or sanctioning system is more like 'standing' (justified punishment does not harm reputation), cooperation is more likely to be stable. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency as a function of group size, error rates, and reputation mechanisms, but do not directly model explicit costly punishment as a game option.

- source: Hauert, C., De Monte, S., Hofbauer, J., & Sigmund, K. (2002). Volunteering as Red Queen mechanism for cooperation in public goods games. *Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games, allowing voluntary participation (the option to become a 'loner' and receive a fixed outside payoff) leads to persistent coexistence and oscillations among cooperators, defectors, and loners. This mechanism can sustain cooperation even in large groups and without repeated interactions, memory, or identification of defectors. The average population payoff can, depending on parameters, be higher or lower than the loner's payoff. The paper contrasts this with punishment and reward mechanisms, which require identification of defectors. The findings are robust across well-mixed and spatially structured populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it focuses on the alternative mechanism of voluntary participation. However, it provides important context: it notes that punishment and reward can establish cooperation if defectors can be identified, but that voluntary participation can also sustain cooperation without identification or repeated interaction. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of a voluntary participation (loner) option is a strong moderator of efficiency and cooperation, and that the absence of punishment does not necessarily doom cooperation if such an option exists. The paper does not provide parameterized predictions about punishment effects, so its direct value for predicting efficiency with/without punishment is limited.

- source: Gao, J., Li, Z., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2010). Diversity of contribution promotes cooperation in public goods games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that allowing cooperators to preferentially contribute more to groups with higher cooperation levels (high-quality groups) significantly promotes the evolution of cooperation in public goods games, both on regular lattices and scale-free networks. The mechanism works by enabling cooperators to cluster and avoid exploitation by defectors, and by increasing the wealth/payoff of cooperators for a given cooperation level. The effect is stronger as the preference parameter (alpha) increases. The paper interprets the reduction of contributions to low-quality groups as a form of indirect, costless group-level punishment, but does not model or analyze explicit costly punishment. Group efficiency or total group payoff is not directly reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for understanding how endogenous contribution patterns (where cooperators can direct more resources to cooperative groups) affect cooperation and the distribution of payoffs in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of explicit, costly punishment on efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that indirect, costless group-level punishment (via reduced contributions to low-quality groups) can promote cooperation and increase the payoffs of cooperators, but the implications for overall group efficiency are not quantified. For prediction tasks focused on explicit punishment interventions, this paper provides only adjacent evidence, mainly relevant for understanding alternative mechanisms to punishment.

- source: Arenas, A., Camacho, J., Cuesta, J. A., & Requejo, R. J. (2011). The joker effect: Cooperation driven by destructive agents. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing destructive agents ('jokers') into a public goods game leads to robust cycles in which cooperation periodically surges. The mechanism is that jokers harm defectors more than cooperators, creating a dynamic where cooperators can invade jokers, jokers invade defectors, and defectors invade cooperators. The cycles are robust to mutation and population size. The model provides explicit payoff expressions for each strategy, but the main focus is on the evolutionary dynamics of strategy frequencies, not on group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are relevant for understanding how destructive threats can promote cooperation, but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in standard PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not study punishment or sanctions per se. Instead, it analyzes the effect of introducing a destructive agent type that harms all non-jokers. The model is a theoretical extension of the PGG and provides insight into how external threats or destructive forces can promote cooperation through population dynamics. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence about efficiency changes due to punishment, nor does it manipulate or analyze standard punishment mechanisms. Its main value is in highlighting that destructive forces can, under certain conditions, increase cooperation, but this is not equivalent to the effect of punishment in PGGs.

- source: Semmann, D., Krambeck, H. J., & Milinski, M. (2005). Reputation is valuable within and outside one's own social group. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study demonstrates that reputation built through cooperation in a public goods game increases the probability of being helped in subsequent indirect reciprocity rounds, and this effect is equally strong whether the helper is from the same or a different PGG group. Cooperation in the PGG is maintained at a high level while IR rounds are expected, but drops sharply when only PGG rounds remain. The experiment does not include punishment or sanctioning options, and does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. It does, however, show that reputation-based reward mechanisms (via indirect reciprocity) can sustain high cooperation rates in PGGs, but this is not equivalent to the effect of punishment. The findings are relevant for understanding how reputation and reward can substitute for or complement punishment, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The game design dimensions are well described, but the absence of punishment and lack of efficiency outcomes limit the paper's direct utility for the prediction task.

- source: Gächter, S., & Fehr, E. (1999). Collective action as a social exchange. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that social approval opportunities alone (i.e., the ability to express approval/disapproval after the game) do not significantly increase cooperation in a standard public goods game among strangers. However, when minimal group familiarity is introduced (via a brief pre-game interaction), the combination of social approval and familiarity leads to a large and significant increase in contributions. The effect is not observed for social approval or group familiarity alone. The main outcome is the contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that social approval can be a powerful motivator for cooperation, but only when social distance is reduced. The study does not test or report on material punishment or pecuniary sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of material punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding the role of non-pecuniary social incentives (approval/disapproval) and their interaction with group familiarity, but not for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that social mechanisms can increase cooperation, but only under certain social conditions. For the downstream prediction task (punishment-enabled efficiency), this paper is only indirectly informative, as it does not manipulate or report on punishment, nor does it report efficiency as a primary outcome.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2010). Reward and cooperation in the spatial public goods game. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing costly reward in a spatial public goods game can promote cooperation, particularly when the synergy factor (MPCR) is low. However, the effect is non-monotonic: moderate rewards can be more effective than high rewards due to the emergence of cyclic dominance among defectors, cooperators, and rewarding cooperators. At high synergy factors, network reciprocity alone can sustain cooperation, and reward mainly determines which cooperative strategy prevails. The paper notes that, compared to punishment, reward is less efficient at promoting cooperation, especially because rewarding cooperators always pay a cost, making them vulnerable to second-order free-riders. The main reported outcomes are the stationary frequencies of each strategy and the phase diagrams of their coexistence, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of reward (not punishment) on cooperation in spatial public goods games. It does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, it suggests that costly reward can promote cooperation under certain conditions, but is generally less effective than punishment. The findings are most relevant for understanding the role of reward mechanisms and the importance of spatial structure, synergy factor (MPCR), and cost-benefit ratios in shaping cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only adjacent: it does not provide direct evidence on punishment or efficiency, but may inform expectations about the relative effectiveness of reward versus punishment and the importance of game parameters.

- source: PARKS, C. D., & HULBERT, L. G. (1995). HIGH AND LOW TRUSTERS RESPONSES TO FEAR IN A PAYOFF MATRIX. *Journal of Conflict Resolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in both public goods and resource dilemma games, the presence of fear (risk of zero payoff for cooperation) interacts with individual trust to affect cooperation rates. High trusters cooperate more than low trusters when fear is present, but this difference disappears when fear is absent. Removing fear increases cooperation among low trusters in resource dilemmas, but decreases cooperation among high trusters in public goods games. The paper does not experimentally manipulate or measure punishment, but discusses its theoretical relevance. No direct efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported; all results are in terms of cooperation frequency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is useful for understanding how trust and fear interact to affect cooperation rates, but does not manipulate or measure punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The discussion suggests that punishment may be more relevant in no-fear situations, but this is not tested. Thus, the paper offers only indirect, contextual support for prediction tasks involving punishment and efficiency.

- source: Gächter, S., Herrmann, B., & Thöni, C. (2004). Trust, voluntary cooperation, and socio-economic background: survey and experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that, in a one-shot public goods game (n=3, MPCR=0.5), both students and non-students contribute substantial amounts, with non-students contributing more on average. However, after controlling for socio-economic background, these differences disappear. Trust attitudes—specifically, beliefs that others are fair, helpful, and that strangers can be trusted—are significantly positively correlated with higher contributions. The widely used GSS trust question is not significantly correlated with contribution. Socio-economic background affects trust attitudes but not contribution behavior. No punishment or sanctioning is present in the analyzed experiment, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as the analyzed experiment does not include a punishment condition and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. It does, however, provide informative context about baseline contribution behavior in a standard one-shot public goods game (n=3, MPCR=0.5) with a diverse subject pool. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper can inform the expected baseline (control) contribution rates in the absence of punishment, but not the treatment effect of punishment. The paper also notes (in passing) that punishment can reduce the trust problem in repeated games, but this is only discussed, not tested.

- source: Wu, T., Fu, F., & Wang, L. (2009). Partner selections in public goods games with constant group size. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that, in public goods games with constant group size on networks, increasing group size generally promotes higher cooperation rates, especially when individuals can select partners based on reputation. Reputation-based partner selection (interpreted as costless exclusion of defectors) is more effective at sustaining cooperation than degree-based selection. The model does not include explicit costly punishment or reward mechanisms. The main reported outcome is the stationary fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. Analytical calculations show that larger group size increases the payoff advantage of cooperators over defectors, but these are not translated into reported efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling explicit punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding how group size and partner selection (especially reputation-based exclusion) affect cooperation rates, but not efficiency or payoff. The reputation-based exclusion mechanism is a form of costless, indirect punishment, which differs from the costly punishment typically studied in experimental PGGs. Therefore, the findings should not be directly transferred to predictions about the efficiency effects of enabling costly punishment. The paper may be useful for understanding the role of group size and partner selection in cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Wang, W. X., Lai, Y. C., & Armbruster, D. (2011). Cascading failures and the emergence of cooperation in evolutionary-game based models of social and economical networks. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in evolutionary games on networks (including PGG), introducing a survival threshold (death/bankruptcy) leads to catastrophic cascades when defection is present. After such cascades, only cooperators survive, regardless of network topology or game type. Defectors may gain higher payoffs temporarily but are ultimately eliminated. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcomes are the fraction of dead agents, the survival of cooperators, and the conditions for extinction or survival, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. The model's 'sanction' is the endogenous removal of agents with low payoffs, not a punishment technology as in standard PGG experiments. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency. It may provide some adjacent theoretical context about the consequences of defection and the importance of cooperation for system survival, but not about the efficiency impact of punishment interventions.

- source: Henrich, J., Boyd, R., Bowles, S., Camerer, C., Fehr, E., Gintis, H., McElreath, R., Alvard, M., Barr, A., Ensminger, J., Henrich, N. S., Hill, K., Gil-White, F., Gurven, M., Marlowe, F. W., Patton, J. Q., & Tracer, D. (2005). Economic man in cross-cultural perspective: Behavioral experiments in 15 small-scale societies. *Behavioral and Brain Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the canonical model of self-interest fails to predict behavior in all societies studied. There is substantial cross-cultural variation in offers and contributions in ultimatum, dictator, and public goods games. Group-level variables such as market integration and payoffs to cooperation are strong predictors of prosocial behavior in these games. However, the study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms in the public goods games, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The main findings are about the determinants of prosocial behavior, not about the effects of punishment or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments. It does provide informative context about baseline contribution rates and the influence of group-level variables on prosocial behavior in public goods games across diverse societies. However, for the downstream prediction task of estimating efficiency with and without punishment, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not manipulate or report on punishment or efficiency outcomes.

- source: Kümmerli, R., Burton-Chellew, M. N., Ross-Gillespie, A., & West, S. A. (2010). Resistance to extreme strategies, rather than prosocial preferences, can explain human cooperation in public goods games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: Across four lab experiments, when the public goods game is modified so that full cooperation is individually optimal (e.g., by setting the multiplier above the group size or by adding group rewards), mean contributions and group efficiency increase significantly compared to the standard PGG. However, contributions remain well below 100% and full efficiency is not achieved. The proportion of subjects contributing fully in the last round of the modified games ranges from 35% to 72%, and the mean group contribution is always significantly less than the maximum possible. The results challenge the interpretation that high cooperation in PGGs is due to prosocial preferences, and instead point to psychological resistance to extreme strategies (full defection or full cooperation) as a key driver. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) or otherwise aligning individual and group incentives increases group efficiency in public goods games. However, even when efficiency-maximizing behavior is straightforward, groups do not reach full efficiency, suggesting that psychological factors limit the effect. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency as a function of mpcr and group reward structures, but do not inform the effect of punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning is present in any treatment. Thus, the paper is highly informative for baseline/control efficiency prediction, but does not support prediction of the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Chaudhuri, A., Graziano, S., & Maitra, P. (2006). Social learning and norms in a public goods experiment with inter-generational advice. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard linear public goods game (5 players, 10 rounds, MPCR=0.4, no punishment or reward), making advice from previous generations common knowledge (read aloud and known to all) leads to significantly higher group contributions and efficiency (average earnings 82% of optimum) than private or public advice or no advice. In the absence of punishment, common knowledge of exhortative advice can create and sustain high cooperation and efficiency. Other advice treatments (private, public) do not significantly improve efficiency over the no-advice control. The study provides strong evidence that social learning and norm formation, via common knowledge of advice, can substitute for punishment in achieving high efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a high-quality control case for public goods games without punishment: with 5 players, 10 rounds, MPCR=0.4, and no punishment or reward, average group efficiency is about 50% of the social optimum in the no-advice control. The study does not include any punishment or sanction treatment, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment. However, it shows that high efficiency (82%) can be achieved through common knowledge of exhortative advice, suggesting that social learning and norm formation can be an alternative to punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper provides a strong baseline for control efficiency in standard PGGs without punishment, and highlights the importance of communication and social learning mechanisms.

- source: Puurtinen, M., & Mappes, T. (2009). Between-group competition and human cooperation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing between-group competition to a standard public goods game (with no punishment or communication) leads to a substantial increase in both cooperation (contributions) and group efficiency (mean earnings). In the control (PG) condition, mean earnings over 10 rounds were 251 MUs, while in the group competition (GC) condition, mean earnings were 333 MUs. The effect is robust to order and persists across rounds. The study also finds that group competition increases moral emotions (anger at free riders, guilt at earning more than others) and shifts perception of group members from competitors to collaborators. However, the experiment does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in either condition. Its findings are relevant for understanding the effect of between-group competition, not punishment. Therefore, it should not be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, given a control game without punishment. It does, however, provide a clear baseline for a standard public goods game (n=4, 10 rounds, no punishment, no communication, MPCR=0.5), which may be useful for contextualizing other studies.

- source: Archetti, M., & Scheuring, I. (2011). COEXISTENCE OF COOPERATION AND DEFECTION IN PUBLIC GOODS GAMES. *Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of the public goods function (linear vs. nonlinear/threshold) fundamentally changes the equilibrium outcome. In nonlinear/threshold (Volunteer's Dilemma-like) games, cooperation and defection can coexist at a stable mixed equilibrium, and the public good is produced with positive probability, even in one-shot, well-mixed populations without relatedness or repeated play. In linear (NPD) games, only defection is stable in the absence of assortment. The paper provides analytical and numerical results for the equilibrium frequency of cooperation and the probability of public good provision as a function of group size, cost/benefit ratio, and the threshold parameter. No punishment, reward, or external enforcement mechanisms are modeled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in public goods games as a function of the public good production function (linear vs. nonlinear/threshold), group size, and cost/benefit ratio, but it does not address the effects of punishment or sanctions. It provides direct theoretical predictions for efficiency and group payoff in the absence of punishment, but cannot inform the effect of enabling punishment. For downstream prediction tasks focused on the effect of punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it is valuable for understanding baseline efficiency in control conditions without punishment, especially in games with nonlinear or threshold public goods functions.

- source: Killingback, T., Bieri, J., & Flatt, T. (2006). Evolution in group-structured populations can resolve the tragedy of the commons. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game with continuous investment, cooperation and high group efficiency do not evolve in well-mixed populations, but can evolve and be stably maintained in group-structured populations with limited dispersal. The mechanism relies on group structure and population dynamics, not on punishment, reputation, or optional participation. The model demonstrates that for a range of dispersal rates, high levels of cooperative investment (and thus high group payoffs) can be sustained. However, the model does not include any form of punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as punishment is explicitly excluded from the model. It is useful for understanding baseline efficiency in the absence of punishment and for considering the effects of group structure and dispersal, but it does not inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is relevant only as a control or baseline for environments without punishment.

- source: Boza, G., & Számadó, S. (2010). Beneficial laggards: multilevel selection, cooperative polymorphism and division of labour in Threshold Public Good Games. *Bmc Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in threshold public good games, stable polymorphism (coexistence of cooperators and defectors) can be maintained by multilevel selection, even in the absence of punishment or spatial structure. Group efficiency (measured as group payoff or average fitness) is maximized when the number of cooperators matches the threshold required for success, not when all members cooperate. Increasing group size or cost of cooperation reduces the range of parameters where cooperation is stable. Division of labour can evolve, with individuals specializing in different collective actions. Spatial structure further promotes cooperation. The paper does not address punishment or its effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effects of punishment or sanctions in public good games. It is highly relevant for understanding baseline efficiency and the evolution of cooperation in threshold PGGs without punishment, and for predicting the efficiency of control games (punishment disabled) as a function of group size, cost, and threshold. However, it does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency when punishment is enabled, nor does it provide evidence on any moderators of punishment effects. It is useful for modeling baseline efficiency in threshold PGGs, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment.

- source: Hauert, C., Holmes, M., & Doebeli, M. (2006). Evolutionary games and population dynamics: maintenance of cooperation in public goods games. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games, if population density is allowed to vary as a function of average payoff, cooperation can be maintained through eco-evolutionary feedback. When defection leads to low payoffs, population density drops, which in turn reduces group size and can make cooperation advantageous. This can result in stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors at intermediate frequencies, with population density and average payoff stabilizing at levels where neither strategy dominates. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as punishment is not modeled. However, it is highly informative about how ecological feedback (variable group size as a function of payoff) can stabilize cooperation and improve group payoffs in the absence of punishment. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it provides a theoretical baseline for understanding efficiency in public goods games without punishment or reward.

- source: Rong, Z. H., & Wu, Z. X. (2009). Effect of the degree correlation in public goods game on scale-free networks. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in scale-free networks, assortative degree mixing (where hubs are connected to other hubs) inhibits the emergence and sustainment of cooperation in the PGG, as the core-periphery structure weakens the ability of cooperative hubs to influence peripheral nodes. In disassortative networks (where hubs connect to low-degree nodes), cooperation can persist even at low multiplication factors, as hubs maintain their initial strategies and can influence their leaf neighbors. The main outcome is the frequency of cooperation, with some analysis of average payoff per degree. No punishment or sanctioning is present in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. It is relevant for understanding how network structure (degree correlation) affects cooperation and individual payoffs in PGGs without punishment. The findings may be useful for contextualizing baseline efficiency or cooperation rates in networked PGGs, but do not provide evidence about the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Jiang, L. L., Perc, M., Wang, W. X., Lai, Y. C., & Wang, B. H. (2011). Impact of link deletions on public cooperation in scale-free networks. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in multi-group PGGs on scale-free networks, deleting links connected to hubs (network attack) predominantly reduces the fraction of cooperators, especially at low multiplication factors. There is a narrow region where the effect is strictly negative, but outside this region, an intermediate number of link deletions can minimize cooperation. Wealth distributions become less heterogeneous as links are deleted, which is associated with lower cooperation. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, but does analyze total payoffs (wealth) at the individual level.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs or their variants. It is relevant for understanding how network structure and heterogeneity affect cooperation and individual payoffs, but not for predicting the effect of punishment. The absence of punishment and the focus on cooperation rates rather than efficiency or group payoff limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task. However, the analysis of wealth distributions may offer indirect insights into how structural features (like network heterogeneity) can moderate payoff outcomes in PGGs.

- source: Santos, F. C., Santos, M. D., & Pacheco, J. M. (2008). Social diversity promotes the emergence of cooperation in public goods games. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing social diversity via heterogeneous (scale-free) network structures and varying individual participation in multiple public goods games can significantly enhance the emergence and stability of cooperation. Two main models are compared: (1) fixed cost per game (each cooperator pays a cost in every game they play), and (2) fixed cost per individual (each cooperator pays a total cost, divided among all games they play). The latter model, combined with network heterogeneity, leads to a dramatic increase in the prevalence of cooperation, even at lower enhancement factors. The paper also shows that in all-cooperator populations, wealth (fitness) distributions are more unequal on scale-free graphs, but the overall number of 'poor' is reduced under the fixed cost per individual model. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or reputation mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff relative to the cooperative optimum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it explicitly excludes punishment and focuses on the effects of social diversity and network structure. It is useful for understanding how heterogeneity in group structure and contribution rules can promote cooperation, but does not inform the impact of punishment or sanctions. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be relevant for understanding baseline cooperation rates in the absence of punishment, especially in heterogeneous network settings.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., Ross-Gillespie, A., & West, S. A. (2010). Cooperation in humans: competition between groups and proximate emotions. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that introducing group competition into a standard public goods game increases individual contributions, but does not increase individual earnings. In the standard PGG (no group competition), higher contributions are associated with lower individual income, while in the group competition treatment, there is no significant relationship between contribution and income. The paper focuses on behavioral outcomes (contribution, emotions, perceptions of group mates) rather than efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is not directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. The findings may be useful for understanding the effect of intergroup competition on cooperation, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment.

- source: Suri, S., & Watts, D. J. (2011). Cooperation and Contagion in Web-Based, Networked Public Goods Experiments. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that, in a networked public goods game with 24 players and no punishment or reward options, the structure of the network (from highly clustered cliques to random regular graphs) does not significantly affect average contributions. Players do exhibit conditional cooperation (responding to neighbors' contributions), but this does not translate into higher overall cooperation in more clustered networks, nor does it produce multi-step contagion. The absence of punishment or reward means the findings are limited to baseline public goods dynamics. No direct measures of efficiency or group payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence about baseline (control) public goods game behavior in networked settings without punishment or reward. It is directly relevant for predicting control efficiency in such games, but does not inform the effect of enabling punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present or discussed in the experimental design. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, network structure alone does not improve cooperation or efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is useful for estimating control efficiency in networked PGGs, but provides no evidence on the effect of punishment or its moderators.

- source: Fischbacher, U., & Gächter, S. (2010). Social Preferences, Beliefs, and the Dynamics of Free Riding in Public Goods Experiments. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a standard linear public goods game with random matching and no punishment or reward, contributions decline over time because most participants are imperfect conditional cooperators. Belief updating is well described by a weighted average of past beliefs and observed contributions, but the main driver of declining cooperation is the nature of conditional cooperation preferences. The paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff, and does not manipulate or include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on the baseline dynamics of cooperation in repeated public goods games without punishment or reward. It is directly informative for predicting control group efficiency (i.e., with punishment disabled) in standard PGG designs. However, it does not provide any evidence on the effects of punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Thus, it is not directly useful for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, but it is highly relevant for understanding baseline (control) dynamics and the role of social preferences and beliefs.

- source: Lamba, S., & Mace, R. (2011). Demography and ecology drive variation in cooperation across human populations. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
