  findings: The study finds significant variation in cooperative behavior (measured by PGG contributions and salt-sharing) across villages of the same cultural group. Demographic factors such as age, social network size, village population size, and number of adult sisters in the village are associated with these cooperative behaviors. The results challenge the view that stable cultural norms alone explain cross-population differences in cooperation, highlighting the role of local demography and ecology. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms were present in the games, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes were reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism was included. However, it is informative for understanding baseline variation in cooperation in one-shot PGGs without punishment, and for the influence of demographic and ecological moderators. For the downstream prediction task, this paper can inform estimates of control (no-punishment) efficiency in similar field settings, but provides no evidence on the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Kurzban, R., & Houser, D. (2005). Experiments investigating cooperative types in humans: A complement to evolutionary theory and simulations. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a standard public goods game (no punishment or reward), individuals fall into stable behavioral types: cooperators, reciprocators, and free-riders. The composition of these types within a group strongly predicts the group's contribution level. Groups with more cooperative types contribute more to the public good, and this relationship is robust across in-sample and out-of-sample games. Despite large behavioral differences, individual earnings are statistically indistinguishable across types. The study provides strong evidence for the existence and stability of cooperative types, and for the predictive power of group composition on cooperation, but does not address the effects of punishment or efficiency changes due to institutional design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding baseline behavior in public goods games without punishment or reward. It provides evidence that group outcomes (contributions, and thus efficiency/payoff) are strongly determined by the distribution of behavioral types. However, it does not provide any evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it manipulate or analyze any institutional design features related to punishment, reward, or efficiency interventions. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with and without punishment), this paper is only useful as a control/baseline reference for games without punishment, and for understanding the role of type composition. It does not inform the effect of enabling punishment or any moderators of that effect.

- source: Brekke, K. A., Hauge, K. E., Lind, J. T., & Nyborg, K. (2011). Playing with the good guys. A public good game with endogenous group formation. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when participants can self-select into groups with a prosocial commitment (charity donation), those groups attract higher contributors and sustain higher contribution rates over time compared to groups with a private bonus. The difference in contributions between group types increases over time, with blue groups showing the typical decline in contributions and red groups maintaining high levels. However, the paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The findings are relevant for understanding self-selection and conditional cooperation in public good games, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public good games. It is not directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in the context of punishment. However, it does provide evidence that self-selection into prosocial groups can sustain higher contributions, which may be relevant for understanding the role of group composition and endogenous group formation. For the downstream prediction task focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant.

- source: Sonnemans, J., van Dijk, F., & van Winden, F. (2006). On the dynamics of social ties structures in groups. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in repeated public good games, social ties between group members develop and are influenced by the relative contributions of each member. Groups with more positive mutual ties tend to have higher average contributions, while groups with more negative or neutral ties have lower contributions. Social value orientation and affective responses play a significant role in the formation of these ties. The study provides evidence that group dynamics and interpersonal attitudes can lead to substantial variation in cooperation, even when incentives and endowments are held constant. However, the paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or their effects on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the design. It does, however, highlight the importance of social ties and group dynamics in driving contribution behavior, which may be relevant for understanding baseline cooperation levels in the absence of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is mainly useful for understanding control (no-punishment) environments and the role of social preferences, but it does not inform the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Smith, E. A. (2010). Communication and collective action: language and the evolution of human cooperation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that participants' contributions in a one-shot, sequential PGG are strongly influenced by the observed contributions of their group members, consistent with strategic reciprocation. Individual differences in motivational traits matter: higher BAS Reward Responsiveness predicts lower contributions when others have contributed highly (i.e., more self-regarding, strategic behavior), while higher BIS-anxiety predicts a lower likelihood of making zero contributions (i.e., less free riding). The experiment does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the experimental design. It is useful for understanding heterogeneity in contribution behavior and the role of personality traits in cooperation, but does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The game design is a standard sequential PGG with no punishment or reward options, so it is only relevant as a control or baseline for contribution behavior in the absence of sanctions.

- source: Barclay, P. (2006). Reputational benefits for altruistic punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that participants contribute more to a public good when they expect their reputation to matter in a subsequent trust game, and that contributions are maintained best when there is competition to be the most altruistic. Trust is preferentially given to those who contributed more in the PGG. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design. The outcomes are primarily behavioral (contributions, trust), not efficiency or payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is useful for understanding how reputation and competitive altruism can increase contributions, but does not inform predictions about punishment-enabled efficiency. The design and findings are relevant for reputation and trust mechanisms, not for punishment or sanctioning treatments.

- source: Wang, B. K., Chen, X. J., & Wang, L. (2012). Probabilistic interconnection between interdependent networks promotes cooperation in the public goods game. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial public goods game played on two interdependent networks, the probability of interconnection between corresponding nodes in the two layers has a non-monotonic effect on cooperation: an intermediate interconnection probability maximizes the fraction of cooperators in the system. The coupling effect between layers can promote cooperation in both, even if one starts with only defectors. The study does not include or analyze punishment, reward, or efficiency/payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is useful for understanding how network structure and interdependence affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based or punishment-related predictions. The absence of punishment, reward, and efficiency outcomes means it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Roca, C. P., & Helbing, D. (2011). Emergence of social cohesion in a model society of greedy, mobile individuals. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial public goods game with voluntary contributions and no punishment or reward, cooperation and social cohesion can emerge if individuals have moderate levels of greediness (aspiration). Too little greediness leads to stagnation in noncooperation, while too much greediness destabilizes cooperation and social ties. The model demonstrates that these beneficial levels of greediness can be evolutionarily selected. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, agglomeration, instability), not efficiency or group payoff. The model does not include or analyze punishment, and thus provides no direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It models a PGG environment without punishment or reward, and focuses on the role of individual learning and mobility. While it is useful for understanding alternative mechanisms for cooperation, it does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects. The game design dimensions are partially covered (e.g., player count, group size, mpcr), but the absence of punishment means this paper cannot be used to predict efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Cubitt, R. P., Drouvelis, M., Gächter, S., & Kabalin, R. (2011). Moral judgments in social dilemmas: How bad is free riding?. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that free riding is generally judged as morally blameworthy, except when both players free ride in sequence, in which case the second free rider is sometimes judged positively. Moral condemnation of free riding increases with the co-player's contribution, especially in sequential settings. Framing matters: failing to give is condemned more than withdrawing support, even when monetary outcomes are identical. The findings suggest that moral judgments in social dilemmas are sensitive to context, framing, and the actions of others, but the study does not address actual behavior, efficiency, or the effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report on efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its findings on moral judgment may be of indirect interest for understanding motivations in social dilemmas, but it does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Szolnoki, A., Perc, M., & Szabó, G. (2009). Topology-independent impact of noise on cooperation in spatial public goods games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in spatial public goods games, increasing the group size (number of players per game) can qualitatively change the relationship between noise (uncertainty in strategy adoption) and the survival of cooperation. On regular graphs with low clustering (no overlapping triangles), pairwise interactions (G=2) show an optimal intermediate noise level for cooperation, but larger groups (G>2) remove this effect and make the system behave more like high-clustering networks. The main outcome is the density of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it provides informative context about how group size and network topology affect cooperation rates in the absence of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in spatial PGGs without sanctions.

- source: Casari, M., & Plott, C. R. (2003). Decentralized management of common property resources: experiments with a centuries-old institution. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a common-pool resource environment with 8 players and continuous contribution/appropriation choices, the addition of a decentralized punishment mechanism (where agents can pay to inspect and fine others for overuse, with fines paid to the inspector) leads to large increases in group efficiency. Without sanctions, efficiency is low (28.4%), below the Nash equilibrium prediction. With weak sanctions, efficiency rises to 57.2% gross (48.3% net after inspection costs). With strong sanctions, efficiency reaches 94% gross (76.9% net). The classical model cannot explain the effect of weak sanctions, but a model with heterogeneous, linear other-regarding preferences (spiteful and altruistic agents) fits the data well. The mechanism channels spiteful behavior into socially useful monitoring. The effect is robust across subject experience and is not due to repeated game effects or communication.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling decentralized punishment in a common-pool resource game (close variant of a public goods game) can substantially increase efficiency, even when the punishment is relatively weak and should not be effective under standard selfish Nash equilibrium predictions. The effect is robust to subject experience and is not driven by communication or repeated game effects. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are: player_count=8, num_rounds~27-33, no chat, continuous contribution, no default, non-linear MPCR (parabolic then exponential decay), punishment cost and tech specified, no reward, number of rounds not known in advance, summary info provided, and punishment identity not revealed. The control (no-sanction) efficiency is low (28.4%), and the treatment (punishment enabled) efficiency is much higher (48-76% net, 57-94% gross depending on punishment strength). This paper should be weighted heavily in predicting efficiency gains from punishment in similar decentralized, continuous-action, no-communication environments.

- source: Vollan, B. (2008). Socio-ecological explanations for crowding-out effects from economic field experiments in southern Africa. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment involved groups of 5 players managing a common-pool resource over 20 rounds, with a baseline (open access) phase followed by an institution phase (external penalty, external reward, or communication) chosen by group vote. Efficiency (group payoff as a percentage of the social optimum) increased from 79.6% in the baseline to 88.6% on average after introducing institutions. However, the effect of punishment (penalty) was highly contingent: when the penalty rule was imposed with low group support (low self-determination), or in high-trust Namibian communities, efficiency actually decreased (crowding-out effect), with final extraction levels as high or higher than the baseline. In contrast, in low-trust South African communities, punishment increased efficiency. Reward (external enabling intervention) did not produce crowding-out in any context and generally improved or maintained efficiency. Communication alone (no external intervention) produced stable, moderate efficiency. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is mixed and moderated by self-determination and social trust.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments is not universally positive. Instead, punishment can reduce efficiency (crowding-out) when imposed without group support or in high-trust, norm-driven communities. In low-trust settings, punishment can increase efficiency. Reward mechanisms do not crowd out cooperation and are generally safe for efficiency. For prediction, the presence of punishment should not be assumed to always increase efficiency; the effect depends on the degree of self-determination (e.g., whether punishment is voted in or imposed) and the baseline level of trust in the group. Control efficiency (baseline) is a strong predictor, but the effect of enabling punishment depends on these moderators. Game design dimensions such as group voting, trust, and the nature of the intervention (controlling vs. enabling) are critical for accurate prediction.

- source: OSTROM, E., WALKER, J., & GARDNER, R. (1992). COVENANTS WITH AND WITHOUT A SWORD - SELF-GOVERNANCE IS POSSIBLE. *American Political Science Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a CPR game with 8 players and a quadratic production function, baseline efficiency (net yield) is low, especially in high-endowment settings. Allowing communication (covenants) alone increases efficiency substantially, with repeated communication leading to near-optimal outcomes. Introducing a sanctioning mechanism (the sword) alone increases net yield before costs, but after accounting for the costs of punishment, efficiency gains are minimal or negative. When both communication and sanctioning are allowed, especially when the group can choose the sanctioning mechanism, efficiency is maximized and defection rates are minimized. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus highly context-dependent: punishment alone is not efficient, but when combined with communication and endogenous institution-building, it is highly effective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments depends critically on the presence of communication and the way the punishment institution is implemented. If the game design allows for communication and endogenous choice of punishment, enabling punishment can dramatically increase efficiency. If punishment is imposed without communication, efficiency gains are small or negative after accounting for punishment costs. The control efficiency (without punishment) is a strong predictor, but the treatment effect of punishment is positive only when paired with communication and institutional choice. Game design dimensions such as player count (8), number of rounds (20+), absence of chat in baseline, continuous contribution, and the specific punishment cost/tech parameters are all relevant for prediction.

- source: Janssen, M. A., Holahan, R., Lee, A., & Ostrom, E. (2010). Lab Experiments for the Study of Social-Ecological Systems. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a dynamic, spatially explicit CPR lab experiment with five players per group, costly punishment is used by participants but does not increase group payoffs or efficiency unless combined with communication. When punishment is available without communication, net group earnings decrease due to the cost of punishment, and resource depletion is not prevented. Communication alone leads to higher group payoffs and more sustainable resource use. When both communication and punishment are available, the positive effects of communication are not enhanced, and the use of punishment can undermine cooperative agreements once communication is removed. The study highlights that in this more complex, dynamic environment, punishment without communication is not an effective tool for increasing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in dynamic, spatially explicit CPR games with real-time harvesting and five players, enabling costly punishment without communication is likely to reduce efficiency relative to the control (no punishment, no communication) due to the direct costs of punishment. Communication is a much more effective intervention for increasing efficiency. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled (but no communication) should be lower than the control, and that the presence or absence of communication is a critical moderator. The punishment mechanism here (cost of 1 token to remove 2 tokens from another) is not sufficient to offset the cost in terms of group payoff. These results are most applicable to dynamic, spatially explicit CPR games rather than standard static PGGs, but the payoff-based outcome is directly relevant.

- source: Ostrom, E. (2006). The value-added of laboratory experiments for the study of institutions and common-pool resources. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper reviews a series of laboratory CPR experiments, including baseline (no punishment), communication, exogenous and endogenous sanctioning, and exogenous rule imposition. In baseline (no punishment) games, groups achieve low efficiency (e.g., 37% or less of optimal). When costly punishment is enabled, groups increase their efficiency (e.g., up to 39% of optimal in one set, and up to 94% in another with strong sanctions), but the net gain is reduced by the cost of punishment. Communication and endogenous rule-making (where groups set their own rules and sanctions) lead to the highest efficiency (up to 93% of optimal). Exogenous rules with imperfect monitoring can crowd out cooperation and reduce efficiency. The effect of punishment is sensitive to its cost and effectiveness, and is most positive when combined with communication or endogenous agreement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in CPR-like games increases efficiency relative to control (no punishment), but the magnitude depends on the cost and effectiveness of punishment. If punishment is costly and not highly effective, net efficiency gains may be modest or even offset by sanctioning costs. If punishment is strong (high impact per cost) or is combined with communication or endogenous rule-making, efficiency can approach optimal levels. For prediction, the presence and parameters of punishment (cost, tech), communication, and endogenous rule-making are key moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) is typically low, and the introduction of punishment generally increases efficiency, especially when punishment is effective and/or combined with communication.

- source: Sigmund, K., Hauert, C., & Nowak, M. A. (2001). Reward and punishment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in two-player public goods minigames with punishment or reward, evolutionary dynamics without reputation lead to asocial equilibria with low efficiency, regardless of the presence of punishment or reward options. When a small chance of reputation is introduced (players can sometimes observe others' punishment/reward behavior), the system can support a stable, high-efficiency equilibrium with cooperation and punishment. This effect is stronger and more robust for punishment than for reward. The model predicts that punishment with reputation is much more effective at promoting group efficiency than reward with reputation, and that without reputation, neither mechanism is effective in the long run.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment alone does not increase efficiency in public goods games unless players have some information about others' punishment behavior (i.e., reputation). In the absence of reputation, enabling punishment does not improve efficiency; the system converges to defection and non-punishment. With even a small probability of reputation, enabling punishment can create a bistable system where high-efficiency outcomes are possible and can be risk-dominant. The effect is parameter-dependent (e.g., punishment cost, effectiveness, benefit/cost of cooperation), but the qualitative result is robust. For prediction, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the presence of reputation or information about others' actions. If the game design does not allow for reputation, enabling punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency. If reputation is present, punishment can have a strong positive effect on efficiency, more so than reward.

- source: Cardenas, J. C. (2004). Norms from outside and from inside: an experimental analysis on the governance of local ecosystems. *Forest Policy and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that introducing external regulation (punishment for over-extraction, with imperfect monitoring) or allowing face-to-face communication both lead to substantial increases in efficiency (lower extraction, higher group earnings) compared to a baseline with no intervention. The difference between high and low penalty treatments was much smaller than predicted by standard theory, with both leading to similar improvements in efficiency. A large fraction of participants complied with the social optimum extraction even when the expected cost of punishment was low, indicating that norm compliance and the salience of the rule were important. Communication (self-governance) was as effective as external punishment in increasing efficiency. The results suggest that both material and non-material incentives (norms, communication) can be effective in improving group outcomes in commons dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that both punishment (external regulation with fines and monitoring) and communication (self-governance) can substantially increase efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, even when the expected cost of punishment is low. The effect of increasing the penalty size (punishment_tech) was much smaller than predicted, suggesting diminishing returns to harsher punishment. The game design dimensions (player_count=5, num_rounds=20, no chat in baseline, chat in self-governance, continuous extraction, known payoff table, monitoring probability, and fine size) are all well specified, making this paper highly informative for predicting efficiency under different punishment regimes. Control efficiency (no punishment, no communication) is lower than in the treatments, and both punishment and communication increase efficiency to similar levels.

- source: ABREU, D., MILGROM, P., & PEARCE, D. (1991). INFORMATION AND TIMING IN REPEATED PARTNERSHIPS. *Econometrica*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated partnership games with imperfect monitoring, the ability to sustain efficient cooperation through punishment is fundamentally limited by the informativeness of the monitoring technology and the timing of information. When monitoring is imperfect, reducing the interest rate (making players more patient) increases the efficiency of equilibrium outcomes, potentially allowing near-first-best payoffs. However, shortening the period of fixed action or reducing the lag with which information is reported can actually decrease efficiency, sometimes eliminating the possibility of cooperation altogether. Delaying information release can, counterintuitively, increase efficiency by reducing the cost of deterring deviations. The paper provides generalizations to n-player symmetric games and shows that these results are robust to more complex action and signal spaces.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency of repeated public-goods-like games with punishment under imperfect monitoring. It shows that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the informativeness of monitoring, the timing of information, and the patience of players. In particular, enabling punishment does not guarantee high efficiency unless monitoring is sufficiently informative and the timing of information and action periods is chosen carefully. For prediction, the paper suggests that increasing patience (lower interest rate) is reliably associated with higher efficiency, but increasing the frequency of action or information updates can reduce efficiency when monitoring is imperfect. The explicit formulas and comparative statics in the paper can be used to estimate efficiency given the relevant game design dimensions.

- source: Bowles, S., & Gintis, H. (2004). The evolution of strong reciprocity: cooperation in heterogeneous populations. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The model shows that in groups where some members are willing to incur costs to punish norm violators (strong reciprocators), high levels of cooperation and group efficiency are sustained. Without punishment, groups devolve to all-Selfish agents and low efficiency. The presence of punishment leads to a stable mix of Reciprocators, Cooperators, and Selfish agents, with low average shirking rates and high group payoffs. The model is robust to changes in group size, cost of punishment, and other parameters. The findings support the idea that costly punishment is a key mechanism for sustaining cooperation and efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling costly punishment in public-goods-game-like environments increases group efficiency and average payoffs, relative to control conditions without punishment. The model is not an empirical experiment but simulates a wide range of parameter values, showing that the positive effect of punishment on efficiency is robust to variations in group size, cost of punishment, and other design features. For prediction tasks, the presence of a costly punishment mechanism (even with moderate cost and effectiveness) should be expected to increase efficiency, especially in settings with repeated group interaction and no central authority. The model's structure and outcomes are directly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Sethi, R., & Somanathan, E. (2003). Understanding reciprocity. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment and reciprocity can support efficient outcomes in both repeated and sporadic interactions, but only under certain conditions. In repeated games, strategies that support cooperation (and thus efficiency) can be stable if complexity costs or implementation errors are considered, but many inefficient strategies can also be stable. In one-shot or sporadic interactions, punishment can induce cooperation and improve efficiency, but this stability is fragile: populations of punishers can be invaded by non-punishing cooperators or defectors, and the cost of punishment can erode its effectiveness. In public goods and common pool resource games, the presence of reciprocators willing to punish can lead to higher group payoffs, but only if monitoring and punishment costs are not too high and if group composition allows reciprocators to coordinate. The evolution of mixed populations (reciprocators and defectors) is common, and the efficiency gains from punishment depend on the game structure and population dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in public-goods-like games can increase efficiency relative to no-punishment baselines, but only under specific conditions: punishment must be sufficiently effective (high impact per cost), not too costly, and the population must contain enough reciprocators or the ability to assort. The effect of punishment on efficiency is not universally positive; in some models, punishment does not prevent the evolution of inefficient outcomes, and the stability of efficient cooperation is sensitive to the cost and observability of punishment, as well as to the possibility of second-order free-riding. For prediction, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, group size, and the ability to observe or assort by type. Control efficiency (without punishment) is often low, but the introduction of punishment can raise efficiency if these moderating conditions are met.

- source: Nakamaru, M., & Iwasa, Y. (2006). The coevolution of altruism and punishment: Role of the selfish punisher. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment can promote the evolution of cooperation (and thus higher group payoffs) in both well-mixed and lattice-structured populations, but only under certain conditions. In the score-dependent viability model, punishment is more effective at suppressing defectors and promoting cooperation than in the fertility model. The presence of selfish punishers (defectors who punish other defectors) can help the evolution of altruistic punishers by first eliminating pure defectors, but pure altruists (who cooperate but do not punish) can hinder the evolution of punishment by being exploited. The benefit of cooperation, the fine imposed by punishment, and the cost of punishing all interact to determine evolutionary outcomes. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus context-dependent and can be positive or negative depending on parameter values and population structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency (group payoff) in public-goods-like games depends on the interaction of several design dimensions: population structure (well-mixed vs. lattice), the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the benefit of cooperation, and the evolutionary update rule (viability vs. fertility). Punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when the punishment fine is large relative to the cost of punishing, and in spatially structured populations. However, if the cost of punishing is too high or if pure cooperators are common, punishment may not evolve or may even reduce cooperation. The findings suggest that predictions of treatment efficiency from control efficiency should account for these moderators, especially the structure of interactions and the punishment parameters. However, as the paper does not report explicit efficiency values, its support is indirect and based on evolutionary stability of cooperation.

- source: Gardner, A., & West, S. A. (2004). Cooperation and punishment, especially in humans. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment can promote and stabilize cooperation in public-goods-like environments, leading to higher group fitness (efficiency), provided that individuals who punish tend to receive more cooperation. The cost of punishment decreases as cooperation increases, making punishment more sustainable in cooperative populations. Relatedness between individuals is not required for punishment to be favored; instead, the key is a positive association between an individual's punishment and the cooperation she receives. The model predicts that punishment is more easily maintained than initially evolved, and that small group size and effective punishment (where the cost of being punished exceeds the cost of cooperation) facilitate the evolutionary stability of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in public-goods-like games can increase efficiency, especially when punishment is effective (i.e., the penalty for being punished is greater than the cost of cooperation) and when there is a mechanism (such as behavioral adjustment) that links punishment to increased cooperation received. The findings suggest that, all else equal, introducing punishment should increase group efficiency, particularly in smaller groups and when punishment is not too costly to the punisher. However, the results are derived from theoretical models rather than empirical data, so quantitative predictions require caution. The paper also highlights that the effect of punishment is not dependent on relatedness or repeated interactions, but on the structure of behavioral associations.

- source: MCCUSKER, C., & CARNEVALE, P. J. (1995). FRAMING IN RESOURCE DILEMMAS - LOSS AVERSION AND THE MODERATING EFFECTS OF SANCTIONS. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both penalty and reward sanction systems increase cooperation in resource dilemmas, with reward sanctions producing the highest cooperation. The difference in cooperation between 'give' and 'take' frames (i.e., public goods vs. commons) disappears when sanctions are present. The effect of sanctions is robust across two experiments, and the framing of the sanction (give vs. take) affects contributions to the sanction fund but not cooperation. However, the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the direct effect of sanctions on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that endogenous, group-funded reward and penalty sanctions increase cooperation in public-goods-like resource dilemmas, with reward sanctions being more effective. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates, not payoffs. The design details (e.g., player count, rounds, sanctioning mechanism) are informative for mapping to other studies, but the lack of efficiency data limits direct applicability to the efficiency prediction task.

- source: Hoff, K., Kshetramade, M., & Fehr, E. (2011). Caste and Punishment: the Legacy of Caste Culture in Norm Enforcement. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that high caste individuals are much more willing to engage in costly third-party punishment of norm violations (defection in a sequential exchange game) than low caste individuals, when the victim is a member of their own specific caste. This difference is not explained by wealth, education, or political experience. The effect disappears when the victim is not a member of the punisher's own caste, indicating that in-group favouritism among high castes drives the result. The paper does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, but discusses the implications for collective action and norm enforcement capacity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the willingness to punish norm violations is moderated by social identity (caste status and in-group affiliation), but does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the paper suggests that punishment may be more effective at sustaining cooperation in groups with strong in-group identity and high willingness to punish, but does not quantify efficiency outcomes. The game design is a close variant of a public goods or trust game with third-party punishment, but efficiency is not measured. Use this paper to inform moderators of punishment behavior, not direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Benard, S. (2012). Cohesion from Conflict: Does Intergroup Conflict Motivate Intragroup Norm Enforcement and Support for Centralized Leadership?. *Social Psychology Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that intergroup conflict increases the likelihood that group members will punish noncontributors (enforce norms) and support centralized leadership, but only when the outgroup is actively competing (i.e., high outgroup contribution). Conflict increases individual contributions to the group regardless of outgroup competitiveness. The effect of punishment is measured as the frequency of norm enforcement, not as its impact on group efficiency or total payoff. The presence of punishment does not appear to be experimentally manipulated as an on/off treatment; rather, punishment is always available, and the focus is on when and how much it is used. No direct analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare is provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about when group members are more likely to use costly punishment in public-goods-like games, specifically under intergroup conflict and high outgroup competition. However, it does not provide data on the effect of enabling punishment on group efficiency or payoff, nor does it compare efficiency with and without punishment. Thus, it is not directly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, given game design dimensions and control efficiency. It does suggest that punishment is more likely to be used in conflictual, competitive intergroup settings, but does not address whether this improves or reduces group efficiency.

- source: Tenbrunsel, A. E., & Messick, D. M. (1999). Sanctioning systems, decision frames, and cooperation. *Administrative Science Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across three studies, the paper finds that introducing a weak sanctioning system (low probability of detection, small fines) in social dilemma settings reduces cooperation rates compared to no sanctions. Strong sanctioning systems (high probability, large fines) increase cooperation rates above both weak-sanction and no-sanction conditions. The effect is mediated by the decision frame: sanctions make participants more likely to view the situation as a business decision, leading to cost-benefit reasoning. When the frame is ethical, cooperation is high regardless of sanctions. The studies use two-person and multi-person social dilemma scenarios with payoff tables, but do not report group efficiency or total payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by implication, efficiency) depends critically on the strength of the punishment system. Weak punishment can reduce cooperation below baseline, while strong punishment can increase it. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and the psychological framing induced by punishment are key moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not reported, but the results imply that adding weak punishment may decrease efficiency, while strong punishment may increase it, conditional on the induced decision frame.

- source: Mulder, L. B. (2008). The difference between punishments and rewards in fostering moral concerns in social decision making. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that introducing the concept of punishment (without actual economic consequences) in a public-goods-like game increases cooperation and moral concerns compared to reward or control. Actual punishment (vs. reward) in a dictator-type allocation game leads to stronger moral disapproval of non-compliers and greater willingness to disadvantage them, even at a personal cost. The effect of punishment on cooperation is mediated by increased moral concerns and collective-interest motives. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment (as a concept or mechanism) increases cooperation and moral concerns more than reward in public-goods-like settings. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, which are the target for prediction. The findings suggest that, under similar game design dimensions, punishment is likely to increase cooperation, but the magnitude of efficiency gains or losses cannot be inferred from this paper. The results are most useful for predicting behavioral and attitudinal changes, not efficiency.

  findings: The paper finds that responsible punishment and cooperation can co-evolve and become stable in populations when interactions are non-anonymous and reputation can be built. In anonymous settings, cooperation and responsible punishment do not emerge, and antisocial punishment can persist. The model provides a threshold for the amount of information (reputation) needed for responsible punishment to be adaptive. The results are robust to errors in reputation and extend to public goods games with more than two players, though larger group sizes raise the threshold for reputation needed. The paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes, but the emergence of cooperation and responsible punishment implies higher group welfare than in the absence of these behaviors.

In anonymous settings, punishment does not promote cooperation or efficiency. The model also suggests that larger group sizes make it harder for punishment to be effective. However, because the paper does not report explicit efficiency or payoff outcomes, its value for direct quantitative prediction is limited; it is most useful for understanding the qualitative role of reputation, anonymity, and group size as moderators.

- source: Lehmann, L., Rousset, F., Roze, D., & Keller, L. (2007). Strong reciprocity or strong ferocity? A population genetic view of the evolution of altruistic punishment. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that strong reciprocity (costly helping plus costly punishment) can evolve in spatially structured populations if helping and punishment are tightly linked (no recombination) and relatedness is sufficiently high. In this case, strong reciprocity can invade even when rare. When helping and punishment are unlinked (recombination is strong), neither costly helping nor costly punishment can invade from rarity unless punishment yields a direct fitness benefit to the actor. The model shows that kin selection (genetic or cultural) is necessary for the evolution of strong reciprocity. The analysis is theoretical and does not provide direct predictions about group efficiency or payoff, but rather about the evolutionary stability and spread of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into the evolutionary preconditions for the emergence of punishment and cooperation in public-goods-like settings, especially the importance of linkage between helping and punishment, population structure (relatedness), and direct fitness effects. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions about group efficiency or payoff with and without punishment, nor does it report outcomes directly comparable to experimental public goods games. Its main value for prediction is in highlighting that punishment is unlikely to increase cooperation or efficiency in anonymous, unstructured, or one-shot settings unless there is kinship or direct benefit to punishers. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative.

  findings: The paper argues that punishment, especially when combined with cultural learning mechanisms (such as conformist and prestige-biased transmission), can stabilize costly cooperative norms in human groups. It reviews theoretical models showing that the effectiveness of punishment in maintaining cooperation depends on the cost of punishing relative to the cost of being punished, and that social norms enforced by punishment can persist even when the cooperative act is individually costly. The paper also discusses how group-level selection can favor groups with more effective cooperative norms, and how cultural evolution allows for rapid adaptation of cooperation strategies. However, the paper does not provide empirical or quantitative findings on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the mechanisms and evolutionary logic.

It highlights the importance of social norms, cultural learning, and group structure in moderating the effects of punishment. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the magnitude of efficiency changes when punishment is enabled. Its value for prediction lies in its mechanistic insights and identification of key moderators (e.g., punishment cost/effectiveness, group size, norm strength), but not in providing direct outcome estimates.

Conformism preserves between-group variation and stabilizes punishment, while punishment protects against the spread of defection. The model demonstrates that cooperation can actually increase with group size when both mechanisms are present. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of behavioral frequencies (cooperation, punishment) rather than direct efficiency or group payoff.

It reviews evidence that, in the absence of punishment, cooperation in public goods games typically decays over repeated rounds, but that the opportunity for punishment can sustain higher levels of cooperation. The paper also discusses the limits of altruism, noting that higher costs of punishment or contribution reduce the frequency of these behaviors. While the paper provides strong theoretical and empirical support for the importance of punishment in sustaining cooperation, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff effects from punishment interventions.

For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is most useful for contextualizing the likely direction of punishment effects (i.e., more positive for cooperation and, by implication, possibly for efficiency), but it does not provide parameter estimates or direct outcome data for model training or calibration.

  findings: The paper finds that punishment can promote cooperation in laboratory public goods games with humans, but evidence for similar effects in non-human animals is rare. It argues that punishment is most likely to evolve in symmetric n-player games or asymmetric two-player games, especially when cheating is a discrete event and repeated interactions are likely. The paper highlights cognitive and ecological constraints that limit the prevalence of punishment in nature. While it discusses the theoretical efficiency of punishment (e.g., when it is wasteful or beneficial), it does not provide empirical data on efficiency or group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and conceptual guidance on when punishment is likely to increase cooperation in public goods-like settings, emphasizing the importance of player number, time horizon, and the nature of cheating. However, it does not provide empirical estimates or direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency given game design dimensions and control efficiency. Its main value is in highlighting moderators and mechanisms that may inform the interpretation of empirical studies.

- source: Buckholtz, J. W., & Marois, R. (2012). The roots of modern justice: cognitive and neural foundations of social norms and their enforcement. *Nature Neuroscience*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper argues that the ability to enforce social norms through punishment is central to the evolution and maintenance of large-scale human cooperation. It reviews evidence from behavioral economics (including public goods games and ultimatum games) showing that the possibility of punishment increases cooperation and norm compliance, and that individuals are willing to incur costs to punish norm violators. The commentary also synthesizes neuroscientific findings on the brain regions involved in norm enforcement, proposing a model in which domain-general cognitive processes (such as value learning, mentalizing, and response selection) are repurposed for social norm enforcement. The paper notes that both punishment and reward can promote cooperation, but does not provide new empirical data on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and mechanistic support for the idea that punishment increases cooperation and norm compliance in public-goods-game-like environments, referencing empirical studies that show this effect. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff outcomes with and without punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the general expectation that enabling punishment will increase cooperation, but does not allow for precise prediction of efficiency changes based on game design dimensions or control efficiency. Its value is primarily in contextualizing the importance of punishment and identifying relevant behavioral and neural mechanisms.

- source: Boyd, R., & Richerson, P. J. (2009). Voting with your feet: Payoff biased migration and the evolution of group beneficial behavior. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that payoff-biased migration—where individuals move from lower-payoff to higher-payoff groups and assimilate—can lead to the spread and predominance of group-beneficial behaviors, resulting in higher average group payoffs (efficiency). This effect is strongest when migration rates are low enough to maintain between-group cultural variation, but not so low as to prevent migration from having an effect. The model is general and can represent public goods, mutualism, and punishment-based norm enforcement. The presence of punishment is discussed as a mechanism that can stabilize group-beneficial equilibria, which are associated with higher group payoffs. The model predicts that, under plausible conditions, group-beneficial (efficient) norms will predominate, but alternative (less efficient) norms may persist in a polymorphic equilibrium.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that mechanisms which stabilize group-beneficial behaviors (including punishment) can lead to higher group efficiency, especially in structured populations with migration. The model is not a direct PGG experiment, but its payoff structure and discussion of punishment-based norm enforcement are closely related. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the presence of effective punishment (or other norm-stabilizing mechanisms) is likely to increase efficiency, particularly when migration or group structure allows for between-group variation and selective migration. However, the model is abstract and does not provide quantitative estimates for specific game design parameters such as player count, MPCR, or punishment cost, so its guidance is qualitative: enabling effective punishment or norm enforcement should increase efficiency, especially when group structure and migration allow for the persistence and spread of efficient norms.

- source: Rankin, D. J., Bargum, K., & Kokko, H. (2007). The tragedy of the commons in evolutionary biology. *Trends in Ecology & Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper reviews the tragedy of the commons as a framework for understanding how individual-level selfishness can reduce group-level outcomes, including group fitness and productivity, in both human and non-human systems. It highlights that mechanisms such as punishment, policing, kin selection, and ecological feedbacks can prevent or reduce the severity of the tragedy, sometimes restoring group productivity or preventing collapse. The review draws analogies to public goods games and discusses the importance of distinguishing between complete collapse (collapsing tragedy) and partial reduction in group fitness (component tragedy).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual support for the idea that punishment and related mechanisms can improve group outcomes (efficiency) in public-goods-like environments by altering incentives and deterring selfish behavior. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence that would allow direct prediction of efficiency changes from game design dimensions. Its value lies in supporting the general expectation that punishment, when effective and not too costly, can prevent or mitigate efficiency losses due to free-riding, but it does not specify how this effect depends on specific game parameters.

- source: Doebeli, M., & Hauert, C. (2005). Models of cooperation based on the Prisoner's Dilemma and the Snowdrift game. *Ecology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper synthesizes theoretical results on how various mechanisms (iteration, spatial structure, indirect reciprocity, punishment, reward) affect the evolution and maintenance of cooperation in social dilemmas. It notes that spatial structure promotes cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma but can reduce cooperation in the Snowdrift game. Indirect reciprocity, punishment, and reputation can promote cooperation, especially when individuals can condition their behavior on others' reputations. The review also discusses the generalization to public goods games and the impact of group size, voluntary participation, and continuous investment. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the frequency and stability of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how design dimensions such as group size, spatial structure, iteration, and the presence of punishment or reward mechanisms can affect cooperation in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that punishment and reputation mechanisms can promote cooperation, and that spatial structure can have divergent effects depending on the game. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, it is not directly usable for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. Its value is mainly in informing mechanistic expectations and highlighting possible moderators, not in providing quantitative effect estimates.

- source: Ostrom, E. (2000). Collective action and the evolution of social norms. *Journal of Economic Perspectives*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost
  findings: Ostrom reviews and synthesizes experimental and field evidence on collective action, focusing on public goods and common-pool resource dilemmas. She identifies that communication and the ability to punish free riders both substantially increase cooperation rates in laboratory public goods games. The paper argues that real-world resource regimes that allow users to design their own rules, monitor each other, and impose graduated sanctions tend to be more sustainable and effective. However, the paper does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report new experimental data. Instead, it provides a conceptual framework for understanding how punishment, communication, and institutional design affect cooperation and the sustainability of collective action.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong conceptual and qualitative support for the idea that enabling punishment and communication increases cooperation in public goods and collective action settings. It suggests that punishment mechanisms, especially when locally designed and enforced, can help sustain cooperation and reduce free riding. However, the paper does not provide quantitative data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it specify the effects of particular game design dimensions in a way that would allow direct prediction of treatment efficiency from control efficiency and design features. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the inclusion of punishment and communication as positive moderators of cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence for efficiency outcomes or for the effect sizes associated with specific design parameters.

  findings: The paper reviews the literature on CPR and public goods experiments, highlighting that communication (e.g., face-to-face chat) increases cooperation and average earnings, while costly punishment is used by participants but can reduce net average returns unless chosen voluntarily by the group. The paper emphasizes the importance of micro-situational variables (such as group size, communication, and context) and the need for richer data collection to understand decision-making. It also discusses the crowding-out effect of external regulation and the mixed effects of ecological dynamics on cooperation and punishment. However, the paper does not provide new empirical estimates or direct analysis of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and methodological guidance for interpreting experimental results on punishment and cooperation in CPR and public goods settings. It suggests that communication and voluntary punishment can improve cooperation, but costly punishment may reduce net payoffs unless used sparingly or chosen by the group. The paper highlights the importance of context and micro-situational variables, which should be considered when predicting the effects of punishment on efficiency. However, because it does not report new empirical data or directly analyze efficiency outcomes, its value for quantitative prediction is limited to qualitative insights and literature synthesis.

- source: Raihani, N., & Aitken, D. (2011). Uncertainty, rationality and cooperation in the context of climate change. *Climatic Change*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper argues that cooperation in large-scale social dilemmas like climate change can be promoted by mechanisms that increase the net benefit of cooperation relative to free-riding, such as punishment, rewards, communication, and reputation. It highlights that uncertainty and irrational responses (e.g., loss aversion, framing effects) can inhibit cooperation. The authors review evidence that punishment and reward can both increase cooperation in public goods games, but that punishment is costly and may only be effective once cooperation is already established, while rewards may be more effective in early stages. Communication and information about others' actions also increase cooperation. The paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes directly, but suggests that these mechanisms can indirectly improve collective outcomes by increasing cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and review-based support for the idea that punishment, reward, communication, and framing can increase cooperation in public goods games and climate change contexts. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or payoff changes due to punishment. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the presence of punishment (especially when combined with rewards and communication) is likely to increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency is not directly addressed. The review highlights that punishment is more effective when cooperation is already high, and that rewards may be more effective in low-cooperation settings. The paper is useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators but does not provide direct empirical input for efficiency prediction.

- source: van der Heijden, E., Potters, J., & Sefton, M. (2009). Hierarchy and opportunism in teams. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment compares two institutions: equal revenue-sharing (control) and leader-determined shares (treatment, where one member can allocate output). In the control, free-riding leads to declining effort and efficiency, with teams achieving on average 61% of maximum possible earnings. In the leader treatment, most leaders reward contributors and exclude shirkers, resulting in average team earnings of 83% of maximum possible. The presence of a leader thus leads to a significant and substantial improvement in efficiency and group payoff. However, the benefit depends on the leader's behavior: teams with unfair or opportunistic leaders perform poorly. The results are explained by a reputation model where leaders may act fairly to sustain cooperation, especially in repeated games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a central authority with reward power (akin to a 'punishment' or sanctioning mechanism, though framed as reward) can substantially increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, provided the leader uses their power to reward contributors and punish shirkers. The effect is robust in a repeated, all-or-nothing contribution game with four players and perfect monitoring. For prediction, the presence of a leader with discretionary allocation power should be expected to increase efficiency relative to equal sharing, especially when the leader can observe and condition rewards on individual contributions. However, the effect depends on the leader's willingness to use their power to enforce cooperation. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where the 'punishment' is implemented via exclusion from rewards rather than explicit costly punishment, but the mechanism is closely related.

- source: Castillo, D., Bousquet, F., Janssen, M. A., Worrapimphong, K., & Cardenas, J. C. (2011). Context matters to explain field experiments: Results from Colombian and Thai fishing villages. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing externally imposed rules with probabilistic sanctions (confiscation of yield upon detection) in a CPR field experiment in fishing villages leads to only modest improvements in group efficiency and earnings. The effect is significant only when the quota rule is chosen and followed, but in most groups, rule breaking remains common and efficiency gains are small or absent. The context—such as economic necessity, lack of trust in authorities, and low legitimacy of rules—plays a major role in limiting the effectiveness of sanctions. The findings suggest that punishment mechanisms, as implemented here, do not reliably increase efficiency in real-world resource-dependent communities, except under specific conditions of rule choice and compliance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in field settings with resource-dependent participants, the introduction of rules with probabilistic sanctions (punishment) does not reliably increase efficiency in public-goods-like games. Efficiency gains are observed only when the group chooses and complies with a quota rule; otherwise, rule breaking and contextual barriers (e.g., lack of trust, economic necessity) undermine the effect of punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment mechanisms alone is not sufficient to ensure higher efficiency—contextual factors and the type of rule matter. Control efficiency (without punishment) is low, and treatment efficiency (with punishment) is only modestly higher, and only in some cases. Game design dimensions such as the absence of communication, the probabilistic and relatively weak punishment, and the lack of participant buy-in to rules are important moderators.

- source: Ahn, T. K., Isaac, R. M., & Salmon, T. C. (2008). Endogenous group formation. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that group formation rules significantly affect both contribution behavior and efficiency in a public goods environment. Restricted entry (a form of social punishment) leads to higher contributions in mid-sized groups but smaller group sizes, resulting in lower overall efficiency compared to free entry/exit or restricted exit. Restricted exit leads to declining contributions over time and does not improve efficiency. The ability to punish by denying entry is more effective at increasing contributions than the ability to punish by reducing one's own contribution. However, the efficiency gains from higher contributions in restricted entry are offset by the smaller group sizes, so overall earnings are lower. The study highlights that the specific form of the punishment or sanction mechanism is crucial for its effect on cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the design of group formation and sanction mechanisms (such as voting to deny entry) can have substantial effects on both contribution behavior and efficiency in public goods games. However, the effect on efficiency is not always positive: mechanisms that increase contributions may also reduce group size, lowering overall group earnings. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence and type of punishment/sanction mechanism, as well as group formation rules, should be considered alongside standard game parameters. The findings caution that not all punishment-like mechanisms increase efficiency, and their effects may depend on how they interact with group size and formation dynamics.
