- source: Gächter, S., Nosenzo, D., Renner, E., & Sefton, M. (2010). Sequential vs. simultaneous contributions to public goods: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that, in two-player public goods games with asymmetric returns, sequential move structures can lead to lower aggregate contributions and earnings compared to simultaneous moves, but only when the high-return player moves first (FMA treatments). When the low-return player moves first, or when asymmetry is very high (NOFMA treatments), aggregate earnings and contributions are similar across move structures. The predicted first-mover advantage is not observed; instead, fairness concerns and the threat of 'punishment' by second movers (via low contributions) compress the distribution of earnings and contributions. Explicit punishment and reward mechanisms are not present, but behavioral analogues are observed. Efficiency (aggregate earnings relative to the social optimum) is highest in simultaneous and low-first-mover sequential games, and lowest in high-first-mover sequential games, but the differences are moderate and depend on parameterization.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in two-player public goods games with asymmetric returns, the move structure (sequential vs. simultaneous) can affect efficiency, but only under certain conditions. Specifically, efficiency is lower in sequential games when the high-return player moves first, but not otherwise. There is no explicit punishment mechanism, so the findings are most relevant for predicting efficiency in games without formal punishment but with the possibility of endogenous 'punishment' via contribution choices. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that, in the absence of explicit punishment, efficiency is sensitive to move structure and player asymmetry, but not to the presence of formal punishment mechanisms. The results are less directly informative for games with explicit punishment options.

- source: Foster, K. R. (2004). Diminishing returns in social evolution: the not-so-tragic commons. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper develops and analyzes mathematical models of social evolution in group-structured populations, focusing on how the functional form of returns to investment (linear vs. diminishing) affects group performance (efficiency) in the face of selfish competition. It finds that, under diminishing returns, the negative impact of competition on group efficiency is greatly reduced, and group performance can be close to the optimum even with low relatedness and no explicit enforcement. The paper provides examples from social vertebrates, insects, and microbes, arguing that diminishing returns are common in real biological systems. Explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are not modeled, but enforcement is discussed as one of several mechanisms that can limit the tragedy of the commons.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency impact of selfish competition in public-goods-like environments is highly sensitive to the shape of the returns to investment. In prediction tasks, if the underlying game or biological system features diminishing returns to cooperation or competition, the efficiency loss from selfish behavior (and by extension, the potential efficiency gain from adding punishment) may be much smaller than predicted by linear models. However, because explicit punishment is not modeled, the paper does not provide direct evidence about the marginal effect of enabling punishment in a PGG. It does, however, suggest that in environments with diminishing returns, the baseline efficiency may already be high, potentially reducing the incremental value of punishment.

- source: Iwasa, Y., Uchida, T., & Yokomizo, H. (2007). Nonlinear behavior of the socio-economic dynamics for lake eutrophication control. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the interplay between social pressure (which increases with both the number of cooperators and the level of pollution) and individual cost-benefit decisions can lead to complex group-level outcomes. Depending on parameter values, the system can settle into a high-cooperation/clean-water equilibrium, a low-cooperation/polluted equilibrium, or exhibit oscillatory or chaotic dynamics. The model shows that increasing the effectiveness of pollution-reducing technology can paradoxically worsen water quality if it reduces the perceived need for cooperation. Conservatism (slower behavioral change) can stabilize cooperation and maintain higher group welfare. The model does not include explicit punishment, but social pressure is discussed as a possible proxy for reputational or sanctioning effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how social feedbacks and behavioral dynamics can affect group efficiency in public-goods-like settings. While it does not directly model punishment, it suggests that mechanisms increasing social pressure (which could include punishment, reputation, or social norms) can promote cooperation and improve group outcomes, but may also lead to multiple stable states or complex dynamics. The findings support the idea that the structure and strength of social incentives (including, potentially, punishment) are critical moderators of efficiency. However, the absence of explicit punishment or experimental manipulation limits the direct applicability of the results to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in empirical PGGs.

- source: Panchanathan, K., & Boyd, R. (2004). Indirect reciprocity can stabilize cooperation without the second-order free rider problem. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that linking collective action (public goods contribution) to indirect reciprocity (mutual aid based on reputation) can stabilize cooperation through exclusion of non-contributors from future aid. This mechanism avoids the second-order free rider problem because punishers (Shunners) do not incur a direct cost; instead, they withhold aid from defectors, which is socially sanctioned. The model identifies conditions under which this strategy is evolutionarily stable and shows that both full cooperation (Shunner) and full defection (Defector) can be stable equilibria, depending on initial frequencies and assortment. The model also shows that strategies linking collective action and indirect reciprocity cannot easily invade populations practicing only indirect reciprocity. While the model discusses group payoff and welfare in the context of equilibrium selection, it does not report explicit efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that indirect punishment via exclusion (rather than direct costly punishment) can stabilize cooperation in public goods games, especially when reputation is visible and linked across domains. It suggests that such mechanisms can lead to higher group payoff and welfare, and that equilibrium selection processes (e.g., group selection, random drift) may favor these strategies. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions about efficiency or the effect of enabling punishment in a standard PGG. Its relevance is strongest for settings where punishment is implemented as exclusion from future benefits rather than direct payoff reduction. The model's insights are most applicable to predicting when and why punishment-like mechanisms (especially exclusion) might be effective, but less so for precise efficiency prediction in standard experimental PGGs with direct costly punishment.

- source: Milinski, M., Semmann, D., Krambeck, H. J., & Marotzke, J. (2006). Stabilizing the Earth's climate is not a losing game: Supporting evidence from public goods experiments. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that cooperation in a climate public goods game can be substantially increased by making contributions public (enabling reputation effects) and by providing expert information about climate change. Social rewards (support in the indirect reciprocity game) reinforce cooperative behavior, and non-cooperators are socially 'punished' by being less likely to receive support. However, the game design does not include direct monetary punishment, and the outcome measured is not group efficiency or total earnings but rather the behavioral willingness to contribute to a public good with real-world impact. The findings suggest that reputation and information can be powerful motivators for cooperation in public goods dilemmas, even when the benefits are not directly redistributed to the contributors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that making contributions public (enabling reputation/reward) and providing expert information can increase cooperation rates in a public goods context, even when direct monetary punishment is absent and the public good is not redistributed. However, because the outcome is not group efficiency or total earnings, and because the 'punishment' is social (withholding support in a separate game) rather than direct, the findings are only adjacent to the core prediction task. The results suggest that enabling reputation or reward mechanisms may have a positive effect on cooperation, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes in standard PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Rand, D. G., Arbesman, S., & Christakis, N. A. (2011). Dynamic social networks promote cooperation in experiments with humans. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that in large groups playing a repeated networked cooperation game, cooperation decays over time in static, random, or slowly updating networks, but is stably maintained when players can frequently update their social ties. Players preferentially break links with defectors and form links with cooperators, which disciplines defectors and incentivizes cooperation. This dynamic network updating acts as a decentralized ostracism mechanism, supporting cooperation without explicit costly punishment. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are most directly about behavioral cooperation, not payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that dynamic, fluid social networks can sustain high cooperation rates in repeated social dilemma games, even in large groups. However, it does not directly address the effect of explicit punishment mechanisms (costly punishment) on efficiency or payoff. Instead, it studies a form of endogenous, non-costly ostracism (link breaking), which is adjacent to but not the same as standard punishment in public goods games. For prediction tasks about the effect of enabling costly punishment on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that non-costly, decentralized exclusion can promote cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or about the effect of costly punishment. The game design dimensions are partially informative (e.g., player count, network updating rules), but the absence of explicit payoff/efficiency outcomes and standard punishment limits direct applicability.

- source: De Cremer, D., & van Dijk, E. (2009). Paying for sanctions in social dilemmas: The effects of endowment asymmetry and accountability. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that in a step-level public good dilemma, high-endowment members contribute proportionally more to the public good than low-endowment members, regardless of accountability. However, when it comes to paying for a sanctioning system (which is framed as a second-order, continuous public good dilemma without a threshold), high-endowment members do not contribute more than low-endowment members unless they are made accountable and expect to be morally evaluated by the group. Accountability and evaluation expectancy increase contributions to the sanctioning system among high-endowment members, but these effects are not observed for contributions to the public good itself. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes, so the effect of these manipulations on efficiency is not directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the psychological and social moderators of willingness to pay for punishment systems in public good dilemmas, especially under endowment asymmetry. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that accountability and evaluation expectancy can increase willingness to pay for sanctions among high-endowment members, but the translation of this effect to efficiency or group payoff is not established. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly about the behavioral mechanisms that might affect the implementation of punishment systems.

- source: Chen, X. J., Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2012). Risk-driven migration and the collective-risk social dilemma. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study shows that risk-driven migration, where players move in response to the risk of collective failure, can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators in a collective-risk social dilemma. Cooperation is promoted most effectively at intermediate population densities and when the collective target is high and the risk function is not too flat. The mechanism outperforms random migration, which can actually inhibit cooperation. The results highlight the importance of spatial structure and self-organized mobility for sustaining cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for understanding how migration and risk of collective loss affect cooperation in public-goods-like environments, but it does not directly address the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or group payoff. The model does not include explicit costly punishment or reward mechanisms; instead, it uses a collective-risk loss as a sanction for group underperformance. Therefore, while the findings may inform predictions about the role of endogenous migration and risk in promoting cooperation, they do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency as required by the downstream prediction task. Use this paper for contextual or mechanistic insight, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Hauert, C. (2006). Spatial effects in social dilemmas. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that spatial structure can both promote and inhibit cooperation in social dilemmas, depending on the type of game (prisoner's dilemma, snowdrift, by-product mutualism, coordination) and the microscopic update rule. In strict prisoner's dilemma or public goods settings, spatial structure allows cooperators to survive by forming clusters, but the parameter range for this effect is limited. In snowdrift-type games, spatial structure can actually reduce cooperation. The effect of spatial structure is highly sensitive to the update rule: the death-birth process is most favorable to cooperation, while the replicator rule can lead to reduced cooperation in some parameter regions. The main outcome is the equilibrium fraction of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it is informative about how spatial structure, group size, cost-benefit ratios, and update rules affect the equilibrium level of cooperation in group social dilemmas. If the prediction task involves spatial structure or local interactions, the findings suggest that such structure can have complex, sometimes counterintuitive effects on cooperation, which may indirectly affect efficiency. The absence of punishment as a mechanism means the paper cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. The main value is in understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in spatially structured games.

- source: Santos, F. C., Vasconcelos, V. V., Santos, M. D., Neves, P. N. B., & Pacheco, J. M. (2012). EVOLUTIONARY DYNAMICS OF CLIMATE CHANGE UNDER COLLECTIVE-RISK DILEMMAS. *Mathematical Models & Methods in Applied Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in collective-risk dilemmas (a variant of public goods games with a threshold and risk of collective loss), cooperation and group efficiency are maximized in small groups with high risk and stringent requirements for success. The evolutionary dynamics show that risk is a key driver for cooperation: without risk, defection dominates, but with increasing risk, stable coexistence or dominance of cooperation can emerge. The model provides analytical and simulation results for both infinite and finite populations, showing how group size, risk, and threshold interact to determine the likelihood of cooperation and the average payoff. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are modeled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for predicting efficiency in threshold public goods games with collective risk, but it does not address the effect of punishment. It provides strong theoretical evidence that group size, risk level, and threshold requirements are key moderators of efficiency and cooperation. However, since punishment is not modeled, the paper does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to enabling punishment. Its results are most relevant for understanding baseline efficiency in collective-risk settings and for interpreting the effects of group size, risk, and threshold, but not for the incremental effect of punishment.

- source: Keller, J., & Pfattheicher, S. (2011). Vigilant Self-Regulation, Cues of Being Watched and Cooperativeness. *European Journal of Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The studies show that a cue of being watched (reputational cue) increases cooperative behavior (donation to a public good) among individuals with a strong prevention focus, but decreases it among those with a weak prevention focus. The effect is specific to prevention focus; promotion focus does not predict donation behavior. The manipulation is not punishment or sanctioning, but rather a reputational cue. The outcome is individual donation, not group efficiency or payoff. No repeated interaction, no explicit public goods game structure, and no punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. The design is a one-shot donation game with a reputational cue, not a repeated PGG with punishment. The findings may be relevant for understanding how reputational cues (such as being watched) interact with individual differences to affect cooperation, but they do not inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled versus disabled. The absence of punishment, reward, or group payoff outcomes limits its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Waite, A. J., & Shou, W. Y. (2012). Adaptation to a new environment allows cooperators to purge cheaters stochastically. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a well-mixed, engineered yeast system where cooperators pay a cost to produce a common good and cheaters do not, adaptation to a novel, nutrient-limited environment can allow cooperators to stochastically dominate and purge cheaters in some replicate cultures. This outcome is driven by the chance acquisition of highly beneficial mutations in cooperators, which can offset the initial fitness advantage of cheaters. The frequency of cooperator-dominated cultures is determined by the initial abundance of cooperators. The paper does not study or manipulate punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff in the sense relevant to public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or related mechanisms, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its findings about evolutionary adaptation and stochastic outcomes in microbial cooperation are only weakly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in human or animal public goods games. The main value is in illustrating that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., adaptation to novel environments) can allow cooperation to persist, but this is not directly transferable to the prediction task.

- source: Stoop, J., Noussair, C. N., & Van Soest, D. (2012). From the Lab to the Field: Cooperation among Fishermen. *Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that, in a real-world field setting where cooperation would require fishermen to forgo catching fish (an activity they enjoy), no cooperation emerges, and group efficiency does not improve relative to the control. This is in contrast to standard lab VCM experiments, where positive cooperation and higher efficiency are typically observed. The lack of cooperation is robust to subject pool, context, and setting, and is attributed to the nature of the activity required for cooperation. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present, so the paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a high-quality control case for field VCM-like games without punishment: efficiency remains at the non-cooperative baseline, with no spontaneous cooperation. However, it does not inform the effect of punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is implemented or analyzed. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper is useful only for establishing the baseline (control) efficiency in a field VCM-like environment with no punishment. It does not provide evidence on how punishment would change efficiency in this context.

- source: Semmann, D., Krambeck, H. J. R., & Milinski, M. (2003). Volunteering leads to rock-paper-scissors dynamics in a public goods game. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing players to opt out of the public goods game (become 'loners') leads to rock-paper-scissors dynamics among cooperators, defectors, and loners. This dynamic sustains a stable, nonzero level of cooperation and prevents the collapse to universal defection typically seen in standard public goods games. Average payoffs for cooperators and defectors converge to the loner payoff over time, as predicted by theory. The presence of the loner option increases the average frequency of cooperation compared to standard PGGs, but does not produce overwhelming cooperation. There is no punishment or reward mechanism in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it provides evidence that introducing an opt-out (volunteering/loner) option can sustain cooperation and stabilize payoffs in public goods games. For the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding alternative mechanisms (volunteering) that can also affect efficiency.

- source: Kameda, T., Tsukasaki, T., Hastie, R., & Berg, N. (2011). Democracy Under Uncertainty: The Wisdom of Crowds and the Free-Rider Problem in Group Decision Making. *Psychological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in group decision-making tasks with diminishing marginal returns (a nonlinear public goods environment), cooperation and free-riding coexist in a stable mixed equilibrium. In both simulation and laboratory experiment (with 6-person groups, 24 rounds, no punishment or reward), about half the group members consistently cooperate (paying a cost to acquire information and vote), while the rest free ride. The majority/plurality rule for aggregating decisions yields higher individual net payoffs (efficiency) than a best-member (dictator) rule, provided cooperation costs are not too high. The equilibrium level of cooperation is locally Pareto efficient, with three contributors and three free riders per group. The study demonstrates that efficient group outcomes can be achieved without punishment, provided the group production function is nonlinear with diminishing returns.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public-goods-like environments with diminishing marginal returns and no punishment, stable and efficient cooperation can emerge. The findings suggest that the pessimistic predictions of linear public goods games (where efficiency collapses without punishment) do not generalize to more realistic nonlinear settings. For the downstream prediction task, this paper indicates that the efficiency of the control game (without punishment) can be high if the production function is nonlinear and the group decision rule is majoritarian, even in the absence of punishment. The paper does not provide evidence about the effect of enabling punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the design. Therefore, it should be used as a control or baseline for understanding efficiency in the absence of punishment, especially in nonlinear group production environments.

- source: Bach, L. A., Helvik, T., & Christiansen, F. B. (2006). The evolution of n-player cooperation - threshold games and ESS bifurcations. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in n-player threshold public goods games, the existence and stability of cooperative equilibria depend critically on the cost-benefit ratio and the threshold required for the collective benefit. For certain parameter regions, both cooperative and defecting equilibria can coexist, and small changes in parameters can lead to sudden transitions (catastrophes) in group efficiency and cooperation. The model predicts hysteresis: once a population falls into full defection, simply restoring favorable parameters may not be sufficient to recover cooperation. The analysis provides explicit mathematical conditions for these transitions and demonstrates that such complex dynamics are only possible in games with at least three players. No punishment or reward mechanisms are included.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how group size, threshold structure, and cost-benefit parameters affect the efficiency and stability of cooperation in public-goods-like games without punishment. It is directly relevant for predicting baseline (control) efficiency in threshold public goods games, especially when the benefit is all-or-nothing and depends on a minimum number of cooperators. However, it does not inform the effect of punishment or sanctions, as these mechanisms are not modeled. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is useful for understanding how efficiency varies with game design in the absence of punishment, but provides no evidence on how enabling punishment would change efficiency.

- source: Tazelaar, M. J. A., Van Lange, P. A. M., & Ouwerkerk, J. W. (2004). How to cope with Noise, in social dilemmas: The benefits of communication. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that negative noise (randomly reducing the partner's intended contribution) significantly reduces cooperation and benign impressions in a repeated two-player give-some game. Allowing the partner to communicate about noise (explaining/apologizing for the noise) restores cooperation and benign impressions to levels indistinguishable from the no-noise baseline. These effects are especially pronounced for participants with low dispositional trust. The main outcomes are cooperation rates and psychological impressions, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does, however, provide strong evidence that communication can mitigate the negative effects of noise on cooperation in repeated continuous-contribution dilemmas. Since no punishment or sanctioning is present, and efficiency or group payoff is not analyzed, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful for understanding the role of communication and noise, but not for the core prediction task regarding punishment.

- source: Buchan, N. R., Grimalda, G., Wilson, R., Brewer, M., Fatas, E., & Foddy, M. (2009). Globalization and human cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both country-level and individual-level globalization are strongly positively associated with contributions to the global public good (World account) in a multilevel public goods experiment. There is no evidence that globalization is associated with increased local cooperation. The experiment does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. The main finding is that globalization predicts broader, more cosmopolitan cooperation, as measured by willingness to contribute to a global public good.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it analyze efficiency, group payoff, or related payoff-based outcomes. Its findings are about the relationship between globalization and willingness to contribute to global versus local public goods, not about the effect of game design dimensions or punishment on efficiency. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Camera, G., & Casari, M. (2009). Cooperation among Strangers under the Shadow of the Future. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In small, randomly matched groups playing indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma games, enabling costly personal punishment (where a player can pay 5 points to reduce the opponent's payoff by 10) in an otherwise anonymous, privately monitored environment increases both cooperation rates and realized efficiency. Specifically, average realized efficiency rises from 0.595 (private monitoring, no punishment) to 0.652 (private monitoring with punishment). The effect is comparable to that of making individual histories public (efficiency 0.815), though the efficiency gain from punishment is partially offset by the cost of punishment itself. The paper also finds that simply making aggregate histories public (without identities) does not improve efficiency. Subjects tend to use selective punishment strategies, and personal punishment is often used as a substitute for informal punishment (defecting in future rounds). Larger group sizes (14 vs. 4) lead to much lower cooperation and efficiency, suggesting strong group size effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence for the effect of enabling costly personal punishment in indefinitely repeated, small-group, random-matching prisoner's dilemma environments. The design is adjacent to public goods games but not identical (pairwise PD rather than n-player PGG), so results should be transferred with some caution. The key finding is that, holding all else constant, enabling punishment increases realized efficiency substantially (from 0.595 to 0.652), though not as much as making individual histories public (0.815). The cost of punishment reduces the efficiency gain relative to the increase in cooperation. The paper also shows that group size is a strong moderator: larger groups have much lower efficiency. For prediction, the paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency in small, anonymous, privately monitored repeated games, with the caveat that the effect is partly offset by the costliness of punishment. The results are most applicable to settings with 4 players, random matching, no chat, continuous contribution/defection, and a punishment cost/impact ratio of 5/10.

- source: Wu, J. J., Zhang, B. Y., Zhou, Z. X., He, Q. Q., Zheng, X. D., Cressman, R., & Tao, Y. (2009). Costly punishment does not always increase cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that, in a repeated two-player Prisoner's Dilemma with Chinese participants, the introduction of costly punishment either had no significant effect or reduced the frequency of cooperation, and led to a significant decrease in average group payoff (efficiency) in one treatment. In the other treatment, the decrease in payoff was not statistically significant. The results are contrasted with previous US-based studies, where costly punishment increased cooperation but did not improve group payoff. The authors attribute the difference to cultural factors, particularly attitudes toward reputation and authority. The study provides direct evidence that costly punishment does not always increase cooperation or efficiency, and may even reduce them depending on context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in repeated two-player Prisoner's Dilemma games (an adjacent but not exact variant of the public goods game), enabling costly punishment can reduce group efficiency (average payoff), especially when cooperation does not increase. The effect of punishment on efficiency is not universally positive and may depend on cultural context and game structure. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency, and in some settings (e.g., repeated dyadic games, certain cultures), it may even lower it. The paper also highlights that increased cooperation does not always translate to higher efficiency if punishment costs offset cooperative gains.

- source: Dreber, A., Rand, D. G., Fudenberg, D., & Nowak, M. A. (2008). Winners don't punish. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing a costly punishment option in a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma increases the frequency of cooperation but does not increase the average payoff of the group. In both parameterizations (benefit/cost ratios of 2 and 3), there is no significant difference in average payoff between the control (no punishment) and treatment (punishment allowed) conditions. Individuals who use punishment more frequently have lower total payoffs, and the most successful players do not use punishment at all. The results suggest that, in repeated dyadic cooperation games, punishment is maladaptive from an efficiency perspective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated dyadic cooperation games with direct reciprocity, enabling costly punishment does not improve group efficiency compared to a control without punishment. The cost of punishment cancels out the gains from increased cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar repeated dyadic games, the efficiency with punishment enabled will be similar to or lower than the control efficiency, even if cooperation rates rise. The findings are most directly applicable to repeated Prisoner's Dilemma designs, but the mechanism (costs of punishment offsetting cooperation gains) is likely relevant for other repeated social dilemma games with similar structure.

- source: Rigdon, M. (2009). Trust and reciprocity in incentive contracting. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that adding ex post punishment or reward options to a principal-agent game does not reduce efficiency or cooperation. Low-level incentives (1:1 cost-impact) have no significant effect compared to the control (implicit contract). High-level incentives (1:3 cost-impact), especially punishment, significantly increase both investment and returns, leading to higher efficiency (89.5% vs. 80% in control). High reward increases returns but not investment. Principals are more likely to use rewards than punishments, but the threat of high punishment is more effective at increasing efficiency. There is no evidence for 'motivation crowding out' or the 'Paradox of Organizational Trust' in this setting.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a bilateral trust/investment game with gains from exchange, enabling high-impact punishment (where the punishment impact is much greater than the cost to the punisher) increases efficiency relative to a control without punishment. Low-impact punishment or reward does not improve efficiency. The results suggest that, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar games, the effectiveness of punishment (punishment_tech) is crucial: only high punishment_tech leads to efficiency gains. The control efficiency (80%) is increased to 89.5% with high punishment. The findings are most directly relevant to principal-agent or trust games, but the mechanisms are adjacent to public goods games.

- source: Chen, Y., & Li, S. X. (2009). Group Identity and Social Preferences. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This laboratory experiment systematically measures the effects of induced group identity on social preferences and economic outcomes in a set of 24 two-person sequential allocation games (including dictator and response games with punishment/reward options). The key findings are: (1) participants are more likely to make social-welfare-maximizing (SWM) choices and earn higher payoffs when matched with ingroup members, and less likely to do so with outgroup members, compared to a control with no group identity; (2) participants are more likely to reward ingroup members for good behavior and less likely to punish them for misbehavior, while the opposite holds for outgroup members; (3) the gap in efficiency/payoff between ingroup and outgroup matches is driven more by losses in outgroup matches than gains in ingroup matches. The study also finds that the presence of group identity (even when randomly assigned) is sufficient to generate these effects, and that design features like chat can increase group attachment but have limited behavioral impact.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments, this paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is context-dependent: punishment is less likely to be used against ingroup members (reducing efficiency losses from antisocial punishment), but more likely against outgroup members (increasing efficiency losses). The presence of group identity can thus moderate the efficiency impact of punishment, with ingroup contexts supporting higher efficiency and outgroup contexts supporting lower efficiency, relative to a no-identity baseline. The findings suggest that group composition and identity salience are important moderators for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment mechanisms, even in games that are not classic PGGs but share key features (sequential allocation, punishment/reward options, payoff externalities).

- source: Abbink, K., Brandts, J., Herrmann, B., & Orzen, H. (2010). Intergroup Conflict and Intra-Group Punishment in an Experimental Contest Game. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a group contest game, the introduction of within-group punishment opportunities causes a large increase in unproductive conflict expenditures and a severe reduction in efficiency. In the baseline (no-punishment) group contest, expenditures are already well above equilibrium, but with punishment, they rise even further: in the last five rounds, contest expenditures are 119% higher than without punishment, and total material losses (including punishment costs) are 869% of the Nash equilibrium level, with rent dissipation exceeding 100% of the prize. Punishment is used primarily to target low contributors, and its presence reduces heterogeneity in team member contributions, but the net effect is to discipline members into even more wasteful over-investment. The results directly contradict the efficiency-enhancing effects of punishment observed in standard public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in contest games with public-good-like intra-group sharing, enabling within-group punishment leads to a large decrease in efficiency, with group payoffs and welfare falling far below both the equilibrium and the no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust across rounds and is not offset by any increase in group earnings. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment in a group contest environment should be expected to reduce efficiency, even if punishment increases contributions or reduces free-riding. The game design dimensions most relevant here are group size, number of rounds, the presence and cost/effectiveness of punishment, and the contest structure (lottery contest for a rival prize with intra-group sharing). Control efficiency (without punishment) is already low, but enabling punishment makes it much worse.

- source: Ule, A., Schram, A., Riedl, A., & Cason, T. N. (2009). Indirect Punishment and Generosity Toward Strangers. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compares two treatments of an indirect helping game: one where indirect punishment is only symbolic (no material effect on the recipient), and one where punishment is harmful (recipient loses 250 francs). In both, helping is costly and beneficial, and donors can observe recipients' recent behavior. The main finding is that, although the frequency of helping does not differ significantly between treatments, the availability of harmful punishment reduces the payoffs of defectors and increases the payoffs of rewarders and cautious defectors. In the control, defectors earn most; with harmful punishment, rewarders and cautious defectors earn more than defectors. Harmful punishment is rare but targeted at defectors, and this is sufficient to change the payoff structure in favor of cooperation-supporting strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling costly, effective punishment in an indirect reciprocity environment (adjacent to PGG) increases the average payoff of cooperative strategies relative to defectors, even if overall cooperation rates do not rise. For prediction, this suggests that, in games where punishment is enabled and is materially costly to the recipient, group efficiency or average payoff can increase relative to a control with only symbolic or no punishment, primarily by reducing defectors' payoffs. The effect is not driven by increased cooperation rates but by the deterrent effect of punishment on defectors' earnings. The design is not a standard PGG but is closely related in its focus on cooperation, punishment, and payoff outcomes.

- source: ELLISON, G. (1994). COOPERATION IN THE PRISONERS-DILEMMA WITH ANONYMOUS RANDOM MATCHING. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that contagious punishment strategies can sustain cooperation and near-efficient payoffs in large, anonymous, randomly-matched repeated prisoner's dilemma games. The equilibrium is robust to small amounts of noise (players making mistakes) and does not require excessive patience except in very large populations or when the gain from defection is very small. The efficiency loss due to noise can be made arbitrarily small as the noise probability decreases. The results hold even without public randomization devices, though the required patience may increase in some parameter regimes. The analysis provides explicit conditions (in terms of discount factor, population size, and gain from defection) under which efficient cooperation is possible.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that punishment mechanisms (here, contagious punishment) can enable high efficiency in repeated social dilemmas, even under severe informational constraints (anonymity, random matching, no identification, no communication). The results are most directly applicable to repeated prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent to public goods games. The findings suggest that, for games with similar structure and sufficient patience, enabling punishment can substantially increase efficiency relative to the baseline (no punishment) case. The paper also provides explicit formulas and numerical examples for the minimum patience (discount factor) required for cooperation at different population sizes and temptation levels. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that punishment can enable near-full efficiency in repeated, anonymous, randomly-matched games, provided the population is not too large, the gain from defection is not too high, and players are sufficiently patient. The results are robust to small noise and do not require public randomization, though the patience requirement may increase in those cases.

- source: Coffman, L. C. (2011). Intermediation Reduces Punishment (and Reward). *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that when a first mover can choose to act selfishly either directly or via an intermediary, third-party punishment for selfishness is significantly lower when the action is taken through the intermediary. This is true even when the intermediary is passive and cannot be blamed. As a result, the poorest player (receiver) earns much less on average (from $4.50 to $1.63), and group earnings fall (from $19.55 to $17.24) when intermediation is available. The effect is robust to various controls and alternative explanations (e.g., diffusion of responsibility, confusion, lack of reasoning, or mere presence of a third party). The reduction in punishment is not explained by standard models of fairness or responsibility diffusion. The paper also finds that even when subjects are aware that intermediation is used to avoid punishment and that it harms the poorest player, they still punish less. A follow-up study shows a similar effect for rewards: indirect charitable giving is rewarded less than direct giving.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the availability of an intermediary (a form of indirectness) in a punishment-enabled environment can dramatically reduce the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining pro-social outcomes, leading to lower efficiency and welfare. For prediction tasks, this suggests that game designs allowing for indirect actions or intermediation—even with punishment enabled—will likely see much lower efficiency than comparable direct-action games. The control (no intermediary) game with punishment achieves high receiver payoffs and group efficiency, but adding the intermediation option sharply reduces both, even though punishment is still available and costless. Thus, the presence of intermediation is a strong negative moderator of punishment's positive effect on efficiency.

- source: Lippert, S., & Spagnolo, G. (2011). Networks of relations and Word-of-Mouth Communication. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in networks of repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games, the ability to transmit information (word-of-mouth) and the use of forgiving, targeted punishment strategies (Multilateral Repentance) allow for cooperation to be sustained under a wider range of conditions (i.e., lower discount factors) than under traditional contagion (Grim Trigger) strategies. This leads to higher efficiency and welfare, especially during punishment phases, as only defectors are punished and cooperation among others continues. The structure of the network (closure, density) and the speed of information transmission further moderate these effects. The results are formalized in terms of equilibrium payoff conditions and sustainability criteria.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling targeted, forgiving punishment (as opposed to all-or-nothing breakdowns) and facilitating information transmission (communication) in networked repeated social dilemmas increases the efficiency of cooperation. The results suggest that, all else equal, punishment regimes that are more targeted and allow for communication will yield higher efficiency than those that rely on contagious, permanent breakdowns. The paper also highlights the importance of network structure (closure, density) and information speed, which may inform how design dimensions like communication, punishment technology, and information feedback affect efficiency. However, the results are theoretical and do not provide quantitative effect sizes.

- source: Hilbe, C., & Sigmund, K. (2010). Incentives and opportunism: from the carrot to the stick. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The model demonstrates that negative incentives (punishment) can stabilize cooperation and lead to higher average payoffs (efficiency) in the population, provided that players have enough information about others' reputations. Rewarding (positive incentives) is not stable in the long run but is important for catalyzing the initial emergence of cooperation, especially when information is limited. When both punishment and reward are available, the population transitions from defection to cooperation more quickly, and the stable outcome is a cooperative, high-payoff equilibrium enforced by punishment. The results are robust to population size and mutation structure, and the authors discuss how the findings generalize to n-person public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in a public-goods-like environment will increase efficiency (average group payoff), especially when players can observe or infer others' incentive-providing behavior. The model suggests that the effect of punishment is more pronounced when information about others is available, and that the presence of reward mechanisms can accelerate the transition to cooperation but does not affect the long-run efficiency as much as punishment does. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, two-stage games but are argued to generalize to n-person PGGs. For prediction tasks, the paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency, especially when information/reputation is high, and suggests that the marginal benefit of adding reward is mainly in catalyzing cooperation rather than sustaining higher efficiency in the long run.

- source: Bendor, J., & Swistak, P. (2001). The evolution of norms. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper proves that in repeated games of enforceable cooperation, only social norms (strategies that prescribe third-party punishment) can be evolutionarily stable. The presence of effective punishment is necessary for stability. Both efficient and inefficient social norms can be stable, but efficient (cooperative) norms are more robust to invasion and require a lower frequency to stabilize. The model predicts that, over time, groups are more likely to drift toward efficient, cooperative equilibria regulated by social norms, but inefficient, punishment-backed equilibria are also possible and robust. The findings are general for two-person repeated games and extend to sparse networks, provided third-party information is sufficiently available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the necessity of punishment (sanctions) for stable cooperation and efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. It shows that the presence of punishment mechanisms is a necessary condition for the evolutionary stability of cooperative, efficient outcomes, but does not guarantee efficiency: inefficient, punishment-backed equilibria can also be stable. However, more efficient equilibria are more robust to invasion and require a lower threshold to stabilize. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in a repeated game with enforceable cooperation will generally increase the likelihood of stable cooperation and higher efficiency, but the actual efficiency achieved may depend on the specific norm that evolves. The robustness of efficient equilibria to invasion is higher, so in the long run, efficiency is more likely with punishment enabled, but inefficient outcomes are possible. The results are most directly applicable to two-person repeated games, but the logic extends to larger groups and sparse networks if third-party information is available.

- source: dos Santos, M., Rankin, D. J., & Wedekind, C. (2011). The evolution of punishment through reputation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that punishment, when reputationally visible, can evolve and be stable because it increases the likelihood of receiving help in the future, offsetting its immediate cost. Analytical and simulation results show that punishing discriminators (who punish defectors and are recognized for it) achieve higher payoffs and can dominate, leading to high cooperation and group efficiency. The effect is robust to a range of costs and benefits, but is sensitive to the accuracy of reputation information and the mutation rate. The findings suggest that reputation is a key moderator of punishment's efficiency-enhancing effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the efficiency gains from punishment depend critically on whether punishment is reputationally visible and can influence future interactions. In environments where reputation for punishing is tracked and used by others to condition their cooperation, punishment can increase group efficiency and stabilize cooperation. The results are robust across a range of costs and benefits, but efficiency gains are reduced if reputation information is noisy or mutation rates are low. For prediction, the presence of a reputation system that makes punishment visible is a key moderator for positive efficiency effects of punishment.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., Iwasa, Y., & Nowak, M. A. (2009). Indirect reciprocity provides only a narrow margin of efficiency for costly punishment. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in models of indirect reciprocity, strategies that use costly punishment (punishing defectors at a cost to oneself) are almost always less efficient than strategies that simply withhold cooperation from defectors. Costly punishment can only be the most efficient equilibrium in a very small region of parameter space, requiring fine-tuned values of benefit, cost, punishment cost, punishment impact, and reputation accuracy. In most cases, the highest group payoff (efficiency) is achieved by not using costly punishment. The results are robust to errors in reputation assignment and action execution, as long as these errors are not too frequent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in indirect reciprocity settings (which are adjacent to public goods games), enabling costly punishment will almost always reduce group efficiency compared to not enabling punishment, except in a very narrow set of parameter values. For prediction tasks, this suggests that unless the game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, punishment impact, benefit/cost ratio, reputation accuracy) are in the narrow region identified, enabling punishment should be expected to lower efficiency. The findings are most directly applicable to indirect reciprocity games with binary reputation and second-order norms, but the logic may transfer to PGG-like environments with similar structure.

- source: Cai, G. S., & Kock, N. (2009). An evolutionary game theoretic perspective on e-collaboration: The collaboration effort and media relativeness. *European Journal of Operational Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in two-player e-collaboration games, introducing or increasing social punishment for defection increases the likelihood of full cooperation, which is Pareto efficient and maximizes group payoff. When punishment is low, defection or asymmetric cooperation is stable; when punishment exceeds a threshold (typically half the cost of cooperation), full cooperation becomes the unique stable Nash equilibrium. This result holds in both discrete and continuous effort models. The paper also shows that the required punishment to induce cooperation decreases as collaboration efficiency increases. The analysis is entirely theoretical, with explicit payoff formulas and equilibrium characterizations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling or increasing punishment in a two-player, public-goods-like game will increase efficiency, provided the punishment is sufficiently strong relative to the cost of cooperation. The analytic results specify the threshold at which punishment changes the equilibrium from defection to cooperation. While the model is for two players and not a standard PGG, the structure is closely related, and the findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in small-group cooperation games. The results are most applicable when the punishment mechanism is salient, effective, and the cost/benefit structure is similar to the model.

- source: KANDORI, M. (1992). SOCIAL NORMS AND COMMUNITY ENFORCEMENT. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that informal sanctions (punishment by community members) can support efficient outcomes in repeated matching games, provided there is sufficient information transmission (public observability or decentralized labels/reputation). Under public observability, any efficient and individually rational outcome can be sustained as an equilibrium, regardless of population size or matching rule. With only private experience and no information processing, cooperation (and thus efficiency) can sometimes be sustained via a 'contagious equilibrium,' but this is fragile and breaks down in large populations. Introducing local information processing (labels/reputation) allows for robust, straightforward equilibria that sustain efficiency for any population size and matching rule. The paper provides explicit equilibrium constructions and conditions for when these results hold.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that the presence of a punishment mechanism, when combined with sufficient information transmission (public observability or decentralized reputation/labels), enables the group to achieve efficient outcomes in repeated matching games, even with random matching and large populations. For the prediction task, this suggests that enabling punishment will increase efficiency to the maximum feasible level, provided the game design allows for sufficient information about past actions (e.g., public histories, visible labels, or reputation). If information is very limited (players only know their own history), punishment is less effective, and efficiency gains are limited, especially as group size increases. The paper does not provide empirical effect sizes but gives clear qualitative predictions about the moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency.

- source: Hopfensitz, A., & Reuben, E. (2009). The Importance of Emotions for the Effectiveness of Social Punishment*. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that allowing punishment in a sequential social dilemma (trust-like game) increases cooperation and the amount returned by second movers, compared to a baseline without punishment. However, punishment also triggers retaliation, leading to multiple rounds of costly punishment and counter-punishment. This escalation increases the cost of enforcing cooperation and can destroy resources, sometimes offsetting the gains from increased cooperation. The emotional mechanisms underlying these behaviors are explored: anger motivates punishment and retaliation, while guilt and shame can inhibit retaliation and promote pro-social adjustment. The presence of punishment opportunities does not guarantee higher group earnings or efficiency, as the costs of punishment and retaliation can outweigh the benefits of increased cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that, in games where punishment is possible and both parties can retaliate, the effect of punishment on efficiency is ambiguous: while punishment can increase cooperation, the associated costs (especially from retaliation) can reduce or even negate efficiency gains. For prediction tasks, this means that the presence of punishment does not always lead to higher efficiency, especially in settings where retaliation is possible and costly. The design dimensions most relevant here are the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the possibility of retaliation, and the structure of the game (sequential, two-player, not a standard PGG). Control efficiency (without punishment) is higher in the absence of costly conflict, but may be lower due to less cooperation. When punishment is enabled, efficiency may increase or decrease depending on the balance between increased cooperation and resource destruction from punishment cycles.

- source: Fehr, E., & Fischbacher, U. (2004). Third-party punishment and social norms. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that third-party punishment is common and increases with the severity of norm violations in both dictator games (distribution norm) and prisoner's dilemma games (cooperation norm). However, the magnitude of third-party punishment is lower than that of second-party punishment. In dictator games, third-party punishment reduces but does not eliminate the profitability of unfair transfers, while second-party punishment makes unfair transfers unprofitable. In prisoner's dilemma games, third-party punishment reduces the gains from defection but does not make cooperation strictly dominant, whereas second-party punishment does. Thus, punishment mechanisms—especially when strong or when multiple punishers are present—can increase efficiency by reducing the incentive to violate norms, but the effect is stronger for second-party punishment. The paper does not directly report group efficiency as a percentage of the maximum, but provides enough data to infer that punishment increases efficiency-related outcomes by reducing the profitability of norm violations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment (especially second-party, but also third-party) increases the cost of norm violations and can shift incentives toward more efficient, norm-compliant outcomes. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling punishment (with sufficient strength or multiple punishers) will increase efficiency or group payoff relative to control conditions without punishment, but the effect size depends on the punishment's strength and whether it is second- or third-party. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the punishment cost/impact ratio, the number of punishers, and whether punishment is second- or third-party. The results are most directly applicable to dictator and prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent to but not exact public goods games. The paper does not provide direct evidence for standard PGGs, but the mechanisms and patterns are likely to generalize to PGG-like environments with similar punishment structures.

- source: Fehr, E., & Rockenbach, B. (2003). Detrimental effects of sanctions on human altruism. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a one-shot sequential trust game, allowing the investor to impose a fine on the trustee for insufficient back-transfer leads to lower back-transfers from trustees and lower payoffs for investors, compared to both a baseline with no sanctions and to cases where the investor refrains from using the fine. The negative effect of sanctions is especially pronounced when the investor's desired back-transfer is high (i.e., when the sanction is used to enforce an unfair distribution). The paper interprets this as evidence that sanctions perceived as self-interested or unfair undermine altruistic cooperation, and thus can reduce overall efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a sequential trust game (not a standard PGG), enabling punishment (sanctions) can reduce efficiency-related outcomes (investor payoff, group surplus) compared to a no-punishment baseline, especially when punishment is perceived as self-interested or unfair. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is context-dependent: in trust games with one-shot interactions and potential for perceived unfairness, punishment may reduce efficiency. The findings are less directly transferable to standard PGGs, but highlight the importance of the perceived legitimacy and purpose of punishment as a moderator.

- source: Fehr, E., Hoff, K., & Kshetramade, M. (2008). Spite and development. *American Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper documents widespread spiteful punishment in a one-shot trust game with third-party punishment in rural India: 61–73% of third parties punished cooperators, not just defectors. This antisocial punishment reduces the expected returns to cooperation and undermines the efficiency benefits of punishment. In a repeated stag hunt game, high-caste pairs (who are more spiteful) were much less likely to coordinate on the efficient, high-payoff equilibrium than low-caste pairs. Dictator games show that high-caste subjects are more likely to engage in payoff-reducing, spiteful choices. The findings suggest that in environments where spiteful preferences are prevalent, punishment opportunities may fail to increase, or may even decrease, group efficiency and welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is context-dependent: when antisocial or spiteful punishment is common, punishment can reduce or fail to improve efficiency, even in games structurally similar to public goods or trust games. The presence of spiteful preferences, as measured by the frequency of punishment of cooperators and by dictator game choices, is a key moderator. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in settings with high antisocial punishment or spite, enabling punishment may not increase efficiency and may even decrease it, especially if punishment is not tightly targeted to defectors. Game design dimensions that allow for antisocial punishment (e.g., anonymity, lack of accountability, or cultural context) should be considered as risk factors for negative efficiency effects.

- source: Rand, D. G., Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2009). Direct reciprocity with costly punishment: Generous tit-for-tat prevails. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper shows that in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with costly punishment, the addition of a punishment option does not increase cooperation or group payoffs in evolutionary simulations. Even when Nash equilibrium analysis allows for cooperative equilibria using punishment, evolutionary selection in finite populations favors generous tit-for-tat strategies that do not use costly punishment. Costly punishment is rarely used, and when it is, it is associated with lower payoffs. The presence of costly punishment does not allow cooperation to evolve in parameter regions where it would not already be supported by direct reciprocity alone. These findings are robust to variations in game parameters and are supported by experimental data from Dreber et al. (2008).
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper suggests that enabling costly punishment in a repeated two-player PD-like environment does not increase efficiency or group payoff, regardless of the punishment cost or effectiveness. Evolutionary dynamics select against strategies that use costly punishment, and the most successful strategies are those that reciprocate cooperation and defect against defectors (generous tit-for-tat), not those that punish. Thus, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar repeated games, one should not expect an increase in efficiency or group payoff, and may even expect a negative association between punishment use and payoff.
