  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that dynamic partner updating—where players can choose to make or break ties with others—substantially increases both cooperation rates and average payoffs in iterated prisoner's dilemma games, compared to static networks. Even low rates of partner updating are sufficient to produce large positive effects. The effect is concave: initial increases in update rate have large effects, but further increases yield smaller gains. When the payoff matrix is modified so that maintaining ties with defectors is more costly, cooperation is even higher and more sustained, as players are more willing to sever ties with defectors. The main mechanism is assortative mixing: cooperators preferentially connect with other cooperators and avoid defectors, rather than punishing defectors directly. The results suggest that dynamic partner selection can substitute for explicit punishment or reward mechanisms in promoting cooperation and increasing group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling dynamic partner updating (i.e., endogenous, bilateral partner choice and unilateral link deletion) in repeated social dilemma games can dramatically increase group efficiency (average payoff relative to the cooperative optimum), even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The effect is robust across a range of update rates and is further enhanced when the cost of interacting with defectors is increased. For prediction tasks, the presence of dynamic partner updating should be treated as a strong positive moderator of efficiency, especially when players can freely choose partners and sever ties. However, the game is an iterated prisoner's dilemma on a dynamic network, not a standard public goods game, and the 'punishment' is implicit (via partner selection) rather than explicit. Thus, while the findings are highly informative for environments with endogenous partner choice, they are only adjacent to explicit punishment in PGGs. The results suggest that, in settings where partner choice is possible, explicit punishment may be less necessary for achieving high efficiency.

- source: Brandt, H., & Sigmund, K. (2004). The logic of reprobation: assessment and action rules for indirect reciprocation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The study shows that indirect reciprocity models with assessment rules that distinguish justified from unjustified defections (STANDING) are more effective at sustaining cooperation and suppressing defectors than simple scoring rules, especially in the presence of errors. However, even simple scoring can support cooperation if the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and players have enough interactions. The model demonstrates that the structure of the population, the number of observed interactions, and the error rates in action and perception all influence the stability of cooperation. Analytical results show that, under certain assumptions about the distribution of the number of rounds, stable mixtures of discriminating and non-discriminating altruists can exist, leading to high average payoffs. The findings suggest that costly punishment (refusing to help) can sustain cooperation, but is less robust than non-costly forms (STANDING).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment-like mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation (and thus efficiency) depends on whether punishment is costly, the benefit-to-cost ratio, error rates, and the number of interactions. While the model is not a standard PGG, the parallels are strong: refusing to help is analogous to punishing defectors, and the group payoff is analogous to efficiency. The results imply that costly punishment can increase efficiency, but is less robust than non-costly punishment, and that the details of the assessment and action rules, as well as the game structure (e.g., number of rounds, observation probability), are important moderators. For prediction, the paper supports the view that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and errors are not too frequent, but the effect is sensitive to the costliness and implementation of punishment.

- source: Aktipis, C. A. (2004). Know when to walk away: contingent movement and the evolution of cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that a simple contingent movement rule ('Walk Away') enables cooperation to evolve and persist in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments, outperforming more cognitively complex strategies like TFT and PAVLOV under many conditions. Walk Away is robust to invasion, can invade populations of defectors at lower initial frequencies than other strategies, and is most successful when movement costs, error rates, and agent density are low. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and outcomes are measured by the evolutionary success of strategies (frequency in the population), not by direct group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that contingent movement (exit) can substitute for more complex memory-based or punishment-based strategies in promoting cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It does not study public goods games or explicit punishment, but shows that contingent movement (exit after defection) can promote cooperation and evolutionary success in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments. The findings suggest that mechanisms allowing agents to avoid defectors (analogous to exit or partner choice) can be highly effective, even without explicit punishment or memory. However, because the model does not report group efficiency or total payoff, and does not manipulate punishment cost or technology, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in public goods games with punishment is limited. The results are most relevant for understanding the role of partner choice, mobility, and spatial structure as alternatives or complements to punishment.

- source: Rustagi, D., Engel, S., & Kosfeld, M. (2010). Conditional Cooperation and Costly Monitoring Explain Success in Forest Commons Management. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that (1) groups with a higher share of conditional cooperators have significantly better forest management outcomes, (2) costly monitoring (enforcement) is more prevalent in groups with more conditional cooperators, and (3) both conditional cooperation and costly monitoring are important for successful commons management. The main outcome is a real-world efficiency proxy (forest management success), not laboratory earnings. The public goods game is used to measure behavioral types, not as the main outcome. The study does not experimentally manipulate punishment or monitoring, but observes natural variation in monitoring (enforcement) and links it to outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that costly monitoring (enforcement) is associated with higher group-level efficiency in a real-world commons context, and that the presence of conditional cooperators amplifies this effect. While the study does not manipulate punishment in a controlled PGG, it supports the idea that enabling or increasing costly enforcement (monitoring and punishment) can improve group efficiency, especially when conditional cooperators are present. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence and intensity of enforcement mechanisms (even if costly) are likely to increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, particularly in groups with more conditional cooperators. However, the evidence is observational and context-specific (forest management), not from a lab PGG with toggled punishment.

- source: Wang, R. W., Sun, B. F., Zheng, Q., Shi, L., & Zhu, L. X. (2011). Asymmetric interaction and indeterminate fitness correlation between cooperative partners in the fig-fig wasp mutualism. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The study finds that asymmetric interference competition among cooperative actors (wasps) can stabilize cooperation by keeping resource use unsaturated, leading to positive fitness correlations (mutual benefit) between partners. When interference competition is prevented (sequential entry), or when wasp oviposition efficiency is high (e.g., in cold season), the system can shift to negative fitness correlation (conflict), indicating overexploitation and reduced group efficiency. The relationship between cooperation and efficiency is not fixed but varies stochastically with environmental and experimental factors. The paper also discusses the role of discriminative sanctions (host reducing egg deposition efficiency of less-cooperative wasps) and rewards in maintaining cooperation, but these are not directly manipulated as in PGG punishment/reward treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. It shows that mechanisms analogous to punishment (sanctions by the host) and interference competition among cooperators can maintain or undermine group efficiency, depending on ecological context and the structure of interaction. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms on efficiency is highly sensitive to the details of resource competition, the number of actors, and environmental conditions. However, the system studied is a biological mutualism, not a PGG or direct human social dilemma, and the 'punishment' is not implemented as a discrete, costly action by peers but as host-imposed sanctions. Thus, while the results are adjacent and informative, they should be transferred to PGG predictions with caution, especially regarding the direct effect of enabling punishment on efficiency.

- source: Abbink, K., & Sadrieh, A. (2009). The pleasure of being nasty. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that a substantial fraction of subjects are willing to destroy the other player's endowment when their actions are hidden, but not when they are observable. The presence of anonymity increases anti-social behavior. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and is not a public goods or punishment game. The findings are relevant for understanding anti-social punishment and spite, but do not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The game studied is a two-player, anti-social destruction game with no cooperation or group payoff structure. The main insight is that anonymity increases anti-social behavior, but this is not directly transferable to the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The findings may be contextually relevant for understanding anti-social punishment or spiteful behavior, but do not inform the prediction task in a quantitative or directly comparable way.

- source: Xiao, E., & Houser, D. (2009). Avoiding the sharp tongue: Anticipated written messages promote fair economic exchange. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The opportunity for receivers to send ex post written messages (expressing disapproval or emotion) in a one-shot dictator game leads to a statistically significant reduction in the frequency of maximally selfish (10%) offers by dictators, compared to the standard dictator game. However, the frequency of fair (50/50) offers is not significantly increased. The effect of non-monetary sanctions (messages) is smaller than that of monetary punishment (rejection) in ultimatum games, which more strongly reduce selfish offers but at the cost of efficiency due to destroyed surplus. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, as the dictator game does not involve group production or a public good.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of informal, non-monetary sanctions (written messages) on selfishness in a simple allocation game, but not in a public goods or group production context. It shows that non-monetary sanctions can reduce selfish behavior, but less so than monetary punishment. However, because the game is a one-shot dictator game (not a repeated or multi-player public goods game), and efficiency is not measured, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that in environments where only non-monetary sanctions are possible, some reduction in selfishness may occur, but the effect on efficiency in public goods games cannot be directly inferred from this study.

- source: Van Lange, P. A. M., Klapwijk, A., & Van Munster, L. M. (2011). How the shadow of the future might promote cooperation. *Group Processes & Intergroup Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the mere anticipation of repeated interaction (10 rounds vs. 1 round) increases cooperation (measured as coins given to the partner) among individualists, but not among prosocials, in a sequential give-some dilemma. Prosocials maintain high cooperation regardless of repetition, while individualists reciprocate more when repeated interaction is expected. The effect is robust to the presence or absence of 'noise' in the translation of choices. The study does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that repeated interaction (longer games) can increase cooperation among individualistically oriented participants, even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. However, it does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not manipulate or include punishment, so its relevance to the prediction of efficiency under punishment is limited. The main implication is that repeated interaction may substitute for some effects of punishment among individualists, but this is not tested in a payoff or efficiency framework.

- source: Jensen, K., Call, J., & Tomasello, M. (2007). Chimpanzees are rational maximizers in an ultimatum game. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Chimpanzees, when tested in a mini-ultimatum game, do not systematically reject unfair offers, even when a fair alternative is available to the proposer. Proposers do not make fair offers, and responders accept almost all nonzero offers, only reliably rejecting offers where they receive nothing. This contrasts with human behavior, where unfair offers are often rejected as a form of costly punishment. The findings suggest that chimpanzees are rational maximizers and do not engage in costly punishment to enforce fairness. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on behavioral responses to fairness and punishment opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies a mini-ultimatum game with chimpanzees and focuses on behavioral outcomes (offer choices, rejection rates) rather than efficiency or group payoff. However, it is informative in showing that, in the absence of a willingness to punish unfairness (i.e., when punishment is not exercised), there is no mechanism to enforce fair or cooperative outcomes. This suggests that, in environments where punishment is not used or is not costly, efficiency gains from punishment are unlikely. The findings are adjacent to the prediction task and may inform models about the necessity of actual punishment behavior for efficiency effects.

- source: Falk, A., & Fischbacher, U. (2002). Crime in the lab-detecting social interaction. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates strong social interaction effects: subjects' willingness to steal increases with the observed or expected stealing of others. About half of the subjects are 'reciprocal' (conditional) types, while the rest are mostly selfish (always steal the maximum). The fraction of selfish types is higher when stealing is a pure transfer (no efficiency loss) than when it is inefficient. The study does not include a punishment or sanctioning phase, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. The findings are relevant for understanding norm compliance and conditional cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on the effects of punishment or on efficiency outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, provide informative context about the importance of social interaction and conditional norm violation: when individuals see more norm violation (stealing), they are more likely to violate norms themselves. The manipulation of the efficiency consequences of stealing (gamma) shows that more selfish behavior occurs when norm violation is less costly to the group, which may be relevant for understanding the impact of efficiency parameters in related games. However, because there is no punishment mechanism and no direct efficiency or payoff analysis, this paper should be considered only as indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Rilling, J. K., Goldsmith, D. R., Glenn, A. L., Jairam, M. R., Elfenbein, H. A., Dagenais, J. E., Murdock, C. D., & Pagnoni, G. (2008). The neural correlates of the affective response to unreciprocated cooperation. *Neuropsychologia*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that unreciprocated cooperation (being defected on after cooperating) in the iterated Prisoner's Dilemma elicits strong negative emotions and increased neural activity in regions associated with aversive and social pain (anterior insula, hippocampus, amygdala). These neural responses are stronger when the unreciprocated cooperation follows a cooperative move by the subject, suggesting sensitivity to perceived partner intent. Functional connectivity between the anterior insula and lateral orbitofrontal cortex predicts whether the subject will defect in the next round. The paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods or Prisoner's Dilemma games. It does, however, provide evidence that unreciprocated cooperation (partner defection) triggers strong negative affect and neural responses that bias subjects toward defection in subsequent rounds. This suggests a psychological mechanism by which cooperation can unravel in the absence of punishment or sanctioning. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the idea that, without punishment, negative experiences with defectors can lead to reduced cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or comparative data on efficiency or payoff, nor does it test the effect of introducing punishment.

- source: Ligon, E., & Schechter, L. (2012). Motives for sharing in social networks. *Journal of Development Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds high baseline sharing in anonymous, random-recipient dictator games, with small but significant increases in sharing when the dictator can choose the recipient or when the dictator's identity is revealed. The decomposition shows that preference-based motives (baseline, directed altruism) account for most mean sharing, but incentive-based motives (sanctions, reciprocity) explain a large share of the variance in sharing and are strongly associated with real-world gift-giving and social network connectedness. The effect of revealing identity (potential for social sanctions or reciprocity) is modest on average but important for explaining individual differences. The study does not measure efficiency or group payoff, and the game is not a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is a set of dictator game variants, not a PGG or linear public goods game, and there is no explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanism—only the possibility of social sanctions or reciprocity inferred from information structure. The findings suggest that the potential for social sanctions or reciprocity can modestly increase sharing, but the context and outcome measures are not directly transferable to PGG efficiency predictions. The results may be useful for understanding how information and recipient choice affect sharing motives, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Seinen, I., & Schram, A. (2006). Social status and group norms: Indirect reciprocity in a repeated helping experiment. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds strong evidence for indirect reciprocity: donors are significantly more likely to help recipients who have helped others in the past, as measured by the recipient's social status. When information about social status is available, cooperation rates are much higher (e.g., 70% in high-cost/information vs. 22% in high-cost/no-information). Individual strategies often use cut-points based on the recipient's or donor's own social status, and group-level norms about the minimum acceptable status to be helped emerge and are stable within groups but differ across groups. The paper does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff, but does provide some expected return calculations for different strategies. There is no explicit punishment mechanism; 'punishment' is indirect, in the sense that donors withhold help from low-status recipients.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the effects of information and social norms on cooperation in repeated helping games, which are adjacent to public goods games. The design does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, but the withholding of help from low-status individuals functions as an indirect sanction. The main outcome is the rate of helping (cooperation), not efficiency or group payoff, though some expected returns are calculated. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that information about others' past behavior and the emergence of group norms can sustain high cooperation rates, but does not provide direct evidence about the efficiency effects of explicit punishment mechanisms. The design dimensions most relevant here are information availability, cost/benefit structure, and group size, but the absence of explicit punishment and the focus on individual rather than group payoffs limit direct applicability.

- source: West, S. A., Gardner, A., Shuker, D. M., Reynolds, T., Burton-Chellow, M., Sykes, E. M., Guinnee, M. A., & Griffin, A. S. (2006). Cooperation and the scale of competition in humans. *Current Biology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that human participants cooperate more frequently in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games when rewards are based on global competition (top scorers in the whole class) than when rewards are based on local competition (top scorer in each small group). This effect is robust across individuals, groups, and classes. The experiment does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and the main outcome is the proportion of cooperative choices, not efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the scale of competition (local vs global) can strongly affect cooperation rates in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games, which are adjacent to public goods games. However, it does not manipulate or include punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Therefore, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but it does highlight the importance of competitive structure as a moderator of cooperation. The findings suggest that contextual factors like competition scale may interact with other mechanisms (such as punishment) in affecting cooperation, but no direct evidence is provided for punishment or efficiency outcomes.

- source: Wardil, L., & da Silva, J. K. L. (2009). Adoption of simultaneous different strategies against different opponents enhances cooperation. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to adopt different strategies against different opponents, combined with a 'replace the worst confrontation' update rule (model A), leads to the dominance or persistence of cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games on both square lattices and rings. This mechanism acts as a form of punishment by neutralizing exploiters, but does not impose a cost on the punisher. The fraction of cooperation remains high for a wide range of defection temptations and is robust to some misjudgment. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its relevance is limited to the insight that certain update rules that allow targeted responses to defectors can sustain high cooperation rates, even without explicit costly punishment. However, because the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a PGG), and the outcome is cooperation rate (not efficiency or payoff), its transfer value to the prediction task is limited and indirect. The paper does not report or analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so it cannot be used to calibrate efficiency predictions for PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Yamagishi, T., Terai, S., Kiyonari, T., Mifune, N., & Kanazawa, S. (2007). The social exchange heuristic - Managing errors in social exchange. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in one-shot PDGs is significantly higher when participants perceive themselves to be in an ongoing social exchange relationship (i.e., when the SEH is activated) compared to when they do not. In Experiment 1, participants who deliberated about the game and their partner before being assigned to a specific exchange relationship (delayed-play) cooperated less than those who made decisions immediately after being assigned a partner (immediate-play). In Experiment 2, participants who made their contribution decision after being told they were matched with a specific partner (partner-specified) contributed more than those who made their decision before being matched (partner-unspecified). The study attributes these effects to the activation of the SEH, which frames the game as an Assurance Game and increases conditional cooperation. No punishment, sanctioning, or efficiency outcomes are studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or group payoff/efficiency. However, it does provide informative evidence that cues of social exchange and perceived interdependence can substantially increase cooperation rates in dyadic social dilemmas. For prediction tasks, this suggests that psychological framing and the presence of cues indicating ongoing relationships may be important moderators of cooperation, but the paper does not address punishment or efficiency directly.

- source: Fu, F., Chen, X. J., Liu, L. G., & Wang, L. (2007). Promotion of cooperation induced by the interplay between structure and game dynamics. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that allowing agents to adapt their social ties by breaking links with the most frequent defectors (after a multi-round PDG) and rewiring to others can promote the emergence and dominance of cooperation in the population. The interplay between network structure adaptation and strategy evolution is key: there is a critical rate of network adaptation above which cooperators can dominate. The model explores how parameters such as temptation to defect, number of rounds, average connectivity, and selection intensity affect the prevalence of cooperation. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates and network properties, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its model is adjacent: it studies a networked PDG with social tie breaking as a form of sanction, but does not include explicit costly punishment or measure efficiency/welfare. The findings suggest that adaptive social structure (link breaking as a response to defection) can promote cooperation, but the results are not directly transferable to standard PGGs with explicit punishment and efficiency outcomes. Use this paper as weak, indirect support for the idea that social sanctions (even non-costly ones) can promote cooperation, but do not use it for quantitative prediction of efficiency effects.

- source: BIEL, A., & GARLING, T. (1995). THE ROLE OF UNCERTAINTY IN RESOURCE DILEMMAS. *Journal of Environmental Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists
  findings: The main finding is that a reputation mechanism that rewards/punishes based on the number of consecutive rounds a player has cooperated or defected can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial PDG. Higher reputation factors and higher upper bounds on consistent strategy times both further promote cooperation. The mechanism is more effective and leads to faster stabilization of cooperation than traditional reputation mechanisms. However, the paper does not analyze or report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation-based punishment/reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial PDG environments, and that the strength and structure of the reputation mechanism matter. However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. Its relevance is further limited by the use of a spatial PDG rather than a PGG or direct payoff-based analysis. It may be useful for mechanism design intuition, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Page, K. M., Nowak, M. A., & Sigmund, K. (2000). The spatial ultimatum game. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that spatial structure in the ultimatum game leads to the evolution of fairer offers and higher acceptance thresholds compared to non-spatial (random encounter) settings. In well-mixed populations, average offers and acceptance levels are low, approaching zero as mutation error decreases. In spatially structured populations (1D and 2D lattices), average offers and acceptance levels are much higher, approaching a fair split in some cases. The paper interprets rejection of low offers as a punishment mechanism that, when combined with spatial structure, supports the evolution of fairness. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to payoff-based prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that spatial structure and local competition can support the evolution of fairer, more cooperative behavior in bargaining games where punishment is endogenous (via rejection). However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of spatial structure and local interactions for the evolution of fairness and the effectiveness of endogenous punishment mechanisms.

- source: Sinatra, R., Iranzo, J., Gómez-Gardeñes, J., Floría, L. M., Latora, V., & Moreno, Y. (2009). The Ultimatum Game in complex networks. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The main findings are that network topology and the type of evolutionary update rule strongly influence the distribution of offers and acceptance thresholds in the Ultimatum Game. Under natural selection, strategies converge toward fair offers (around 50%) in both homogeneous and heterogeneous networks, but scale-free networks allow for a broader range of surviving strategies due to the influence of hubs. Under the social penalty rule, which penalizes the lowest-payoff player and its neighbors, scale-free networks promote polarization: hubs can afford to be highly generous, while low-degree nodes may be more selfish. When players can independently choose offers and thresholds, the population tends to self-organize into pragmatic (role-ignoring) strategies, especially under social penalty. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency or total payoff, so the implications for efficiency are not directly addressed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies the Ultimatum Game and focuses on the evolution of behavioral strategies rather than payoff or efficiency outcomes. The 'social penalty' mechanism is a form of network-level selection rather than direct costly punishment. While the findings may be informative about how network structure and update rules affect the evolution of cooperation and fairness-related behaviors, they do not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency changes due to punishment in PGGs. Therefore, this paper should be considered only as weak, adjacent evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Bshary, R., & Grutter, A. S. (2006). Image scoring and cooperation in a cleaner fish mutualism. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The study shows that client fish preferentially associate with cleaner fish that are observed to behave cooperatively, providing evidence for image scoring in a non-human animal. Cleaner fish, in turn, behave more cooperatively (feed against their preference) when their actions are being observed by a potential client (image-scoring scenario) compared to when they are not observed. This supports the hypothesis that reputation-based partner choice can promote cooperation. However, the study does not measure group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and the experimental setup is not a public goods game but an asymmetric mutualism with partner choice.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation mechanisms (image scoring) can increase cooperative behavior in asymmetric mutualisms, which may be relevant for understanding the effects of reputation or partner choice in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their close variants. The findings may inform predictions about the role of observation and reputation in promoting cooperation, but not about the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Bandiera, O., Barankay, I., & Rasul, I. (2005). Cooperation in collective action. *Economics of Transition*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that in a workplace setting with relative incentives (where individual effort imposes a negative externality on co-workers), workers cooperate more (i.e., reduce their productivity) when in smaller groups, with a higher share of co-nationals, with more homogeneous ability, and with more co-workers who have more to lose from shirking. These group characteristics do not affect productivity under piece rates, where there is no incentive to cooperate. The literature review highlights that institutions enabling monitoring, communication, and punishment can increase cooperation in public goods and common pool resource settings, but monetary punishment often reduces group welfare due to its cost. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment and group characteristics on cooperation in collective action settings, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes. The empirical results are most relevant for understanding how group size, heterogeneity, and incentive structure affect cooperation (proxied by productivity), rather than efficiency. The literature review suggests that punishment can increase cooperation but may reduce efficiency due to its cost, and that communication and monitoring are important moderators. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the paper's findings should be used as indirect signals about the likely direction of cooperation and the possible tradeoff between increased cooperation and the cost of punishment.

- source: Bshary, R., Grutter, A. S., Willener, A. S. T., & Leimar, O. (2008). Pairs of cooperating cleaner fish provide better service quality than singletons. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: Pairs of cleaner fish provide higher service quality to clients than singletons, mainly because females behave more cooperatively in pairs. Theoretical modeling shows that cooperation in pairs can yield higher service quality if there is added search efficiency, and empirical data (field and lab) support this. Aggression by males toward cheating females may function as a form of punishment, but this is not systematically manipulated or measured as a treatment. The outcomes are behavioral (cheating, cooperation, aggression), not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that, in a biological system with prisoner's dilemma-like structure, the presence of a partner (pair vs singleton) can increase cooperative behavior and service quality, especially when there is some mechanism for partner control (e.g., aggression/punishment). However, the study does not use a public goods game, does not manipulate punishment as a treatment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff. Thus, it is only adjacent to the prediction task and should be used as weak, indirect support for the idea that social context and partner control can moderate cooperation and service quality.

- source: Gardner, A., West, S. A., & Barton, N. H. (2007). The relation between multilocus population genetics and social evolution theory. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that multilocus population genetics provides a general framework for modeling the evolution of social behaviors, including cooperation and punishment. In a simple two-locus model (one for cooperation, one for punishment), it finds that cooperation and punishment cannot invade unless cooperation is already favored by relatedness (i.e., punishment does not expand the parameter space for cooperation). Moreover, increased relatedness directly selects against punishment, as punishment is mutually costly. The only benefit to punishment comes from its association with cooperation, but this is insufficient to overcome the direct costs. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on evolutionary stability and gene frequency change.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding the evolutionary logic of cooperation and punishment, and suggest that costly punishment may not increase cooperation or group benefit except under restrictive conditions. However, because it does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it should not be used as a primary source for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in experimental public goods games. It may be useful for mechanistic or theoretical context, especially regarding the evolutionary logic of punishment and cooperation.

- source: Falk, A., & Fischbacher, U. (2006). A theory of reciprocity. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper develops a psychological game-theoretic model of reciprocity, where individuals' utility depends on both material payoffs and the perceived kindness or unkindness of others' actions, which in turn depends on both outcomes and intentions. The model explains why people sometimes punish defectors or reward cooperators, even at a cost to themselves, and why the same material outcome can elicit different responses depending on the perceived intention behind it. The theory is shown to be consistent with a range of experimental findings, such as higher rejection rates for unfair offers in the ultimatum game, positive wage-effort relationships in gift-exchange, and conditional cooperation in public goods games. However, the paper does not provide new empirical data or direct predictions about efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical framework for understanding when and why individuals will punish or reward others in social dilemmas, including public goods games. It highlights the importance of both outcomes and intentions in driving reciprocal behavior. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions or empirical estimates of how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper offers conceptual guidance on the psychological mechanisms underlying punishment, but does not supply direct evidence or parameter values for efficiency outcomes. Its value is mainly in informing the types of behavioral responses to expect under different game structures, especially regarding the role of intentions and available alternatives.

- source: Giardini, F., & Conte, R. (2012). Gossip for social control in natural and artificial societies. *Simulation-transactions of the Society for Modeling and Simulation International*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that gossip, understood as the spread of reputation (meta-evaluations with hidden sources), enables more efficient social control by reducing the costs and risks (especially retaliation) associated with punishing or excluding cheaters. Ethnographic evidence shows that gossip is structured to avoid direct responsibility and retaliation, while agent-based simulations demonstrate that reputation-based information exchange can maintain higher product quality and more robust partner selection even with many cheaters. However, these findings are in the context of market-like artificial societies and do not directly address efficiency or payoff in public goods games with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence that decentralized, reputation-based information exchange (gossip) can improve the effectiveness and reduce the costs of social control mechanisms, such as punishment or exclusion, in environments where cheating is possible. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. The agent-based simulation results suggest that reputation mechanisms can sustain higher system performance (e.g., product quality) under high cheating rates, but these are not directly translatable to PGG efficiency outcomes. Thus, the paper may inform mechanistic reasoning about the role of information and retaliation risk in punishment systems, but does not provide parameter-level evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Taylor, C., & Nowak, M. A. (2007). Transforming the dilemma. *Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper provides a unified framework for understanding how different mechanisms (direct reciprocity, indirect reciprocity, kin selection, group selection, network reciprocity) transform the payoff structure of the Prisoner's Dilemma to allow for the evolution of cooperation. For each mechanism, it derives the threshold conditions under which cooperation can be stable or invade. The analysis is mathematical and does not include empirical or simulation results. Punishment is discussed only as an extension, not as a primary focus, and is said to be subsumed under direct or indirect reciprocity. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment on these outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is a theoretical synthesis of mechanisms for cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma, with only a brief, conceptual discussion of punishment. The paper does not analyze or model the effect of punishment on group payoff, efficiency, or related outcomes, and does not provide parameterized results for game design dimensions relevant to the prediction task. Its main value is in clarifying that punishment is not a separate mechanism but an extension of reciprocity, and that efficiency or payoff outcomes are not directly addressed.

- source: West, S. A., Griffin, A. S., & Gardner, A. (2007). Social semantics: altruism, cooperation, mutualism, strong reciprocity and group selection. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that much confusion in the literature on cooperation, altruism, and punishment arises from inconsistent or ambiguous use of key terms. It clarifies that in many models (including public goods games), behaviors labeled as 'altruistic' or 'strong reciprocity' may actually be mutually beneficial, providing direct fitness benefits to the actor as well as to the group. Punishment, often called 'altruistic punishment,' can also be selfish or spiteful, depending on the context. The paper argues that clear definitions and careful distinction between proximate and ultimate explanations are essential for progress in the field. It also notes that the group selection and kin selection frameworks are mathematically equivalent in most cases relevant to cooperation and public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it offers important theoretical context: it cautions that the effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency depends on the underlying fitness consequences and the structure of the game. The paper suggests that punishment can increase cooperation and group benefit if it provides a direct or indirect fitness advantage, but the terminology used in the literature may obscure whether the effect is truly altruistic or mutually beneficial. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the need for precise definitions of efficiency and careful mapping of model assumptions to experimental designs. It also highlights that the effect of punishment may depend on whether it is costly, whether it increases group survival or productivity, and how benefits are distributed.

- source: Hill, K. (2002). Altruistic cooperation during foraging by the ache, and the evolved human predisposition to cooperate. *Human Nature-an Interdisciplinary Biosocial Perspective*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: Ache men and women spend about 10% of their foraging time in cooperative acts that primarily benefit others, with some days exceeding 50%. Most cooperation is directed toward helping others acquire game, and the acts are often short-term altruistic. Cooperation is not limited to kin and is linked to widespread food sharing. The paper argues that such cooperation and sharing are part of an evolved human predisposition, and draws parallels to findings from experimental economics (including public goods games with punishment), but does not itself test or manipulate punishment, nor does it measure efficiency or group payoff in a controlled game-theoretic sense.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is an observational ethnographic study of real-world foraging cooperation, not an experimental manipulation of game design dimensions. While it discusses the importance of cooperation, sharing, and the possible role of punishment in maintaining social norms, it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment interventions. Its main value is in providing context for the prevalence and forms of cooperation in small-scale societies, and in supporting the plausibility of mechanisms (such as contingent sharing and informal sanctions) that are modeled in public goods game experiments.

- source: MOLM, L. D. (1994). DEPENDENCE AND RISK - TRANSFORMING AND STRUCTURE OF SOCIAL-EXCHANGE. *Social Psychology Quarterly*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the risk inherent in social exchange relations (mutual dependence) can be reduced by structuring transactions as negotiated (joint decision, binding agreements) or as serially dependent (allowing for contingent action and trust development). Negotiated transactions most directly transform dependence into interdependence, reducing risk and making outcomes more predictable. Serially dependent transactions (such as repeated reciprocal exchanges) also reduce risk, but less so, and rely more on emergent trust and norms. Generalized exchange (indirect reciprocity) is riskier and less amenable to these risk-reducing mechanisms. The paper suggests that these structural features influence the likelihood of stable cooperation, trust, and solidarity, but does not provide empirical evidence or direct predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how structural features of social exchange (e.g., repeated interaction, negotiation, direct vs. generalized exchange) may moderate the risk of defection and the emergence of cooperation. While it does not address public goods games or punishment directly, it suggests that mechanisms which increase interdependence (such as repeated play or enforceable agreements) should improve group outcomes and stability. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that game designs with more interdependence, repeated interaction, or enforceable agreements are likely to see higher efficiency or more stable cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence. The relevance to punishment is indirect: punishment is mentioned as a possible influence tactic in negotiation, but not as a central mechanism.

- source: McNamara, J. M., & Leimar, O. (2010). Variation and the response to variation as a basis for successful cooperation. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that variation among individuals in traits relevant to cooperation (such as cooperativeness, partner quality, and social sensitivity) fundamentally alters the evolutionary dynamics of cooperation. Variation can make it worthwhile for individuals to explore, negotiate, and respond contingently to others, which can stabilize or increase cooperation. Mechanisms such as partner choice, reputation, and negotiation are all promoted by variation. The paper also discusses how sanctions (including punishment, partner switching, and reduced investment) can penalize uncooperative individuals, but the focus is on the role of variation in enabling these mechanisms rather than on the direct effects of punishment on efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical or quantitative evidence to support prediction of efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It is primarily theoretical and focuses on the role of variation in the evolution of cooperation, with only indirect discussion of sanctions and payoff efficiency. It may inform high-level reasoning about the importance of trait variation, partner choice, and negotiation in shaping cooperation, but does not provide parameterized or dimension-specific evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in experimental game settings.

- source: HEINSOHN, R., & PACKER, C. (1995). COMPLEX COOPERATIVE STRATEGIES IN GROUP-TERRITORIAL AFRICAN LIONS. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The study finds that female lions show consistent individual differences in their willingness to participate in group territorial defense, with some acting as leaders and others as laggards. Laggards are recognized by leaders, who become more cautious in their presence but do not punish them or withdraw cooperation. Some laggards adjust their participation based on the odds of group success, while others do not. The diversity of strategies suggests that cooperation in large groups is more complex than predicted by simple reciprocity or punishment models. No evidence of punishment or sanctioning of laggards is found.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the maintenance of cooperation in group settings without formal punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It suggests that in naturalistic, high-stakes group conflicts, cooperation can persist with a mix of unconditional and conditional cooperators and laggards, and that punishment is not always used to enforce cooperation. However, because the study does not use a public goods game or measure efficiency/payoff outcomes, it offers only adjacent, qualitative insight for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. It highlights the possibility that cooperation can be stable without punishment, but does not provide quantitative or design-specific evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Boyd, R., & Richerson, P. J. (2009). Culture and the evolution of human cooperation. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The paper argues that human cooperation at large scales is best explained by cultural group selection, where rapid cultural adaptation leads to persistent differences between groups. These differences, when linked to group-beneficial norms (such as those enforced by punishment and reward systems), can spread through group competition, imitation of successful groups, and selective migration. The authors posit that moral systems enforced by sanctions and rewards increase group welfare and the prevalence of prosocial motives. However, the paper is theoretical and does not present empirical data or experimental results on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or similar environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for why punishment and reward systems may support the evolution and maintenance of cooperation and group-beneficial norms, potentially increasing group welfare. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence that can be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. Its value for prediction is mainly in supporting the plausibility of positive effects of sanctions on group-level outcomes, but it does not inform how specific game design dimensions or control efficiency moderate these effects.

- source: Mazar, N., & Ariely, D. (2006). Dishonesty in everyday life and its policy implications. *Journal of Public Policy & Marketing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that dishonesty is not solely determined by external incentives (such as probability and magnitude of punishment), but also by internal psychological reward mechanisms. It reviews evidence that increasing the probability or severity of punishment does not always reduce dishonest behavior, especially when internalized norms or self-awareness are strong. The authors propose that interventions increasing self-awareness or internalization of honesty norms can be more effective than increasing punishment. The paper references experimental studies on cheating in test settings, showing that the opportunity to cheat increases dishonesty, but only up to a point, and that self-awareness interventions (e.g., recalling the Ten Commandments, signing an honor code) can reduce cheating to control levels. The paper does not report on group efficiency or public goods game outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for why punishment may not always increase efficiency or reduce norm violations in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by internal psychological factors, such as internalized norms and self-awareness. However, it does not provide empirical estimates or direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment effectiveness may depend on context and psychological factors not captured by game design dimensions alone. It is not directly informative for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

The value and stability of leadership depend on the structure of the underlying coordination problem, the information available, and the costs of changing leaders. - source: Tyler, T. R. (2002). Leadership and cooperation in groups. *American Behavioral Scientist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that both incentives (rewards) and sanctions (punishments) can influence cooperative behavior in groups, but their effects are typically small. Internal motivations—such as intrinsic interest, group identification, morality, and legitimacy—are more effective and sustainable in promoting cooperation. The use of sanctions or incentives may undermine intrinsic motivation in the long run. The paper highlights the importance of procedural justice and legitimacy in fostering voluntary cooperation. No direct findings are reported on efficiency or group payoff, and the discussion is conceptual rather than quantitative or experimental.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual background on the limited effectiveness of punishment and incentives in promoting cooperation, suggesting that their impact on efficiency or group payoff is likely to be modest in most group settings. It emphasizes that internal motivations and legitimacy are more important for sustained cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment mechanisms alone is unlikely to produce large efficiency gains unless accompanied by supportive group culture, identification, or legitimacy. However, the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence that can be directly mapped to public goods game design dimensions or used for efficiency prediction.

  findings: The paper finds that the interaction between incentives (including punishment) and social norms is complex and context-dependent. It reviews theoretical and empirical literature showing that punishment and social disapproval can increase cooperation and norm compliance in public goods and related settings, but also that incentives can sometimes crowd out intrinsic motivation, leading to ambiguous or even negative effects on aggregate outcomes. The paper emphasizes that the effects of punishment and incentives on efficiency or welfare are not straightforward and can depend on factors such as the framing of incentives, the type of norm (honour vs. stigma), and the emotional responses of participants. It does not provide quantitative estimates or direct predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and conceptual background on how punishment and incentives interact with social norms in public goods and related environments. It suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not universally positive or negative, but depends on context, framing, and the underlying motivations of participants. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that game design dimensions such as observability, the nature of the norm, and the emotional context may moderate the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does not provide direct empirical evidence or parameter estimates. Its value is mainly in highlighting the need for caution and context-sensitivity when predicting the effects of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Leimar, O., & Hammerstein, P. (2010). Cooperation for direct fitness benefits. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that mechanisms such as by-product benefits, pseudo-reciprocity, sanctions, and partner choice are important and successful in explaining the evolution of cooperation for direct fitness benefits, especially in mutualisms. Sanctions (punishment-like mechanisms) can help maintain cooperation, but their importance varies by system and is sometimes debated. Partner choice and biological market dynamics are highlighted as widespread and effective mechanisms for sustaining cooperation, even in the absence of repeated interactions or high common interest. The paper emphasizes the need for more empirical testing and a broader theoretical framework beyond kin selection and reciprocity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for the role of punishment (sanctions) and partner choice in sustaining cooperation, but does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence relevant to predicting efficiency changes in public goods games with or without punishment. It suggests that punishment-like mechanisms can be important in some systems, but their effect on efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed. The discussion of biological markets and partner choice may inform hypotheses about moderators of punishment effects, but the paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Hauser, M., McAuliffe, K., & Blake, P. R. (2009). Evolving the ingredients for reciprocity and spite. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that reciprocity and spite are rare or absent in non-human animals due to both ultimate (evolutionary) and proximate (mechanistic) constraints. In contrast, humans evolved the necessary cognitive integration and social structures (such as large groups of unrelated individuals) that favor the emergence of reciprocity and punishment. Punishment is argued to be essential for maintaining cooperation and enforcing social norms in humans, and psychological spite is seen as a byproduct of selection for punishment. The paper reviews animal experiments (e.g., with jays, tamarins, chimpanzees) and human studies (including children), showing that only humans reliably exhibit the full suite of behaviors required for sustained reciprocity and norm-enforcing punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and comparative context for why punishment and reciprocity are effective in humans but not in most non-human animals. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants. It is useful for understanding the evolutionary and cognitive prerequisites for punishment and cooperation, but does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in specific game designs. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of group size, repeated interactions, norm enforcement, and cognitive integration for the emergence of punishment and cooperation.

  findings: The paper finds that behavioral economics provides important insights for climate change policy, especially regarding the need for lower social discount rates and the role of social preferences in cooperation. It reviews evidence that cooperation in public goods games and real-world analogues is conditional and often requires the possibility of punishment or sanctions to be sustained over time. The authors argue that, in the context of climate negotiations, sanctioning mechanisms are likely essential for long-term effectiveness, as cooperation otherwise tends to unravel. However, the paper does not provide new empirical estimates of efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it systematically analyze the effect of specific game design dimensions.

However, it does not provide quantitative evidence or direct predictions about efficiency changes when punishment is enabled versus disabled. The discussion is at the level of mechanisms and qualitative expectations, not empirical effect sizes. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the general expectation that punishment mechanisms are likely to increase cooperation and possibly efficiency, but does not allow for fine-grained, dimension-specific predictions.

- source: Clutton-Brock, T. (2009). Cooperation between non-kin in animal societies. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation between non-kin in animal societies is most often maintained by immediate mutualistic benefits or manipulative tactics (including punishment and coercion), rather than by direct reciprocity as modeled in iterated Prisoner's Dilemma or public goods games. It reviews evidence that punishment and coercion can enforce cooperation, but emphasizes that most animal cooperation occurs in kin groups and that non-kin cooperation with delayed payoffs (as in human economic games) is rare. The paper highlights the limitations of applying standard economic game models to animal societies, due to differences in partner choice, communication, and the immediacy of benefits.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and comparative context for interpreting the mechanisms that maintain cooperation and the role of punishment in animal societies. It suggests that, in natural animal groups, punishment and coercion can enforce cooperation, but that the structure and payoffs of animal cooperation often differ from those in laboratory public goods games. The paper does not provide quantitative evidence or direct predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games, but it does suggest that punishment is more likely to be effective in small, stable groups where benefits are immediate and cheating is costly. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency may depend on group structure, immediacy of benefits, and the ability to enforce sanctions, but it does not provide direct empirical estimates.

  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in animals can be explained by several evolutionary mechanisms, including kin selection, reciprocity, byproduct mutualism, and group selection. Punishment is highlighted as an important mechanism that can promote and stabilize cooperation, especially in larger groups where cooperation would otherwise be difficult to sustain. The discussion of punishment draws on theoretical models (e.g., Boyd and Richerson 1992) and qualitative animal examples (e.g., macaques, vervet monkeys, wasps), but does not provide quantitative data on efficiency or group payoff. The paper suggests that the presence of punishment or retribution in models makes cooperation more likely, but does not quantify the effect in public-goods-game-like terms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation and stabilize prosocial behavior in group settings, including those analogous to public goods games. However, it does not provide quantitative evidence or direct predictions about efficiency or group payoff outcomes with and without punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the plausibility of positive effects of punishment on cooperation, especially in larger groups, but does not inform the magnitude or specific moderators of efficiency effects in experimental public goods games.

  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that current interpretations of experimental games, especially those invoking strong reciprocity and cultural group selection, are limited by insufficient understanding of how participants cognitively frame the games and categorize other players. It highlights that both explicit features (such as punishment options) and implicit cues (such as social context or subtle signals) can strongly influence cooperation and punishment behavior. The authors caution against drawing strong conclusions about the evolution of cooperation or the existence of group-selected norms from experimental game results, given the variability in framing and the lack of direct evidence about participants' cognitive processes. The paper does not provide findings on efficiency or payoff outcomes.

It does, however, highlight that behavioral outcomes in such games are highly sensitive to framing, implicit cues, and cultural context, which may moderate or confound the effects of punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests caution in generalizing from experimental results, especially when game design dimensions do not capture the full range of contextual and framing variables that influence behavior. The paper does not report or synthesize efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it offers limited direct support for efficiency prediction.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
